A signal for the whole process goes to the main thread, unless the main thread blocks it and another thread does not. Then, as on Linux, it goes to that thread: its handler runs there, or with no handler its default action ends (or stops) the whole process at once. So the "signal thread" pattern works: block in main, unblock in a worker (#520). It waits on the main thread only when every thread blocks it. A signal sent to one thread (`pthread_kill`) that it blocks waits for that thread, and if it has no handler, the thread's unblock ends the whole process by the signal, whatever the other threads block. `slicc.sigmask_poll(set_lo, set_hi, in_ptr, out_ptr, nsubscriptions, nevents_ptr)` is `pselect`, `ppoll` and `sigsuspend`: it swaps the thread's mask to the given set for the call and puts the old one back before it returns. It delivers a pending signal the new mask unblocks, without polling, or polls as `poll_oneoff` does, under the new mask, so a signal it unblocks interrupts the wait with no window between the swap and the wait; with no subscriptions it waits until a signal is delivered. An interrupted call returns `EINTR` with no events, after the handler has run. `slicc.sigmask_epoll_wait(set_lo, set_hi, epfd, events_ptr, maxevents, timeout_ns, nevents_ptr)` is `epoll_pwait` (#771). It swaps the thread's mask to the given set in the same way. A pending signal the new mask unblocks runs its handler and makes the call return `EINTR` with no events, without waiting. Otherwise the call waits exactly as `wasix_32v1.epoll_wait` with the same arguments does (the same event layout and errors; a `timeout_ns` of all bits set is forever, 0 doesn't wait), under the new mask, so a signal it unblocks ends the wait with `EINTR` after the handler. The old mask is back before the call returns, on every path, and `*nevents_ptr` is always written. A kernel without it answers `ENOSYS`, and the libc keeps its non-atomic swap around `epoll_wait`. For both masked waits, a signal that came while the program computed and was delivered as the call began (its handler ran under the old mask, as on Linux, where it would have run before the call) ends the call with `EINTR` only if the new mask leaves it unblocked; when the new mask blocks every signal delivered then, the call waits as if none had come (#799). `slicc.sigwait(set_lo, set_hi, timeout_ns, sig_ptr)` is `sigwait`, `sigwaitinfo` and `sigtimedwait` (#520). If a signal of the set is pending for the thread, or else for the process, it takes it: the lowest-numbered first, with no handler run. It writes the number as a `u32` at `sig_ptr` and returns 0. Otherwise it waits until one is pending. `timeout_ns` of all bits set is forever and 0 doesn't wait; when the time runs out it answers `EAGAIN` (`*sig_ptr = 0`). A signal outside the set that the thread has unblocked and handles runs its handler and ends the call with `EINTR` (also one that came while the program computed and was delivered as the call began, as for the other waits), and so does a job-control stop, once continued. While a thread waits, the kernel counts it as able to take the signals of its set: a process-directed signal that every thread blocks goes to it (the main thread first, if it is the one waiting), so the signal-thread pattern works with `sigwait` too. Only kernel signals 1 to 31 can be waited for. A kernel without it answers `ENOSYS`. `slicc.sigaction_inherited(ign_ptr)` (#820) writes, as a little-endian `u64` at `ign_ptr`, the signals the program arrived with set to `SIG_IGN`, inherited across `exec` or spawn (bit `sig - 1`; never `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP`; kernel signals are 1 to 31, so the high word is 0), and returns 0. A libc calls it once before constructors and `main` and seeds its `sigaction` table with `SIG_IGN` for each bit, so `sigaction(sig, NULL, &old)` reads an inherited ignore back (wasix-sysroot 2025.9.30-32 and later); a kernel without it answers `ENOSYS`, and the libc keeps its defaults. **A handler and its own signal** (#971): while a handler runs, the same signal waits, as if `sa_mask` held it: it arrives once after the handler returns, not nested inside it, unless the handler was set with `SA_NODEFER`. The hold is part of the handler's mask, so a handler that unblocks its own signal (`pthread_sigmask(SIG_UNBLOCK, …)` or a `SIG_SETMASK` without it) lifts it: a signal raised after that nests at once, as on Linux (#1236). A program that is sent its fatal signal twice, or raises it from its own handler, therefore no longer recurses (gpg's `got_fatal_signal` under `timeout` overflowed the stack). Other signals still run nested, as with an empty `sa_mask`. Handlers are never inherited. **The sender** (#892): `slicc.sigwait_info(set_lo, set_hi, timeout_ns, info_ptr)` is `slicc.sigwait` that also says who sent the signal it took, for `sigwaitinfo` and `sigtimedwait`. `slicc.siginfo_take(sig, info_ptr)` fills the same struct with the sender of the instance of `sig` just delivered to this thread's handler, for an `SA_SIGINFO` handler's `siginfo_t`, and consumes it; with none recorded it answers `ENOENT` and leaves the struct alone, and libc fills `SI_USER` with pid and uid 0. Both write `struct { u32 size; i32 signo; i32 code; i32 pid; u32 uid; i32 status; }`, little-endian, 24 bytes:
  - the caller puts in `size` how many bytes it has, and the kernel fills the fields that fit and writes back how many it filled, so later fields can follow without a new import;
  - `kill()`/`raise()` by a process give `SI_USER` (0), the sender's pid as `getpid()` shows it and its real uid; a `SIGPIPE` from a write is `SI_USER` from the process itself, as on Linux;
  - `pthread_kill` to another thread gives `SI_TKILL` (-6) from the process. wasix-sysroot's `pthread_kill` of the calling thread itself goes through `kill(getpid())` and so arrives as `SI_USER`, until its libc takes the thread route for itself too;
  - `SIGCHLD` gives `CLD_EXITED` (1) with the exit status, `CLD_KILLED` (2) with the signal, `CLD_STOPPED` (5) or `CLD_CONTINUED` (6), with the child's pid and real uid;
  - a terminal's ^C/^Z/^\, `alarm` and the kernel's own signals give `SI_KERNEL` (0x80) with pid and uid 0, and so does a `sigwait_info` take with nothing recorded;
  - a standard signal doesn't queue, so the first pending instance's sender is kept until the signal is taken. A caught signal sent again while it's still pending coalesces with it: the handler runs once and the program goes on, as on Linux ([#1088](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1088)). One deliberate deviation: a second `SIGINT`, `SIGTERM`, `SIGQUIT` or `SIGHUP` while the first is still pending **and the program has made no kernel call since** ends the program with the signal's default action. A WASI program that computes without making calls can't run a handler, so this is what lets a second ^C end it (a Python `while True: pass`). Timer and user signals (`SIGALRM`, `SIGUSR1` …) always coalesce, and so does a signal the program has blocked, or is still handling, while it makes calls.
  A kernel without the imports answers `ENOSYS`, and the libc reports `SI_USER` with pid and uid 0, as before. **SIGCHLD:** with `SIGCHLD` set to `SIG_IGN` (by the program, or inherited), or with `SA_NOCLDWAIT` in the flags `slicc.sigaction_set` gets for `SIGCHLD`, an exited child is reaped at once and never stays a zombie: `waitpid` for it is `ECHILD`, and `wait` blocks until every child has exited, then fails with `ECHILD`, as on Linux. `SIGCHLD` is still sent. An explicit `SIG_DFL` turns it back off. A child that a process waits on for `exec`, or that `kernel.run` and the run calls collect, is never reaped that way.
- **Pipes** have no file type for WASI and WASIX programs' `fstat` (`st_mode` type 0), because WASI has no FIFO file type. A socket type, as kernels 1.47.3 to 1.48.1 gave them, made Ruby treat a `popen` pipe as a socket (`close_write` then never ends the child's input). The kernel's `slicc_fs.fd_mode` answers `S_IFIFO | 0600` (Linux numbering) for a pipe, and wasix-sysroot 2025.9.30-21 and later take the type from it, so programs built on it see a FIFO (`S_ISFIFO`); older libcs keep only the permission bits. Until then Python's asyncio refuses a pipe for a subprocess (`Pipe transport is only for pipes, sockets and character devices`). They are read and written as pipes; socket calls on them fail with `ENOTSOCK`, and `isatty()` is false. Emscripten programs, bash included, see a FIFO. The same holds for any kernel descriptor that is neither seekable nor a terminal or device, such as captured output.
- **Sockets**: the WASIX socket calls (`sock_open`, `sock_pair` for an `AF_UNIX` stream or datagram pair, `bind`, `listen`, `connect`, `accept`, options, local and peer names) are the kernel's loopback sockets, and preview1's `sock_recv` / `sock_send` / `sock_shutdown` work on them and on inherited ones. Writing to a socket whose peer is gone fails with `EPIPE`, through `fd_write` as through `sock_send`, as if `MSG_NOSIGNAL` were set. A write to a pipe, FIFO or terminal whose reader is gone follows the program's `SIGPIPE` disposition, as the kernel knows it (#810): at the default it ends the program with status 141; ignored (also an ignore inherited across `exec` and spawn, as bash's `trap '' PIPE` passes on), the write fails with `EPIPE`; with a handler, the handler runs on the thread that wrote and then the write fails with `EPIPE` (#820); blocked, `SIGPIPE` stays pending and the write fails with `EPIPE`. An Emscripten program's write to a pipe, and its `send` without `MSG_NOSIGNAL`, follow the same rules, except that the kernel doesn't see an Emscripten program's signal mask, so a blocked `SIGPIPE` there still ends it. A WASIX program's libc reads an inherited ignore back through `slicc.sigaction_inherited` (wasix-sysroot -32 and later; see below). An Emscripten program's shim reads it from `Module.sliccKernel.sigInherited()` (#965, homescoop#973). That's a mask with bit n set for signal n (not bit n-1, as the WASIX import has it) holding only inherited `SIG_IGN`s, never `SIGKILL` or `SIGSTOP`. The shim seeds its `sigaction` table from it in a constructor that runs before any user constructor. The kernel puts the function on `Module.sliccKernel` before the glue runs constructors, ahead of the rest of `sliccKernel`. On a kernel without the function it is undefined, and the shim keeps the defaults. HTTP and HTTPS leave through the realm proxy that `https_proxy` names, as for any other program; `resolve` answers `/etc/hosts` names and literal addresses, and the [uplink](#uplink)'s names. `sock_open(SOCK_DGRAM)` on `AF_INET`/`AF_INET6` gives a [datagram socket](#datagram-sockets-udp); `sock_send_to` and `sock_recv_from` carry its addresses, and `RECV_DATA_TRUNCATED` is set in `ro_flags` when a datagram did not fit.
- **Diagnostics**: a trap ends the program with 134 and its message on stderr; a program that crashes its worker otherwise (a JS stack overflow, `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`, or any other uncaught error) ends with 70, and its message goes to the process's own fd 2 too, so `2>` catches it (#1237), or to the session's stderr when it has no fd 2 to write; with `SLICC_WASM_BACKTRACE=1` the wasm frames follow, named from the module's name section, or from a sidecar for a module shipped without one (`<module>.names` beside it, or the same path in a `<package>-names` package). A sidecar that doesn't fit the module, naming another number of functions than the module has (imported and defined), gets one warning line before the trap message, and the frames keep their raw indices instead of wrong names (#1239). `SLICC_WASI_STATS=1` makes a program print its calls, counted and timed, as it ends.

### Program imports

A WASI command can name an ES module of its package that provides imports of its own, for host functions a program needs beyond WASI (`"imports": "host/pnpm-host.mjs"`). Each worker of the process (the process and every thread) loads it, with the same trust as Emscripten glue, and calls its `createImports(ctx)`, which returns import namespaces (`{ pnpm_host: { … } }`). It may not define `wasi_snapshot_preview1`, `wasix_32v1`, `wasi` or `env`; any other namespace the module imports is then accepted, and functions it does not provide answer `ENOSYS`. A WASI program (one that imports `wasi_snapshot_preview1` or `wasix_32v1`) needs no imports module for that: without one, its function imports from namespaces of its own all answer `ENOSYS`, so the same binary runs the commands that need no host functions anywhere. A stub answers in the import's own result type: 52 for `i32`, `f32` and `f64`, `52n` for `i64`, and nothing for a function without a result. An import whose result cannot carry an errno (several values, `v128`, a reference) is refused (126) unless the imports module provides it. A module with no WASI imports, or a foreign import that is not a function, is still refused (126). `ctx` has:

- `memory()`, `instance()` (after instantiation), `tid` (1 for the main thread), `argv`, `env`, `cwd()`;
- `fs`: the synchronous filesystem the WASI calls use (absolute paths);
  - `fs.copyFile(from, to, { mode, excl })` copies a regular file inside the kernel, as `slicc_fs.path_copy_file` does (below): `mode` (default `0666`) less the umask, `excl` for `O_EXCL`. It throws the kernel's fs errors (`code` `EEXIST`, `ENOENT`, `EXDEV`, …). An older kernel's `ctx.fs` has no `copyFile`, so a module checks `typeof ctx.fs.copyFile === 'function'`;
- `fds`: `open(path, { read, write, append, create, exclusive, truncate, directory, nofollow, mode })` returns a WASI descriptor of the program; `mode` is a new file's mode. With `create` and `exclusive` under users `report` or `enforce`, the file is created with it, less the umask, as `open(2)` does, and the name is only checked by the create itself (a symlink there is `EEXIST`). Otherwise the mode is set exactly once the file is open; `close`, `fstat` (`{ kind, path, mode, uid, gid, size, ino, mtimeMs }`; an open file's size is the descriptor's own, so writes not yet flushed count, while the other fields come from a stat of its path, as WASI's `fd_filestat_get` gives them), `fchmod`, and `tryLock(fd, exclusive)` / `unlock(fd)`: advisory locks held by the kernel across processes and threads, keyed by path, released on unlock, on closing the descriptor and at exit (`tryLock` returns 0, or 6 when another process holds the lock);
- `syscall(req)`: a kernel syscall, synchronously;
- `net.connect({ host, port, nonblock })` connects a TCP socket to the kernel's loopback and returns its kernel descriptor. `host` is `localhost` or `127.x.y.z`; anything else, or a port outside 1–65535, is `EINVAL`. A refused connection throws `ECONNREFUSED`. The connection carries the program's own identity, as any connect from its process does, so a [page facade](#page-facades) such as `SLICC_LINK_URL` sees the program's stamp. With `nonblock` it returns at once, and `net.ready(fd)` answers 0 once a connect is complete, `EINPROGRESS` (26) while it isn't, or the connect's WASI errno (`SO_ERROR`). On the loopback a connect completes at once, so `ready` is 0 straight away. `ready` is meant for a descriptor from `net.connect`. The descriptor is read and written with `fd-read` and `fd-write` and closed with `fd-close`, as below, and `fd-select` (`{ op: 'fd-select', read: [fds], write: [fds], timeoutMs }`, answering `{ read, write }` with the ready ones; `timeoutMs: -1` waits without a limit) waits until it's readable or writable;
- `spawn({ argv, env, cwd, stdin, stdout, stderr })` starts a command (resolved like `kernel.run`) as a child and returns `{ pid, stdin, stdout, stderr }`. `env` is the child's whole environment (the program's own by default), `cwd` the program's by default. Each stdio is `'inherit'` (the default: the program's own), `'null'`, or `'pipe'`: a kernel pipe whose end the result holds as a descriptor, read and written with `fd-read` (`{ op: 'fd-read', fd, max }`, an empty read at the end), `fd-write` (`{ op: 'fd-write', fd, body }`) and `fd-close`, through `syscall` or `async.submit`; the child's ends are closed in the program. A command that does not exist throws `ENOENT`;
- `wait(pid)` blocks until a child ends and returns `{ status }`, or `{ status: 128 + signal, signal }` when a signal ended it; `kill(pid, sig)` signals it;
- `async`: operations of the process that any of its threads can wait for: `submit(req)` runs a kernel syscall without blocking the caller, `resolve(value)` completes at once, `hold()` never completes, and each returns an id; `wait(timeoutMs?)` returns the id of a completed one (0 after the timeout, -1 once `close()` was called), `take(id)` its `{ value }` or `{ error }` (`undefined` while it is pending), and `cancel(id)` drops one;
- `errno(err)`: the WASI errno of an error;
- `uid` and `gid`, with `users: 'report'` or `'enforce'`: the process's effective uid and gid, read again at each use, so they follow a `setresuid`. `fstat` then shows a file's real owner, and the process's ids for a descriptor with no path. With `users: 'off'`, `ctx` has no `uid` or `gid`, and `fstat` answers 1000 for both, as before.

### JS programs

A command can be an ES module instead of a wasm program: `"abi": "js"` and `module` name the module in the package, which must be one self-contained file (no relative or bare imports), and `args`, `argv0` and `env` work as for other commands. An entry with `"abi": "js"` but no `module` (or one outside the package) is not a command, like a wasm entry without its module, so running it fails with 127. Every run gets its own process worker, so each process evaluates the module afresh: module-level state never carries over from one run to the next. The kernel keeps an importable URL per module source (a `blob:` URL in the browser, a base64 `data:` URL under Node) for the 16 sources it ran last, so a command run in a loop doesn't encode its module again each time ([#846](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/846)). A changed file is a new source and gets a new URL. An evicted URL is revoked once every run it was handed to has exited, so a run that is still loading its module never loses it.

```json
{ "slicc": { "commands": { "jstool": { "abi": "js", "module": "dist/jstool.mjs" } } } }
```

The module's default export (or its `main` export) is called with a context and returns the exit status, or nothing for 0. It runs as an ordinary process in a process worker of its own: it has a pid and a process group, shows in `ps` and `/proc`, takes part in pipes, redirects and job control, and `kill`, `^C` and `^Z` reach it. A stopped program freezes at its next turn of the event loop (an `await`, a timer, a callback) and stays frozen, its timers and callbacks included, until `SIGCONT`; a synchronous loop that never yields runs on until it does, as a WASI program runs on until its next system call, since nothing can preempt a worker's running code short of ending it. A process worker is a dedicated worker, so a JS program can use what browsers offer there, such as WebCodecs (`VideoEncoder`, `VideoDecoder`, `AudioEncoder`, `AudioDecoder`, `ImageDecoder`) and `OffscreenCanvas`, without the page's main thread. The context (`JsProgramContext`, exported as a type) has:

- `kernel.version`, the slicc-kernel version, as in `/proc/version` (also `sync.kernel.version`; read-only);
- `argv` (with `argv[0]`), `env`, `pid` (the pid `ps` shows, also after an `exec`), `ppid()` (asked each time, so an orphan sees 1) and `cwd`, and `resolve(path)` to make a path absolute against `cwd`;
- `stdin`, `stdout` and `stderr` as web streams, and `read(fd, max?)` (empty at end of file), `write(fd, bytes | string, { nonblock }?)` and `close(fd)` on any descriptor it inherited. A write waits until the pipe has room, so a slow reader slows the program down, and answers the byte count. With `{ nonblock: true }` (also `sync.write`), it doesn't wait: it takes what fits now (at most 1 MiB) and answers that count, possibly fewer bytes than given, or fails with `EAGAIN` when nothing fits. The descriptor's own flags don't change. A regular file takes it all, as on Linux. A pipe with no reader is still `EPIPE` with `SIGPIPE`, and a socket is `EPIPE` alone ([#1124](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1124));
- `fdStatus(fd)` (`{ type, tty, seekable }`, `type` being `file`, `pipe`, `tty`, `socket`, `device` or `directory`) and `isatty(fd)`, to tell `< file` from a pipe;
- `open(path, { read, write, append, create, exclusive, truncate, nonblock, signal })`, which returns a file with `read(position, length)`, `write(position, bytes)`, `size()`, `truncate(size)`, `sync()` and `close()`. Reads and writes go through the kernel's paged files at those positions, so a large file is never read whole; **A FIFO** ([#935](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/935)) opens through the kernel's FIFO open, as fifo(7) says. A read-open waits for a writer and a write-open for a reader, unless the open is `read` and `write` both. `nonblock: true` gives a read-open at once and a write-open with no reader `ENXIO`, and `signal` aborts a waiting open with its reason. Its file reads and writes the pipe: `read(0, length)` and `write(0, bytes)`, any other position is `ESPIPE`, `size()` is `0`, and `truncate` is `EINVAL`. A socket node is `ENXIO`.
- `fs`: `stat`, `lstat`, `exists`, `readdir`, `mkdir` (with parents, or, with `{ recursive: false }`, as Node's `fs.mkdir` without `recursive`: `EEXIST` for an existing entry and `ENOENT` for a missing parent, atomically among the processes of one kernel, so it can serve as a lock there, but not between two kernels on one origin, such as two tabs ([#519](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/519)); `sync.fs.mkdir` takes the same option), `rm` (recursive), `unlink`, `rename`, `symlink`, `readlink`, `statfs`, `chmod(path, mode)`, and `readFile`/`writeFile` for small files. `chmod` takes the permission bits (`0` to `0o7777`, `EINVAL` otherwise) and follows a symlink; as `chmod(2)`, only the file's owner or root may (`EPERM`), so a program can make a script it wrote runnable by path. `sync.fs.chmod` is the same, synchronously. `statfs(path)` answers `{ type, bsize, blocks, bfree, bavail, files, ffree, quota, usage }`: the fields of Node's `StatFs`, plus `quota` and `usage` in bytes (what jsh 0.17 reads). The values come from the same source as an Emscripten program's `statfs`/`df`: the driver of the mount the path is on, or, on the root file system, the origin's storage estimate (`navigator.storage.estimate()`). `bsize` is 4096, `bfree` and `bavail` are what's left of the quota, and `type`, `files` and `ffree` are 0, since the kernel doesn't know them. Where there's no estimate (the Node entry's root), every count is 0. A missing path is `ENOENT`. `sync.fs` has it too. `rm` removes a whole tree in one kernel call (on the OPFS root, one recursive removal and one metadata delete: 50,000 files take about 3 s in Chrome, where `rm -r` takes about 25 s) with the result of a file-by-file removal: symlinks inside the tree are removed, never followed; a tree holding a protected path is refused with `EROFS` and nothing is removed; a mount inside the tree is emptied and its mount point answers `EBUSY`, while the rest is removed; under `users`, what the user may remove is removed and the call then fails with the first `EACCES`/`EPERM`; files a process holds open stay readable to it;
- **`fs.watch(path, options?, onEvent)`** resolves with `{ close() }` ([#1031](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1031)). `sync.fs.watch` returns the watcher directly and is in place when it returns, so it sees every change made after the call. This is the shape for Node's synchronous `fs.watch`.
  - **What it reports:** a file reports its own changes. A directory reports its direct entries, or with `{ recursive: true }`, everything below it.
  - **Events:** each one is `{ type, filename, seq }`, in the order the operations happened.
    - `'rename'`: an entry appeared or went away (create, `mkdir`, `unlink`, `rm`, both names of a `rename`, `link`, `symlink`).
    - `'change'`: an existing entry's data or metadata changed (a write, `truncate`, `chmod`, `chown`, `utimes`). A write that creates a file gives `'rename'` and then `'change'`, as on Linux. Writing a whole file over an existing one is a truncate and a write, so it gives two `'change'`s, or one if the new content is empty ([#1357](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1357)).
    - `seq` numbers the operations across the whole kernel, increasing. One operation heard by several watches (a directory's and a file's in it) has the same `seq` in each, so a program with several watches can merge their events back into one queue in operation order and, as inotify does, merge only events that are adjacent in it. `{ overflow: true }` and `{ ended: true }` carry the `seq` of the operation that caused them.
    - `filename` is the entry's name relative to the watched directory (`sub/a.txt` when recursive), or the file's basename for a file watch.
  - **Overflow:** a watch holds 1,000 events between reads. Beyond that, the rest are dropped, and `{ overflow: true }` follows the ones kept.
  - **The watched path itself:** like inotify, a watch follows the file or directory it was opened on, not its name. When a directory above it is renamed, the watch stays on the moved path with no event, and later events keep the name it was opened with. When the path itself is renamed, the watch gets one `{ type: 'rename', filename, moved: true }` and stays on the new path. Only a removal ends it: `rm` of the path or a directory above it, or a rename of something else over it gives one `'rename'` with its own name, then `{ ended: true }`. Renames are followed as this kernel makes them ([#1356](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1356)). `close()` is safe at any time and idempotent, and nothing is delivered after it returns.
  - **Errors at watch time:** a path the program may not read (under `users`) is `EACCES`, and one that doesn't exist is `ENOENT`. Permission is checked first, so a path under a directory the program can't search is `EACCES` whether or not it exists. A process holds at most 8,192 watches; past that, `ENOSPC`.
  - **Permissions per event:** under `users`, a program only hears about paths it may reach. An event about `P` below the watched directory is delivered only if the program's user can search every directory down to `P`'s parent and read that parent, as Node's recursive watch would need in order to add a watch there. The check runs when the program takes the events, so another user's `0700` directory stays silent until it's opened up. A consequence is that events inside a directory that is gone by the time the program takes them are dropped: after `rm -r sub` the program sees `rename sub` but not `sub/a`. A watcher should rescan on a directory's `rename`, as Node users do anyway.
  - **Who counts as a writer:** every process in the kernel, the embedder (`kernel.writeFile` and the like) and the mounts that report changes. A `hostfs` change from the host comes as `'change'`, since the host doesn't say which kind it was.
  - **Not reported:** another kernel's changes on the same origin (another tab), as inotify on a shared file system doesn't see other hosts.
  - **Lifetime:** a watch ends with its process and doesn't keep the program alive.
  - **The older watches** (`kernel.watch`, a client's `fs.watch` with `{ paths }`) are unchanged.
- `/dev`, in `open`, `fs` and their `sync` forms, as a WASI or Emscripten program sees it, with the same modes and owners as under `users`: `null`, `zero`, `full`, `random`, `urandom` and `tty` (character devices, `0666`, root's), `stdin`, `stdout` and `stderr` (links to `/proc/self/fd/0`, `1`, `2`), `fd` (a link to `/proc/self/fd`, so `/dev/fd/N` is descriptor N) and the kernel's terminals `/dev/ttyN` and `/dev/pts/N` (with users on, owned by the session's user or the master's opener, `0620`). `/dev/shm` is the kernel's shared tmpfs (see [Mounts](#mounts)), read and written like any other mount; `/dev/ptmx` isn't there. `stat` through `/dev/stdin`, `/dev/stdout` or `/dev/fd/N` describes the program's own descriptor (the file it is redirected to, a pipe, a terminal), `readlink` of `/dev/fd/N` names it (`pipe:[N]` and `socket:[N]` for a pipe or a socket), and a closed descriptor is `ENOENT`. Opening a device gives a kernel descriptor on it; opening `/dev/std*` or `/dev/fd/N` opens the file again with the flags asked for when the descriptor is on a file (as Linux reopens `/proc/self/fd/N`, so `append` appends and `truncate` truncates), and otherwise duplicates the descriptor; `/dev/tty` is the program's controlling terminal (`ENXIO` without one); and `/dev/ttyN` and `/dev/pts/N` are opened by name through the kernel, so a terminal that isn't the user's is `EACCES` under `enforce`. A file on anything but a regular file reads and writes as a stream (the position is ignored) and only in the directions it was opened for (`EBADF` otherwise), its `size()` is 0 and `truncate` is `EINVAL`. `readFile` reads a device or a descriptor to its end, up to 64 MiB, and fails with `EFBIG` past that (where Node's `readFile` throws `ERR_FS_FILE_TOO_LARGE`), so `readFile('/dev/zero')` fails instead of hanging, while a bounded `read` of it works. Other paths pay only a prefix check: they make the same kernel calls as before. Removing a `/dev` entry fails (see [`/dev`](#dev-entries)), and so does a new name directly in `/dev` or `/proc`;
- `/proc`, in `fs`, `open` and their `sync` forms, the same as a WASI program's (see [`/proc`](#proc)): `stat`, `lstat`, `exists`, `readdir`, `readlink` and `readFile` answer it, and `/proc/self` is the program's own pid. It's read-only, so every change in it is `EROFS`: `writeFile`, `mkdir`, `rm`, `unlink`, `chmod`, a `rename`, `link` or `symlink` into or out of it, and an `open` for writing, creating or truncating. A file opened in it is a snapshot read at the open, with no descriptor (`fd` is `-1`): its `read` and `size` answer from the snapshot until it's closed, and `write` and `truncate` are `EBADF`. Other paths pay only a prefix check;
- `signals`: `on(signal, handler)` catches a signal (by number or as `'SIGINT'`), `ignore(signal)` ignores it and `reset(signal)` restores its default action. A call blocked in a read or write is restarted after a handler runs; signals without a handler keep their default actions, so an uncaught `SIGINT` ends the program with 130 and `SIGKILL` always does;
- `sync`: `read`, `write`, `close`, `open` (whose files' methods are synchronous too) and `fs`, made synchronously, for code that cannot `await`, such as a compiled program's file system or a `readFileSync`. A synchronous call holds the worker until the kernel answers, so nothing else of the program runs meanwhile, timers and callbacks included; a signal that arrives during a blocked read or write runs its handler, and the call is then restarted. Descriptors are shared with the rest of the context, so a file opened with `sync.open` can be closed with `close`, and the other way around; `sync.run` and `sync.exit` are described next;
- `sync.cred()` returns the process's credentials, `{ ruid, euid, suid, rgid, egid, sgid, groups }`, and `sync.setcred(change)` changes them, in the shape of the Emscripten call (see [Users](#users)): each field is optional, `-1` keeps an id, and `groups` sets the group list. They are synchronous, so a runtime can build Node's `process.setuid`, `seteuid`, `setgid`, `setegid`, `setgroups` and `initgroups` on them, and follow the same rules as every other program: root may set anything, anyone else only its own real, effective and saved ids (`EPERM` otherwise), and only root may set the groups; a bad value is `EINVAL`. The new ids count at once for the program's own files and for every child it starts, with any `users` mode (with `'off'`, only the ids change);
- `umask(mask?)` (and `sync.umask(mask?)`) returns the process's umask and, given a mask from `0` to `0o777`, sets it (anything else is `EINVAL`). It's the kernel's umask, so `/proc/self/status` shows it and a child inherits it. The kernel applies it when the program creates: `fs.writeFile(path, data, { mode })`, `fs.mkdir(path, { mode })` and `open(path, { create, mode })` (and their `sync` forms) create with `mode` less the umask, `0o666` for a file and `0o777` for a directory without one, as `open(2)` and `mkdir(2)` do, with `users` off too. A `mode` outside `0` to `0o7777` is `EINVAL`;
- `sync.run({ argv, argv0, env, cwd, pgid, session, input, stdin, stdout, stderr, maxBuffer, timeout, killSignal })` runs a command to its end and answers `{ pid, status, signal, stdout, stderr, error }`, as Node's `spawnSync` does. `pgid` and `session` work as for `spawn` below; a group that doesn't exist or is another session's answers `error: { code: 'ESRCH' }` or `'EPERM'`, and a bad option throws `EINVAL`. It suits `execSync`, `spawnSync` and `execFileSync`. `argv0` works as for `spawn` below. The kernel does the whole run while the program's worker waits; other processes go on.
  - Each stdio is `'pipe'` (the default), `'ignore'`, `'inherit'` (the program's own, a terminal included), or one of the program's descriptors by number, which the child gets as it is (two slots on one descriptor share it, so their writes keep their order). `stderr: 'stdout'` sends stderr to whatever stdout is, the stdout capture included, in the order the child writes. With `'pipe'`, stdin gets `input` (bytes, or a string as UTF-8) and then its end, so `cat` stops; `stdout` and `stderr` come back as bytes and are absent for the other choices. Both are drained while the child runs, so a child that writes more than a pipe holds never stalls. As in Node, the call returns once the child has exited and every captured stream has reached its end, so output a background grandchild writes later is still collected; `timeout` bounds that wait too, so, as with Node's `spawnSync`, a run blocks for as long as a background process keeps a captured stream open unless `timeout` is set; pass one for a command that may leave something in the background. Once the outcome is decided (the end of output, an overflow or a timeout), the captured streams are closed, so a writer still left on them gets `EPIPE` (and `SIGPIPE`) instead of running on.
  - `status` is the exit status, or `null` with `signal` set when a signal ended the child. A non-zero status is not an error. A command that cannot start (it does not exist, or is not executable) answers `error: { code: 'ENOENT' }` (or the errno), `status: null` and `pid: 0` instead of throwing.
  - More than `maxBuffer` bytes on `stdout` or `stderr` (1 MiB by default) sends the child `killSignal` (`SIGTERM` by default), keeps the first `maxBuffer` bytes, and answers `error: { code: 'ENOBUFS' }`. The kernel keeps at most 64 MiB of each stream whatever `maxBuffer` says, `Infinity` included, since the buffers live in the kernel worker that all processes share. `timeout` (ms) sends `killSignal` when it runs out and answers `error: { code: 'ETIMEDOUT' }`. A child that ignores `killSignal` is still waited for, as in Node.
  - The child is in the program's process group, so `^C` reaches it. The kernel reaps it, so it leaves no zombie, and its CPU time counts as the program's children's. A signal the program catches does not cut the run short; its handler runs when the call returns. A signal that kills the program kills it as usual;
- `sync.spawn({ argv, argv0, env, cwd, pgid, session, parentDeathSignal, stdin, stdout, stderr, fds })` starts a child as `spawn` below does, with the same options and the same child, but synchronously: it returns `{ pid, stdin, stdout, stderr, wait(), kill(signal) }` at once, so a runtime can set Node's `child.pid` before `spawn()` returns. A spawn error (`ENOENT`, `EACCES`, `ESRCH` or `EPERM` for a group, `EINVAL` for a bad option, `EBADF` for a descriptor the program doesn't have) is thrown at once, with its `code`. The streams, `wait()` and `kill()` are the asynchronous ones, used afterwards from async code; the child doesn't wait for the program to read, since its output goes to a kernel pipe, which holds what it writes until the program reads it (a child that fills the pipe waits, as with `spawn`);
- `sync.kill(pid, signal)`, a synchronous `kill`. When the signal reaches the program itself (its own pid, `0` for its process group, or `-pgid` of its own group) and its action is to end the program (no handler, not ignored), the call never returns: the program's writes reach their descriptors and its terminal modes are put back, as for `sync.exit`, then the rest of the group is signalled and the program ends by the signal, so a parent's `wait` and `sync.run` report `signal`, as Node does for `process.kill(process.pid)`. A signal with a handler runs it when the call returns, an ignored one does nothing, and a stop signal stops the program. A signal whose default action dumps core (`SIGQUIT`, `SIGABRT`, `SIGSEGV`, …) ends it the same way; the kernel writes no core and sets no core flag in the wait status. `-1` signals every process the program may signal except itself and pid 1, as on Linux, and returns (`ESRCH` when there was none, `EPERM` when there were some but it may signal none of them); a process that exec'd gets the signal once. Without users, every process runs as root, so `kill -9 -1` from any program ends every other process; with users it reaches only what the sender may signal (its own uid's processes, or all of them as root);
- `sync.exit(status)`, which ends the process at once, as Node's `process.exit()` does: no `catch`, `finally`, microtask, timer or signal handler runs after it, because the call never returns. First the writes the program has made reach their descriptors, in order (a broken pipe drops the rest for that descriptor), and terminal modes the program set with `tty` are put back. Then the process ends the way any exit does: its descriptors are closed and written back, its parent gets `SIGCHLD`, and `wait` reports `status`. Children keep running. In the main thread it ends the process with all its threads (`Worker` itself is withheld; threads come from `thread.spawn`, below); in a thread it ends that thread only. `exit(status)` (below) unwinds instead, so the program's `catch` and `finally` blocks run;
- `select({ read?, write?, timeoutMs? }, { signal }?)` waits until a descriptor of either list is readable or writable, and answers `{ read, write }` with the ready ones; `timeoutMs` (default: no limit) bounds the wait, and `0` only checks. After a nonblock write's `EAGAIN`, `select({ write: [fd] })` says when room returns ([#1124](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1124)).
- `socketpair()` (and `sync.socketpair()`) answers `[a, b]`, two descriptors of the program on a connected `AF_UNIX` stream pair, and `pipe()` (and `sync.pipe()`) answers `[readFd, writeFd]` ([#1093](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1093)).
  - **The same descriptors either way:** the async and `sync` forms give the program's own kernel descriptors, which `read`, `write`, `close` and their `sync` forms all take, until the program closes them or ends.
  - **Reading** a socket or pipe whose peer has closed gives an empty `Uint8Array` (end of file). A `read` still pending when the program `close`s that descriptor rejects with `EBADF` instead of waiting. A `read` and a `write` on one socket can be pending at the same time, as a socket is full duplex.
  - **Writing to a socket whose peer is gone** fails with `EPIPE` and raises no `SIGPIPE`, at the default disposition too, as for WASIX and Emscripten programs (as if `MSG_NOSIGNAL` were set). A pipe's broken write follows `SIGPIPE` as described below.
- **`fds`**, an option of `spawn` and `sync.spawn`, gives the child more descriptors: `{ 3: f }` puts the program's descriptor `f` at the child's fd 3, on the same open file description, as `dup2` would.
  - **Keys:** each key must be an integer of 3 or more, as stdio stays `stdin`, `stdout` and `stderr`. Each value must be an open descriptor of the program. Anything else is `EINVAL` before the child starts.
  - **Only the named descriptors reach the child.** Others the program holds, the other end of a pair included, don't, and a grandchild gets fd 3 only if the child names it again.
  - **The parent's copy:** the program keeps `f` and closes it itself when it no longer needs it, so the child sees end of file once it's the only one left.
  - **Why a `Record`:** it can't name a child fd twice, and it reads like Node's `stdio` index, so it's this rather than a list of `{ fd, from }`.
- `asyncContext` (the Node entry only; absent in a browser, so a runtime feature-detects it; #1350, slicc-jsh#375) is `{ run(store, fn, ...args), getStore(), enterWith(store) }`, backed by one host `AsyncLocalStorage` the process worker makes for each program, since a program can't import `node:async_hooks` and the realm has no promise hooks of its own. A store follows the program's native `await` continuations and promise jobs, as an `AsyncLocalStorage` does in Node, so Next.js, OpenTelemetry and request-context loggers keep their context. Each process and each thread has its own. Only these three frozen functions on a frozen null-prototype object reach the program: they hold the host storage in a closure, `run` calls the program's function with its own arguments, and nothing they return or expose is a host object.
- `memory()`, synchronous and without a kernel call, measures the calling isolate: each `thread` answers for its own (#1240, slicc-jsh#397). It returns a fresh plain object of numbers in bytes, each field only where it's measured: `heapUsed`, `heapTotal`, `heapLimit` (V8's heap size limit), `external` and `arrayBuffers`. On the Node entry they come from V8's heap statistics and Node's memory usage, through functions the worker captured before its lockdown, so nothing reachable from the program leads back to `process` or `v8`; there is no `rss`, which is the whole host's. In a browser `memory()` answers `{}` for now.
- **`thread`, kernel threads of the same process** ([#1094](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1094)), the base for Node's `worker_threads` (tinypool, piscina, vitest's and jest's thread pools, eslint's concurrency). `thread.spawn({ data, transfer })` resolves with `{ tid, port, exited, terminate() }`.
  - **What a thread is:** a new process worker in the **same process**. It has the same pid, descriptor table, cwd, umask, credentials and lockdown, and a sync lane of its own, so `sync` calls in one thread don't hold another.
  - **What it runs:** the program's same default export, called with a context whose `thread` is `{ tid, port, data }`. In the main thread `thread.tid` is `0` and `thread.port` is `undefined`. Threads are numbered from 1 per process, and a thread can spawn threads too.
  - **The port** is a native `MessagePort`, one end of the platform's `MessageChannel`, which the kernel keeps from before the program runs, so a runtime that replaces the global `MessageChannel` (as a Node runtime does) still gets real ports. Messages are structured clones: a `SharedArrayBuffer` is shared (`Atomics.wait` and `Atomics.notify` work across threads), and what is listed in `transfer` (an `ArrayBuffer`, a `MessagePort`) moves and is detached in the sender. `data` is cloned the same way, with `transfer`.
  - **Ending:**

    | How | `exited` |
    |---|---|
    | `exit(n)` or `sync.exit(n)` in the thread (only that thread ends) | `n` |
    | the thread's function returns `n` | `n` (nothing is `0`) |
    | an uncaught error (the kernel prints nothing: a runtime reports it, as Node does with the `Worker`'s `error` event) | `1` |
    | `terminate()`, which resolves once the thread has ended | `1` |
    | out of memory (`RangeError: Array buffer allocation failed`, or the worker dying of it) | `thread.NO_MEMORY`, `256` |
    | the worker died otherwise (nothing is printed either; the runtime reports it) | `thread.CRASHED`, `257` |

    The two codes are outside 0–255, so no `exit(n)` gives them. The main thread's exit (any exit, or a signal that ends the process) ends every thread.
  - **The process:** a thread ending changes nothing for `wait`, `ps` or the process's status. Signals go to the main thread only, so a thread's `signals.on`, `ignore` and `reset` change nothing (as Node, where a worker gets no signal events); the main thread's disposition is the process's. Kernel calls a thread leaves in flight when it ends (a `read` it never awaited, say) are cancelled, so they can't take another thread's data. A stopped process (`^Z`, `SIGSTOP`) stops its threads as well, timers included, until `SIGCONT`.
  - **The cap:** at most twice `navigator.hardwareConcurrency` threads per process at once, and at least 16 (so piscina's default of 1.5 × the parallelism fits), or what `SLICC_JS_THREADS` in the program's environment says, up to 256. Past it, `spawn` rejects with `EAGAIN`, and an ended thread frees its slot. Each thread is a dedicated worker, like a process worker, so threads count against the browser's worker budget the same way processes do.
- **`sync.wasi.create({ args, env, preopens, stdio, returnOnExit })`, an in-process `wasi_snapshot_preview1` instance** ([#1111](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1111)). It is the base for Node's `node:wasi`, which napi-rs packages built for `wasm32-wasi` need (unrs-resolver for jest 30, oxc, rolldown, swc, lightningcss). The guest runs in the program's own worker, on memory it shares with JavaScript, and is called synchronously.
  - **What it returns:** an object shaped like Node's `WASI`:
    - `imports`: `{ wasi_snapshot_preview1 }`, with `wasiImport` as the same table;
    - `bind(memory)`: the guest's `WebAssembly.Memory`, shared or not, before or after instantiation;
    - `start(instance)`: binds `exports.memory`, runs `_start`, and returns its `proc_exit` code (`0` if it returns);
    - `initialize(instance)`: the same for `_initialize`, if there is one;
    - `close()`.
  - **Exit:** with `returnOnExit: false`, `proc_exit(n)` throws a `JsWasiExit` whose `code` is `n`, for the caller to handle (jsh calls `process.exit`). With the default, `true`, `start` returns `n` instead.
  - **Arguments and environment:** `args` and `env` are exactly what the guest sees, with nothing added.
  - **Preopens and stdio:** `preopens` maps a guest name to a path of the process (relative to its cwd), and these become guest fds 3, 4, …. Guest `0`, `1` and `2` are copies of the `stdio` descriptors, `[0, 1, 2]` by default, so the guest closing its stdout leaves the program's own open.
  - **One preview1 host:** the calls are the kernel's own WASI host, the one WASI processes run on, answered over the program's sync lane. Every call has the process's credentials, umask, file permissions (`users: 'enforce'`) and lockdown, with no kernel call of its own, so the guest can do nothing the JavaScript program couldn't do itself.
  - **Confinement mirrors Node's and isn't a security boundary:** a path that leaves its preopen by `..`, or an absolute path, fails with `ENOTCAPABLE`. That's a lexical check, as in Node's uvwasi; a symlink inside a preopen can still lead out. The kernel's permissions are the boundary.
  - **Guest descriptors:**
    - **Its own fd table:** the guest has its own numbering. Each guest fd stands for a kernel descriptor of the process, kept in a map at the import boundary: fd arguments, `path_open`'s and `sock_accept`'s results, `fd_renumber`, both directories of `path_link` and `path_rename`, and the fds inside `poll_oneoff`'s subscriptions. So a guest fd 4 is never the program's own fd 4, and a guest fd with no entry is `EBADF`.
    - **Counted and closed like any other:** these kernel descriptors count against the process's descriptor limit. They are closed when the guest closes them, on `close()`, when the thread that made them ends (by returning, `exit`, `sync.exit`, `terminate()` or a crash; the kernel keeps the list for a thread that cannot clean up itself), and when the process ends.
  - **Threads:** in a `thread` (above), each thread makes its own instance with the same options, as emnapi's workers do with Node's `WASI`. It runs over that thread's own lane, on the `WebAssembly.Memory` posted to it. The view of the file system is the same, while each instance has its own fd table, as in Node.
  - **Scope:** preview1 only, as Node's `node:wasi`. There are no WASIX calls, and no `thread-spawn` from the guest: emnapi starts its threads as JS workers, which are `thread`s here.
- `spawn({ argv, argv0, env, cwd, pgid, session, parentDeathSignal, stdin, stdout, stderr, fds })` starts a command (found as `kernel.run` finds it) as a child process and resolves with `{ pid, stdin, stdout, stderr, wait(), kill(signal) }`. Each stdio is `'inherit'` (the default: the program's own), `'pipe'` (a web stream on a kernel pipe), `'null'`, or one of the program's descriptors. `env` is the child's whole environment (the program's by default), and `cwd` defaults to the program's. The command run is `argv[0]`; `argv0`, when given, is the `argv[0]` the child sees instead, as given (no basename; an empty string too), as with Node's `options.argv0`: `spawn({ argv: ['/bin/sh', '-c', 'echo $0'], argv0: '/bin/sh' })` prints `/bin/sh`, where without it the child sees the command's own name (`sh`). A command whose package manifest sets its `argv0` (a multi-call program's applets, such as coreutils') keeps that one. An `argv0` that isn't a string is `EINVAL`. The child is in the program's process group and session, as after `fork` (Node's `detached: false`), so the terminal's `^C` and `^Z` reach it along with the program. `pgid: 0` makes it lead a new group in the program's session, set before it runs, so a `kill(-pid)` right after the call reaches it and everything it starts (`npx`, then `node`); on the program's terminal that group is in the background, so a read from the terminal stops it with `SIGTTIN` (`EIO` when it ignores `SIGTTIN`), as on Linux. `pgid: N` joins group N, which must be in the program's session: `EPERM` for another session's group, `ESRCH` when there is none. `session: true` makes it lead a new session and a new group (`setsid`, what Node's `detached: true` does on POSIX): its pid is its `pgid` and its `sid`, it has no controlling terminal (`/dev/tty` is `ENXIO`) and the terminal's keys don't signal it. `session` together with `pgid`, or a `pgid` that isn't a non-negative integer, is `EINVAL`. A command that does not exist rejects with `ENOENT`. `parentDeathSignal` (a number or `'SIGTERM'`, as Linux's `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG`) is sent to the child when the program ends, killed or not: to the child's whole group when it leads one (`pgid: 0` or `session: true`), else to the child alone. It's sent with the program's credentials, so a child the program may no longer signal (one that gained privilege) doesn't get it. If the program has already ended by the time the spawn completes, it's sent at once. A child of its own (not a group leader) that has been waited for gets nothing, and `sync.run` has no such option. A bad signal is `EINVAL`;
- `wait(pid, { untraced })` resolves with `{ status }` once a child ends, or `{ status: 128 + signal, signal }` when a signal ended it, without blocking the program; with `untraced: true` it also resolves when the child stops, with `{ status: 128 + signal, signal, stopped: true }`, as `waitpid` with `WUNTRACED` (a child's `wait({ untraced })` is the same), and options that aren't an object are `EINVAL`; `kill(pid, signal)` signals any process, by number or as `'SIGTERM'` (the default);
- `sudo(args)`, what the [`sudo`](#sudo) command calls: `args` are its arguments after `sudo`, and it resolves with `{ pid }`, the command the kernel started as the program's child, to `wait` for; `{ run }`, an argv to run as the caller (with `users: 'off'`); `{ list }`, the text of `sudo -l`; or `{ error, errno }`, the message to print;
- `crontab({ action, user?, text? })`, what the [`crontab`](#cron) command calls: `action` is `'list'`, `'remove'` or `'install'` (with the table as `text`), on the caller's own table or, for an effective uid of 0, on `user`'s. It resolves with `{ text }` (the table for `list`, `''` otherwise) or `{ error }`, the message to print. A malformed call is `EINVAL`;
- `net`: `listen({ port, host, backlog })` and `connect({ host, port, signal })` (`host` defaults to `127.0.0.1`) on the kernel's loopback, the same sockets WASIX programs use, so a JS server is reached by other processes, by the page as `<port>.kernel.localhost` (`loopbackFetch`, `dial`) and by attached clients. A listener has its `port` (as bound, so port `0` picks a free one), `accept({ signal })`, `close()` and `for await` over its connections. A connection has `local` and `remote` addresses, `readable` and `writable` web streams (closing the writable shuts down the sending side, so the peer reads the end), and `close()`. The loopback is IPv4 (`127.0.0.1` by default, or `0.0.0.0`), so an IPv6 host such as `::1` fails with `EADDRNOTAVAIL` for both `listen` and `connect`, and binding and connecting fail as they would for a WASIX program (`EADDRINUSE`, `ECONNREFUSED`, `EADDRNOTAVAIL`). A blocked accept, read or write waits without holding anything in the kernel, so aborting it or closing the socket leaves nothing behind. A write to a connection the peer closed fails with `EPIPE` and no `SIGPIPE`;
- `net.connect({ host, port, tls: { servername, alpn, rejectUnauthorized, ca, path, cert, key, passphrase, sni, checkHostname } })` takes one of two paths. By default it's **A1, TLS through the transport** ([#914](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/914)), described first below. For a destination the uplink routes it can also be **A2, a TLS handshake in the kernel** ([#922](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/922)), described after it. `conn.tls.path` (`'transport'` or `'kernel'`) and `conn.tls.substitutes` say which one was taken.
  - **What it does:** there's no handshake in the kernel. The connection carries plain HTTP/1.1 (and WebSocket upgrades) to the realm proxy's HTTP path for the origin `https://<servername or host>[:port]`, exactly as a `CONNECT` through `127.0.0.1:3128` does after the kernel terminates its TLS. The transport (the page's `fetch` or WebSocket, or the uplink's) makes the real TLS connection and verifies it. So `https.request` over a socket, keep-alive agents, `agent-base` and `wss://` clients work with the transport's semantics. That includes sealed placeholders in headers, which reach the transport exactly as from `ctx.fetch`.
  - **The `tls` property:** the connection's `tls` is `{ path: 'transport', substitutes: true, protocol: 'transport', alpnProtocol: 'http/1.1', authorized: true, servername }`. `authorized` means the transport verified the server. There's no peer certificate chain and no TLS version of the kernel's own, so don't read it as a real handshake's result.
  - **Options:** the transport always verifies, so `rejectUnauthorized: false` is accepted and changes nothing. Outside the uplink, a custom `ca` is refused (`ENOTSUP`), and so is an `alpn` list without `http/1.1` (`EPROTONOSUPPORT`). The kernel's own loopback (`localhost`, `*.localhost`, `127/8`, `0.0.0.0`, `::1`, as `servername` or as `host`) is `ENOTSUP` at connect, as `ctx.fetch` refuses `https:` and `wss:` there. Connect in plain text to a kernel server. `host.slicc.internal` goes to the transport under its policy, as for `ctx.fetch`.
  - **Non-HTTP protocols aren't carried:**
    - the well-known TLS ports of other protocols (465 SMTPS, 993 IMAPS, 995 POP3S, 636 LDAPS, 853, 8883 MQTT, 6697 IRC, 5671 AMQP and the like) are refused at `connect` with `EPROTONOSUPPORT`;
    - non-HTTP first bytes on any other port get a `400` and a close;
    - a server-speaks-first protocol on another port (the client sends nothing) waits for the proxy's 120 s idle limit.
    - For a destination the uplink routes, these cases take A2 instead (below).
  - **Which path** (A2 needs a destination the uplink routes, `net.destination(host) === 'uplink'`; an uplink-routed host on 443 with default options stays on A1, so its sealed placeholders keep working):

    | The call | Uplink destination | Any other destination |
    |---|---|---|
    | default options (443 or another HTTP port) | A1 | A1 |
    | a custom `ca` | **A2** | `ENOTSUP` |
    | `alpn` without `http/1.1` | **A2** | `EPROTONOSUPPORT` |
    | a well-known non-HTTP TLS port (465, 993, 995, 636, 853, 8883, 6697, 5671 …) | **A2** | `EPROTONOSUPPORT` |
    | `tls: { path: 'kernel' }` | **A2** | `ENOTSUP` |
    | `cert` and `key`, `sni: false` or `checkHostname: false` | **A2** | `ENOTSUP` |
    | `tls: { path: 'transport' }` | A1 | A1 |
    | `connect({ socket: conn, host, tls })` (STARTTLS) | **A2** | `EOPNOTSUPP` |

  - **A2, the handshake in the kernel** ([#922](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/922)): the kernel dials the destination through the uplink as raw TCP and runs a TLS client there, with [`@ai-ecoverse/wasm-tls-engine`](https://www.npmjs.com/package/@ai-ecoverse/wasm-tls-engine) (4.2.0-4 or later, loaded on the first A2 connect). The connection is then an ordinary uplink socket that carries the decrypted stream, so any protocol works: SMTP, IMAP, Postgres, MQTT, HTTP/2.
    - **Verification** is required. The chain must reach a trusted root, the leaf must name `servername` (default `host`), and every certificate must be within its validity by the page's clock, with serverAuth usage. The trusted roots are Mozilla's set the engine package ships (`dist/roots.der`, its date in the package's `slicc.tls.roots`), read on the first A2 connect without a `ca`. A `ca` (PEM text, DER bytes, or a list of them) **replaces** them, as in Node.
    - **A refusal** rejects the connect with Node's code: `ERR_TLS_CERT_ALTNAME_INVALID`, `UNABLE_TO_GET_ISSUER_CERT_LOCALLY`, `CERT_HAS_EXPIRED`, `CERT_NOT_YET_VALID` or `INVALID_PURPOSE`, with the engine's reason as `detail`. A failed handshake otherwise is `ERR_SSL_HANDSHAKE_FAILURE`.
    - **`rejectUnauthorized: false`** finishes the handshake whatever the chain says and reports `authorized: false` with `authorizationError`.
    - **`servername`** must be a host name (letters, digits and `-` in labels of at most 63, at most 253 in all, one trailing dot allowed) or an IP literal, else `EINVAL` before anything is dialled. An IP literal sends no SNI and must match an iPAddress subjectAltName.
    - **The `tls` property** is the handshake's: `{ path: 'kernel', substitutes: false, verified, protocol: 'TLSv1.3' or 'TLSv1.2', alpnProtocol (the agreed protocol, or false), authorized, authorizationError?, servername, peerCertificates, cipher, clientCertificate, sni, hostnameChecked, hostnameMatches? }`.
      - `peerCertificates` is the server's chain as DER, leaf first.
      - `verified` is the kernel's own verdict on the session, whatever the caller's flags: the chain verifies against the kernel's **bundled roots** (never a `ca` the caller passed, which only decides `authorized`) and the leaf names `servername`, which is a host name, not an IP literal. `rejectUnauthorized: false`, `checkHostname: false` and `sni: false` don't change it; a custom `ca` makes it false even when the chain would pass both. Sealed substitution in an A2 session is gated on it, or on a vault anchor (below; [#1090](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1090)). `verifiedBy` is `'bundled'` when `verified` is true, otherwise `'none'`. It's never a claim the kernel can't back at connect. The vault's requests use the vault's own names for the same verdicts: `'roots'` where `conn.tls` says `'bundled'`, and `'anchored'` for a vault anchor.
      - `cipher` is the negotiated suite as Node's `getCipher()` gives it: `{ name, standardName, version }`, where `name` is OpenSSL's (`ECDHE-RSA-AES128-GCM-SHA256`) and `standardName` IANA's. As in Node, `version` is the suite's own version, not the session's: `TLSv1.0` for a CBC-SHA suite on a TLS 1.2 session.
      - `clientCertificate` says whether a client certificate was offered. `sni` says whether the server name was sent: it's false with `sni: false` and for an IP-literal `servername`, which TLS never sends.
      - `hostnameChecked` says whether the kernel checked the leaf against `servername`. `hostnameMatches` says whether it names it.
      - With `rejectUnauthorized: false`, `chainError` says what is wrong with the chain apart from the name, with Node's names, and is absent when the chain is fine:
        - a self-signed leaf alone is `DEPTH_ZERO_SELF_SIGNED_CERT`;
        - an untrusted chain ending in a self-signed root is `SELF_SIGNED_CERT_IN_CHAIN`;
        - self-signed means the certificate's own key verifies its signature (RSA PKCS#1 v1.5, RSA-PSS, ECDSA P-256/384/521 or Ed25519; a signature the kernel can't verify is not self-signed), not just that its issuer and subject names match, so a self-issued certificate signed by another key, such as a CA rollover certificate, is not self-signed, as in Node ([#1059](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1059));
        - a lone leaf whose issuer is unknown is `UNABLE_TO_VERIFY_LEAF_SIGNATURE`;
        - an untrusted longer chain is `UNABLE_TO_GET_ISSUER_CERT_LOCALLY`;
        - otherwise it's the reason's code, such as `CERT_HAS_EXPIRED`.

        It's never `ERR_TLS_CERT_ALTNAME_INVALID`; that's `hostnameMatches: false`. `authorized` and `authorizationError` are as before.
    - **A client certificate** ([#1004](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1004)): `cert` (PEM text, DER bytes or a list: the leaf first, then its intermediates) and `key` (PEM, PKCS#1, SEC1 or PKCS#8, or DER), with `passphrase` for an encrypted key, as in Node.
      - The key goes to the engine before anything is dialled, and the kernel keeps no copy of it (nor of the passphrase) once the engine has it.
      - Before dialling, `cert` without `key` (or the reverse) is `EINVAL`. A key that doesn't match the leaf is `ERR_OSSL_X509_KEY_VALUES_MISMATCH`, a wrong passphrase `ERR_OSSL_BAD_DECRYPT`, a missing one `ERR_OSSL_CRYPTO_INTERRUPTED_OR_CANCELLED`, and a key the engine can't read `ERR_OSSL_UNSUPPORTED`.
      - `pfx` is `ENOTSUP`: pass `cert` and `key`.
      - A server that requires a certificate and gets none refuses with an alert. On TLS 1.2 that fails the connect (`ERR_SSL_HANDSHAKE_FAILURE`). On TLS 1.3 the client's side of the handshake is already done, so the next read or write fails with `ERR_SSL_FATAL_ALERT`, as Node's fails after `secureConnect`.
      - **A fatal alert from the server**, at the handshake or after it, is in the error: its `detail` ends in ` (alert <n>)`, and a JS program's error has `alert: n`, the alert's description number (116 certificate_required, 40 handshake_failure).
    - **`sni: false`** sends no server name. The leaf is still checked against `servername` exactly as with SNI.
    - **`checkHostname: false`** checks the chain (trust, validity, usage) but **not that the leaf names `servername`**. The connection reports `hostnameChecked: false`, and `hostnameMatches` says what the skipped check found.
      - **The caller must then check the server's identity itself**, as slicc-jsh does with Node's `checkServerIdentity` and `peerCertificates[0]`; pass it only when the program supplied one.
      - With `rejectUnauthorized: false` it's `EINVAL`.
    - **STARTTLS:** `net.connect({ socket: conn, host, tls })` upgrades an open plain connection to an uplink destination in place, as Node's `tls.connect({ socket })` does. It resolves with a connection whose streams now carry the decrypted bytes and whose `tls` is the session. Don't hold a pending read on the plain connection while it upgrades. Pass `host` (or `tls.servername`): the plain connection only knows the address it reached, so without a name the certificate is checked against that IP, and a certificate for the host name fails.
    - **Sealed values in an A2 session** ([#1090](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1090)) are substituted only at a credential position, only in a session the kernel verified itself (by its bundled roots, or by a vault anchor), and only as the vault allows. `substitutes` means the session **may** substitute: a placeholder at a recognized position will be offered to the vault, and whether a value comes back is the vault's and the anchor check's decision. It's true when all of these hold:
      - `verified` is true, or the engine can verify a chain on its own (wasm-tls-engine 4.2.0-5's `tls_chain_verify`) and the server name isn't an IP literal, so a vault anchor may verify the session (below);
      - ALPN didn't agree `h2`;
      - a vault is connected;
      - the uplink said which exit the connection leaves by (`UplinkConn.exit`: `tailscale` or `device:<id>`, the only exits an uplink connection takes). Without one, or with any other value, nothing is substituted.

      Then:
      - **HTTP/1.1:** if the **first request head** the program writes on the session (it starts with a standard method) has placeholders (`fnox_proxy_<24 hex>`) in exactly one header line, either in the value or in the `user:password` of `Basic` credentials, the kernel asks the vault once (`sessionMaterialize`, below) for that header's position.
      - **SMTP:** a session whose first line is `EHLO` or `HELO` (implicit TLS, or after a STARTTLS upgrade; bytes written before the upgrade are never substituted) is read line by line, and only the `AUTH` exchange is a position:
        - `AUTH PLAIN <blob>`, or the line after a bare `AUTH PLAIN`: the kernel decodes the blob, and asks the vault (`auth-plain`) only when the placeholders are in the **password field**. A placeholder in the user or authorization identity goes out as written. It substitutes there and re-encodes.
        - `AUTH LOGIN`: the password line (the second line, or the first after `AUTH LOGIN <user>`) is decoded, substituted (`auth-login`) and re-encoded; the user line goes out as written.
        - Every other line goes out as written. After the first `AUTH`, or once `MAIL`, `RCPT`, `DATA` or `BDAT` is seen, so is the rest of the session. A line still unfinished after 2 s, or longer than 4 KiB, is sent as written, and so is the rest.
      - **pg:** a session that starts with a protocol 3 `StartupMessage` (after `sslmode`'s `SSLRequest` upgrade, or with direct TLS) is followed message by message. The only position is the **`PasswordMessage` answering `AuthenticationCleartextPassword` (`R` 3)**, as the program read it from the server. The password is substituted (`password-cleartext`) and the message is framed again. **SCRAM-SHA-256** (`R` 10, the pg 14+ default) works with an unmodified client:
        - **Only where a value can be substituted:** the kernel follows SCRAM only in a session where a sealed value could reach this host: one the bundled roots verified, or one a vault anchor verified (`sessionAnchors` found an anchor for this destination; it's asked once, when the pg session starts). In any other session, such as one verified by the program's own `ca` (a private-CA server, `sslrootcert`) or not verified at all (`sslmode=require`), the mechanisms, the GS2 header and `c=` pass through byte for byte, so a program's own password with any `channel_binding` works as it does without the kernel ([slicc-jsh#584](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-jsh/issues/584)).
        - **In an anchored session:** `-PLUS` is not offered and the server side is `n,,`, so a program's own password with `channel_binding=prefer` against a `-PLUS` server is refused there, and `require` fails closed, until the binding is taken from the anchor-verified leaf ([#1259](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1259)).
        - **What the program is offered:** the server's mechanisms. `SCRAM-SHA-256-PLUS` is offered only when the kernel can bind to the server: a verified session whose leaf certificate's signature hash is SHA-256, SHA-384 or SHA-512, with MD5 and SHA-1 counting as SHA-256 (RFC 5929 §4.1). With any other hash (RSA-PSS or Ed25519, say), `-PLUS` is dropped. So libpq's default `channel_binding=prefer`, `require`, and node-postgres' `enableChannelBinding` all work.
        - **Channel binding (`tls-server-end-point`):** the kernel changes only what the server would refuse.
          - `n,,` goes out as written.
          - `y,,` goes out as written to a server that didn't offer `-PLUS`. Otherwise it becomes `SCRAM-SHA-256-PLUS` with `p=tls-server-end-point,,` (or `n,,` when the kernel can't bind), because pg refuses `y,,` from a client after offering `-PLUS`.
          - `p=tls-server-end-point,,` keeps its header. Its `c=` binding data becomes the hash of the leaf the kernel verified, when the program's differs.
          - Any other `p=` type is changed to the kernel's choice.

          So a program that asks for binding is bound to the real server. A program using its own password in such a session still authenticates whenever nothing had to change: `n,,`, `y,,` without `-PLUS`, or a right `p=`.
        - **The vault:** substitution with `-PLUS` (a program's `p=tls-server-end-point`) needs slicc-vault ≥ 1.15.1. An older vault refuses `p=` with `EINVAL` and fails closed. A program's `p=tls-unique` or `p=tls-exporter` fails in a substituting session either way: the vault refuses it with a placeholder (`EINVAL`), and with the program's own password, the kernel's rewrite to `tls-server-end-point` invalidates its proof. PostgreSQL supports only `tls-server-end-point` anyway.
        - **Two auth messages:** the vault and the check against the placeholder see the program's own messages (P). The server sees the kernel's (S), with its header and `c=`. The real proof is computed over S, and the server's signature is verified over S before the program gets the placeholder's signature over P.
        - **The match:** the kernel sends the three SCRAM messages and that proof to the vault (`scram-sha-256`, with no placeholders), and the vault names the one bound placeholder whose proof matches.
        - **The exchange:** the kernel recomputes the proof from the value and sends that instead. It checks the server's final signature against the value **before** it hands the program the signature its own placeholder-based check expects. A server whose signature doesn't verify, or that answers with an error, ends the session (`EPROTO`) without passing it on. The salted password and the derived keys are wiped after the exchange.
        - **Requirements:** a GS2 header without an authorization identity (`n,,`, `y,,` or `p=<type>,,`), whose `c=` starts with it, and the bare RFC 5802 messages; at most 100000 iterations.
        - **A stale vault value:** on a verified session, a value the server no longer accepts makes its final signature fail to verify. The program sees `EPROTO`, not pg's `28P01`. This is by design: the kernel never passes on a signature it couldn't check.

        Anything else goes out as written: the startup, an MD5 (`R` 5) answer, every later message, and a frame over 16 KiB. A server whose `pg_hba` asks for MD5 gets the placeholder, and refuses.
      - **Vault anchors** (§3.19.6) are for private-CA services such as managed pg and corporate APIs.
        - **The request:** at the first credential position, before it asks for any value, the kernel asks the vault `sessionAnchors { stamp, session, host, port, protocol, exit }`. That request carries no value. The vault answers with the CAs (`anchors`, DER) and SPKI pins (`pins`, sha256) of the session rules for that host, port, protocol and exit.
        - **The check:** the kernel re-verifies the server's chain against a store holding **only** that answer, never the program's `ca`, with the same checks as a session. An anchor that issues must be a CA. A non-CA anchor, or a pin on the leaf, matches only the exact presented leaf, and the name must still match. A pin on an issuer makes that certificate of the chain the anchor.
        - **The result:** a match makes the session's `sessionMaterialize` say `verifiedBy: 'anchored'`, with `anchor` set to the anchor DER's sha256 or the pin itself. Without one, a session the bundled roots verified asks as before. A rule with anchors refuses those unless it says `roots = true`. Otherwise nothing is asked. `conn.tls` is reported at connect, before this check, so an anchorable session shows `verified: false`, `verifiedBy: 'none'` and `substitutes: true` (eligible). In v1 the anchored verdict isn't reported to the program. The secret's owner sees it: every `sessionMaterialize` names how the session was trusted (`verifiedBy: 'roots'`, or `'anchored'` with the matching anchor's sha256 or pin), and the vault records that with the substitution.
        - **Not supported:** name constraints on an anchor, which Mbed TLS can't parse when critical, so such an anchor never verifies.
      - **What goes out:** the placeholders the vault answers with a value of at least 8 bytes go out as that value (`Basic` decoded, substituted and re-encoded). Everything else goes out as written:
        - the request line and the body;
        - other headers;
        - a head with placeholders in two header lines;
        - later requests on the same connection;
        - anything once the head passes 16 KiB, or once the program pauses for 2 s in the middle of it;
      - **What's read back:** once a process has had a value substituted for a host, everything its whole process tree reads from any A2 session to that host is scrubbed: the process itself, its ancestors and every descendant (forked, spawned or exec'd, before or after the substitution; a forked child's own new session included) with [slicc-scrub](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-scrub), as S2 does: the value comes back as its placeholder, and a fragment or an encoded form (JSON, percent, HTML, base64, hex) as `[redacted:sealed]`. The scrub stays until the whole tree has ended. A process outside the tree isn't covered. A value shorter than 8 bytes is never substituted, as it couldn't be scrubbed.
      - **The bound:** the server the value goes to is the one the vault rule names, verified against the kernel's own roots. Substitution trusts that server not to reflect the value back in a form the scrub doesn't know (encrypted under a key the program chose, say). That's the same residual as A1's.
      - **Elsewhere:** a placeholder written into any other A2 session reaches the server as written. It isn't the secret, so nothing leaks.
    - **Limits:** no revocation checking (neither OCSP nor CRLs, as with Node's default), no session resumption, and the clock is the page's `Date.now()`.
- `net.resolve(name, { family, signal })` asks the kernel's resolver, which WASIX `resolve` and Emscripten `getaddrinfo` use too: entries in a real `/etc/hosts`, the kernel's own names (`localhost`, the node name, `host.slicc.internal`) and then the uplink's DNS ([#913](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/913)). It answers the addresses, in the resolver's order, for `family` `4`, `6` or `0` (both, IPv4 first; the default). A name with no answer rejects with a `JsCallError` of code `ENOTFOUND`, as Node's `dns.lookup` does. A lookup the uplink fails or doesn't answer within 10 s rejects with `EAI_AGAIN` (the kernel's `sock-resolve` is `ETIMEDOUT` there, `ENOENT` for no answer), as Node's does for a transient failure, so a client can retry; the failure isn't cached ([#1363](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1363)). Another family rejects with `EAI_FAMILY`, and an aborted `signal` with its reason.
- `websocket(url, { protocols, headers, signal })`, a WebSocket over the kernel's transport (its `websocket`, so `fetchTransport({ webSocket: true })` in a browser), shaped like `WebSocketStream`. It resolves once the socket is open, with `url`, the `protocol` the far end chose (always one the program offered: a transport that hands back a socket with another fails the open with `EPROTO`, while browsers and Node's `WebSocket`, which `fetchTransport()` uses, fail such a handshake themselves without saying why, so the open fails with `ECONNREFUSED`), a `readable` of messages (`string` for text, `Uint8Array` for binary), a `writable` for sending them, `closed` (the `{ code, reason }` of the close, from either side, whether or not the program reads) and `close({ code, reason })`. An `http(s):` URL is taken as `ws(s):`. Sending waits while more than 1 MiB is buffered. A signal aborts the open, and after it the socket. A transport without WebSockets rejects with a `TypeError` whose `cause.code` is `ENOTSUP`. One that cannot connect rejects with the `code` its error carries (an errno, such as `EAI_NONAME`, `ECONNREFUSED`, `ETIMEDOUT` or `EPROTO`), else `ECONNREFUSED`. `ws://host.slicc.internal:port` reaches the real machine's loopback, as it does through the proxy;
- `fetch(input, init)`, the web's `fetch` over the kernel's transport, the one the proxy uses, so the embedder's transport, its WebSocket and proxy settings and the kernel's routes apply. The response body streams as the program reads it. On a transport with `traits.streamingBodies` (below), a `ReadableStream` body (with `duplex: 'half'`, which `Request` requires) streams to it as the program produces it, with no size limit of the kernel's, and a redirect that would send it again (a 307 or 308) rejects with a `TypeError`, as the fetch standard has it. Other bodies (a string, a buffer, a `Blob`, a `FormData`) can be sent again, so they go whole even there. Otherwise the request body is read whole first, and one larger than the transport's `maxRequestBody` is answered `413`, as through the proxy. A signal aborts the request at any stage. Redirects follow the fetch rules: at most 20, a 301 or 302 after a `POST` and a 303 turn into a `GET` without a body, `Authorization` is dropped across origins, and `redirect: 'manual'` returns the redirect itself. On a transport that follows redirects on its own (`fetchTransport()` in a browser) only `'follow'` works: `'manual'` and `'error'` reject with a `TypeError` whose `cause.code` is `ENOTSUP`, since such a transport never shows the program a redirect. A status the transport chose comes back as a response whose body is the reason. An unreachable host, or no transport at all, rejects with a `TypeError` whose `cause.code` is `ECONNREFUSED` or `ENETUNREACH`, as Node's `fetch` does. A `data:` URL is answered by `fetch` itself, as the fetch standard decodes it, without a transport: for any method, a `HEAD` without a body, and its type parsed and serialized as a MIME type (lowercase, no spaces, invalid parameters dropped), as Node's `fetch` answers; any other scheme than `http:` and `https:` rejects with a `TypeError` whose `cause.code` is `EPROTONOSUPPORT`, at the start as for a redirect target.
