A URL on one of the fetch standard's bad ports (25, 6000 and the rest) rejects too, as Node's `fetch` does: a `TypeError` `fetch failed` whose `cause` is an `Error` `bad port` (no `code`), at the start and as a redirect target, on the kernel's own loopback too ([#822](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/822)). `redirect: 'error'` rejects with a `TypeError` whose `cause` is an `Error` `unexpected redirect`, and a body whose connection ends early fails its read with `TypeError` `terminated` whose `cause` is undici's: `name` `SocketError`, `message` `other side closed`, `code` `UND_ERR_SOCKET`. The request goes out with its headers in the order the program gave them (an object's or an array's order; a `Headers` object's is sorted), lowercase as `Headers` keeps them. The global `fetch` stays withheld;
- **`fetch` and `websocket` to the kernel's own loopback:** a URL whose host is `127.0.0.0/8`, `0.0.0.0`, `[::1]`, `localhost` or `*.localhost` goes to the kernel's own loopback, as a guest's sockets do (`<port>.kernel.localhost` takes its port from the name). So it reaches whatever listens on that port in the kernel, the CDP facade at `SLICC_CDP_URL` and its `/json` included. It never goes to the transport: with nothing listening, it rejects with a `TypeError` whose `cause.code` is `ECONNREFUSED`, with or without a transport. The kernel speaks HTTP/1.1 and WebSocket (RFC 6455) to the listener itself, and `fetch` handles the redirects and compressed bodies it answers, as for any server. Its request head starts as undici's does, `host` then `connection`, then the program's headers in their order, then `content-length`. It sends `connection: close` where undici sends `keep-alive`, because each request is a connection of its own; a server sees no other difference. A WebSocket's `closed` settles after the closing handshake once the server has closed the connection (RFC 6455 §7.1.4), as undici's `close` event does, or at the latest 5 s after the Close frames. A refused upgrade rejects with a `TypeError` whose `cause` is an `Error` naming the status, `the server answered the upgrade with 503 Service Unavailable`, with `code` `EPROTO`. `https:` and `wss:` there reject with `ENOTSUP`. The listener sees a connection from `127.0.0.1` whose `SO_PEERCRED` is the program's pid, uid and gid, as if the program had connected itself. `host.slicc.internal` still goes to the transport as the real machine's loopback, for `fetch` too, under the transport's policy (`nodeTransport({ hostLoopback })`). A redirect from any other URL into the kernel's loopback rejects with `cause.code` `EACCES`, so a server elsewhere can't steer a program's request to the kernel's own servers; `redirect: 'manual'` still returns it;
- `tty`, for programs on a terminal: `getAttr(fd)` and `setAttr(fd, termios)` read and set its termios (`{ c_iflag, c_oflag, c_cflag, c_lflag, c_cc }`, Linux values), `setRaw(fd, raw)` switches raw mode on as libuv (and Node's `setRawMode`) does (no echo, no line editing, no `^C` signal, each key read as it comes) and off again to the termios it had before (per terminal: after raw mode on fds 0 and 1 of one terminal, turning it off on either restores the termios from before the first), `size(fd)` answers `{ columns, rows }`, and `foreground(fd)` and `setForeground(fd, pgrp)` get and set the foreground process group, under the same job-control rules as other programs. A window resize arrives as `SIGWINCH` through `signals`. A terminal the program left raw is set back when the program ends, whether it returns, exits or throws (a program killed by a signal cannot do so). On a descriptor that is not a terminal, each call fails with `ENOTTY`; `sync.tty` has `getAttr`, `setAttr` and `setRaw` in synchronous form, sharing the saved modes with `tty`, so a raw mode set either way is set back when the program ends. `sync.tty.setRaw(fd, raw)` returns only once the terminal's line discipline has the new mode, so input that arrives after it returns is never cooked or echoed under the old mode, as Node's synchronous `setRawMode` promises (#1109);
- `exit(status)`, which ends the program once its pending writes are done;
- `onUncaught(listener)` and `onUnhandledRejection(listener)`, which take the errors the program throws outside any call and the promises it leaves rejected (`listener(error, promise)`), as Node's `uncaughtException` and `unhandledRejection` events do. While a listener is registered, the program decides what such an error means; each call answers a function that removes its listener. A listener that throws ends the program as an unhandled error does.

A program that throws, or leaves an error or a rejected promise unhandled with no listener for it, ends with status 1 and the message on stderr; one whose module cannot be loaded, or exports no function, ends with 126. A write to a pipe whose reader is gone ends the program with 141, unless it ignores or catches `SIGPIPE` (an ignore inherited from its parent counts), in which case the write fails with `EPIPE`. Errors carry a POSIX `code`. Reads and writes of pipes, terminals and sockets go through the kernel's asynchronous operations, so a program blocked reading stdin can still write and handle signals; other calls of one process are made one at a time. A JS program cannot `fork`, and threads it starts itself are not the kernel's.

The module has the trust of an Emscripten program's glue or a WASI program's imports module: it comes from a package that was installed, and it runs in the worker of its own process. The kernel withholds the worker's own ways out before it loads the module: `XMLHttpRequest`, `WebSocketStream`, `WebTransport`, `EventSource`, `indexedDB`, `caches`, `cookieStore`, `SharedWorker`, `close`, Chrome's old file system API (`webkitRequestFileSystem`, `webkitRequestFileSystemSync`, `webkitResolveLocalFileSystemURL` and `webkitResolveLocalFileSystemSyncURL`, which reach the origin's OPFS), and `navigator.storage`, `navigator.storageBuckets`, `navigator.locks`, `navigator.serviceWorker`, `navigator.usb`, `navigator.hid` and `navigator.serial` are `undefined`, also through their prototypes, and a worker where one of them cannot be removed refuses to run the program (126). `fetch`, `WebSocket`, `BroadcastChannel`, `importScripts`, `Worker` and `postMessage` are `undefined` too, but stay writable and configurable, so a program can install its own over `ctx.fetch` and `ctx.websocket`, a `BroadcastChannel` within the program (as slicc-jsh does), or the globals napi-rs's wasm32-wasi worker template assigns (`Object.assign(globalThis, { self, require, Worker, importScripts, postMessage })`); the worker's own are gone before the program runs, so deleting the program's brings back `undefined`. `self` stays the global, but as an own writable slot of the scope, so a program can assign its own (the template does) without reaching the worker's. The platform's `BroadcastChannel`, which would reach the origin's other contexts around the kernel, is gone from the scope and its prototypes before the program runs, so deleting the program's own brings back `undefined`, not it; a worker where it can't be removed refuses to run the program (126), as for the others. A program's files are the kernel's, through the context; it reaches the kernel only through its own process's system calls, as the context makes them, and no other process's. This is a guard against programs going around the kernel, not a sandbox: `import()` of a URL is a request the kernel cannot withhold. In a browser, every process worker gets the same lockdown before any program code runs, so an Emscripten program's glue and a WASI program's imports module (any user can write either) see the same globals `undefined`, and a worker where one cannot be removed ends the process with 126. Under the Node entry, every process worker gets the same lockdown before any program code runs: an Emscripten program's glue, a WASI program's imports module and a package driver all see `require`, `process`, `module`, `__dirname` and every Node builtin `undefined`. An Emscripten program's worker is also shaped like a dedicated worker's, as it is in a browser, with `WorkerGlobalScope`, `self` (the global) and `location.href` (the glue's own path in the kernel as a `file:` URL, never the host's install path) as writable slots, so a glue built with `-sENVIRONMENT=web,worker,node` and assertions (`emcc -O0`) reads it as a worker, not a JS shell (#1238); JS and WASI programs don't get them (`require`, `module`, `exports`, `__filename` and `__dirname` writable and configurable, as `process` is, so a program can define its own, never Node's), and `import` of a builtin or a non-`data:` URL fails with `ERR_ACCESS_DENIED`.

Under the Node entry, the kernel also withholds the host's Node from the program before its module loads ([#297](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/297)). `localStorage` and `sessionStorage` are `undefined` for good. `require`, `module`, `exports`, `__filename`, `__dirname`, `process`, `global`, `setImmediate` and `clearImmediate` are `undefined` too, but stay writable and configurable, so a program can install its own (as slicc-jsh and napi-rs's wasi worker template do); Node's are gone before the program runs, so deleting the program's brings back `undefined`. `Buffer` stays Node's own, since Node's `Response`, `Request`, `Blob` and stream bodies read it when they run, and a program can replace it. Process workers share Node's memory allocator, so its `allocUnsafe` and `allocUnsafeSlow` zero-fill and its pool is off (`Buffer.poolSize` is 0): a program never sees memory another process freed. A host can also start Node with `--zero-fill-buffers`. Imports of Node's builtin modules (`node:fs`, `fs`, `node:worker_threads`, …) and of anything but a `data:` URL, `file:` included, fail with `ERR_ACCESS_DENIED`, whether static, dynamic or through `import.meta.resolve`. A process worker that exits or crashes on its own ends its process with 70. This is the same guard, not a sandbox: a host that runs code it does not trust, such as an agent's, needs its own isolation around the Node process as well.

## Network

Programs talk to each other over sockets, and to the outside world through a proxy that hands every request to a transport the page passes in.

```js
import { createKernel, fetchTransport } from './node_modules/@ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/dist/index.js';

const kernel = await createKernel({ network: { transport: fetchTransport() } });
await kernel.run(['curl', '-sS', 'https://registry.npmjs.org/@ai-ecoverse/wasm-bash/latest']);
```

- **Sockets**: `AF_INET` stream sockets on `127.x` and `AF_UNIX` sockets are kernel descriptors on one loopback network per kernel, so processes can serve and connect to each other. They survive `dup`, `fork` and `exec` and work with `select`, `poll` and WASIX epoll, like pipe ends. A bind of an `AF_UNIX` path socket creates a socket node there, as on Linux. The node is owned by the caller, its mode is `0777 & ~umask`, and `stat`, `ls -l` and `test -S` show it as a socket. The bind needs write and search permission on the directory, and an existing name is `EADDRINUSE`. A `connect` needs search permission on the directories and write permission on the node, so under `users: 'enforce'` a socket in a `0700` directory (gpg-agent's, ssh-agent's) is `EACCES` to other users. A missing name is `ENOENT`. `chmod` and `unlink` work on the node, `open` is `ENXIO`, and a renamed socket answers at its new name. Once its listener closes, the name stays as a stale socket: connect is `ECONNREFUSED` and a new bind is `EADDRINUSE` until it's removed. Socket names persist across a page reload, because `/tmp` and `/home` are OPFS, not tmpfs. A daemon that doesn't remove a stale socket before it binds gets `EADDRINUSE` after a reload, as on Linux with a persistent `/run`. On tmpfs and hostfs mounts the node is an owned empty regular file: its permissions apply, but it has no socket type. `SO_PEERCRED` isn't there yet. Abstract (`\0`) names stay out of the file system. A program's `connect` to a listener whose accept queue is full waits for a slot, as on Linux, instead of failing. A blocking connect waits, and a nonblocking one answers `EINPROGRESS` and turns writable once it's queued. After 30 s it fails with `ETIMEDOUT`, and it's `ECONNREFUSED` if the listener closes. Other addresses are unreachable (`ENETUNREACH`) unless the [uplink](#uplink) routes them: otherwise programs reach the outside world only through the proxy.

#### Datagram sockets (UDP)

WASIX programs get `AF_INET`/`AF_INET6` `SOCK_DGRAM` sockets, and `AF_UNIX` ones (see below). They are kernel descriptors like stream sockets: they survive `dup`, `fork` and `exec`, and work with `select`, `poll`, WASIX epoll and `fstat` (`SOCK_DGRAM`).
- **Addresses:** `bind` follows the stream rules: loopback or `0.0.0.0`/`::`, port `0` for an ephemeral port, the kernel's reserved ports refused (`EADDRINUSE`), any other address `EADDRNOTAVAIL`. The first send binds an unbound socket. UDP and TCP ports are separate.
- **Where a datagram goes** is decided as for a TCP `connect`, by the same route table: loopback (`127.0.0.0/8`, `::1`, `0.0.0.0`) is delivered inside the kernel, an address the uplink routes goes through `uplink.udp` when `traits.udp` is set, and everything else is `ENETUNREACH`: `host.slicc.internal`, link-local (`169.254.0.0/16`, so the metadata service too), reserved, multicast and broadcast (`224.0.0.0/4`, `240.0.0.0/4`, `255.255.255.255`, `ff00::/8`), with or without an uplink; an unspecified destination (`0.0.0.0`, `::`) is the kernel's loopback, as for TCP. Such a datagram never reaches the uplink.
- **One datagram per call:** `recv`/`read` returns one datagram, and a buffer that is too short gets its first bytes and loses the rest. `MSG_PEEK` leaves it queued. A datagram is at most 65 507 bytes (65 527 to an IPv6 address); a larger send is `EMSGSIZE`. A send with no address on an unconnected socket is `EDESTADDRREQ`.
- **`connect`** sets the default destination and keeps only datagrams from it. On a connected socket, a datagram to a loopback port nobody bound, or an uplink's report that the destination is unreachable, fails the next send or receive once with `ECONNREFUSED` (or the uplink's `EACCES`, `ENETUNREACH`, `EHOSTUNREACH`), and the socket polls readable until then (epoll reports `EPOLLERR` with `EPOLLIN`), as on Linux. An unconnected socket is told nothing.
- **Queues:** at most 512 datagrams or 256 KiB wait to be read; more are dropped. A socket polls writable while less than 256 KiB is on its way through the uplink.
- **Options:** `SO_TYPE`, `SO_ERROR`, `SO_DOMAIN`, `SO_RCVBUF` and `SO_SNDBUF` answer; `SO_BROADCAST` can't be turned on (`EOPNOTSUPP`), and the `IP_MULTICAST_*` options are `ENOPROTOOPT`. There is no multicast or broadcast.
- Emscripten programs have no datagram sockets yet.
- **The proxy** listens on `127.0.0.1:3128` from the first connection on. It takes absolute-form `http:` requests and `CONNECT` tunnels, which it terminates with a certificate for the requested host, issued by the kernel's own CA, then hands each request to the transport. Programs start with `http_proxy`, `https_proxy` (and the upper-case names) pointing at it, `no_proxy` covering loopback and the [node name](#node-name), and `SSL_CERT_FILE`, `CURL_CA_BUNDLE` and `GIT_SSL_CAINFO` pointing at the CA bundle in `/etc/ssl/certs/slicc-kernel-ca.pem`; variables in `env` override them. The CA's key never leaves WebCrypto; it is kept in IndexedDB, in `<metadata>-ca`, or in `slicc-kernel-ca` when `metadata` is the default or `false`, so kernels on one origin share one CA whether or not they keep metadata. `ca` names another database, and `ca: false` keeps a CA in memory for the kernel's lifetime. Each kernel adds its CA to the bundle instead of replacing it, so every kernel on the origin stays trusted; the bundle keeps the newest 16.
- **TLS** needs [`@ai-ecoverse/wasm-tls-engine`](https://www.npmjs.com/package/@ai-ecoverse/wasm-tls-engine) installed under `modules`, like a command. Without it, `CONNECT` to port 443 is answered `501` with the reason. A tunnel to another port is accepted, and what the program sends first decides: plain HTTP is served as `http://host:port`, without TLS, and a TLS handshake is terminated as above, or the tunnel closes when it cannot be.
- **The transport** is an object on the page with `traits` (`manualRedirects`, `encodedBodies`, `maxRequestBody`, and `crossOrigin`: `'cors'` or `'any'`) and `fetch(request)`, which answers `{ status, statusText, headers, body, cancel }` with `body` an async iterable of `Uint8Array`, and optionally `url` and `redirected: true` when it followed redirects itself (`fetchTransport()` does), so a JS program's `fetch` reports where it ended. The kernel pulls the body one chunk at a time, so a slow program slows the download. A rejection with a numeric `status` is answered with that status; any other with `502`.
- **Streamed request bodies** ([#238](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/238)): a request comes with its body whole, as `body` (a `Uint8Array`), unless the transport sets `traits.streamingBodies`. Then a body the kernel doesn't hold whole comes as `bodyStream`, an async iterable of `Uint8Array`, never together with `body`. That's an upload through the proxy (`Content-Length` or chunked, curl's `-T`, git's push) and a JS program's `fetch` with a stream body. The kernel reads from the program only as fast as the transport pulls, applies no `maxRequestBody` and reserves no memory for it. A transport with a limit of its own rejects with `status: 413`. When the transport answers before it has read the whole body, the proxy closes the program's connection after the response, since the rest of the body is still on it. A transport without the trait keeps getting whole bodies, capped at its `maxRequestBody`, so existing transports need no change. A transport that wraps another (a router, an injector, a logger) and passes on the inner `traits` must forward `bodyStream`, or not claim `streamingBodies`: a wrapper that rebuilds the request from `url`, `method`, `headers`, `body` and `signal` would drop a streamed upload and still answer. `nodeTransport()` streams (undici's `fetch` with `duplex: 'half'`). `fetchTransport({ streamingBodies: true })` turns it on for a page that knows its targets speak HTTP/2 or later, since Chrome streams a `fetch` upload only there, so it's off by default. The trait is also how an embedder's own transport, such as the `slicc` CLI link's exit, takes streamed uploads; across the page and the kernel's worker, and to attached clients, the body is pulled one chunk at a time. `fetchTransport()` is the plain `fetch` of the page, bound by CORS; its `hint` option is appended to the message of a request it could not make, so a program's 502 can name a way around CORS (`registry.npmjs.org` and jsDelivr allow it); an embedder with a way around CORS passes its own. A response body that delivers no data for `bodyIdleMs` (default 5 minutes; `0` or `Infinity` turns it off) fails with `ETIMEDOUT` instead of waiting forever, which matters because Chrome under heavy load can stop handing a page the rest of a body. A slow body that keeps delivering is never cut off.
- **Traits of the embedder's own:** `traits` may carry more fields than the kernel reads. The kernel keeps them, and attached clients get them as they are (`client.transport.traits`, `onTraits`). In TypeScript, `NetworkTraits` types its own fields and leaves any other as `unknown`. One is typed: `exit`, the default exit an embedder routes through, `{ kind, caps }` (`NetworkExit`). `kind` is `'local'`, `'remote'` or `'tailnet'` (or another string), `caps` lists what the exit carries (`['net', 'http', 'ws']`), and further fields are `unknown`. When traits change later, call `kernel.updateTransportTraits()`.
- **Redirect hops** ([#302](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/302)): a transport that follows redirects but can see them (a browser extension's `webRequest.onBeforeRedirect` can) sets `traits.redirectHops` and answers `redirects: [{ status, url, headers }]`, one entry per 3xx it followed, in order (`NetworkRedirectHop`). The kernel then treats it as one that does not follow. Programs get each 3xx as it was answered: through the proxy, curl, requests and `hf` see the real redirect, `X-Repo-Commit` and `ETag` included, and `ctx.fetch` with `redirect: 'manual'` answers it. The rest of the chain is held for the process that asked, at most 30 s and 8 chains per process (64 in all), with the final body unread. A follow-up from that process, with the method the redirect leads to and the same `Range`, `If-None-Match`, `If-Modified-Since`, `If-Range` and `Accept-Encoding` (and an `Authorization` if the first had one), is answered from it without a second request; any other request goes to the transport. A chain that expires or is pushed out is cancelled, and so is one whose follow-up arrives and does not match. After a redirect to another origin the follow-up is expected without `Authorization`, which HTTP clients drop when they follow one; a `Location` matches without its `#fragment`. The page's own `fetch` cannot show a redirect, so `fetchTransport()` in a page stays a transport that follows, and `redirect: 'manual'` fails there as before. `fetchTransport({ redirect: 'manual' })` hands redirects back unfollowed where the platform's `fetch` shows them (Node; a browser answers an opaque status 0), and `nodeTransport()` does so. A kernel socket also records who connected it (`SO_PEERCRED`): the connecting process's pid, uid and gid on the accepting side, and the listener's on the connecting side, kept when the socket passes to another process. The proxy uses this to keep each process's held chains its own.
- **WebSockets:** a `GET` with `Upgrade: websocket`, in absolute form or inside a tunnel, is bridged to the transport's optional `websocket({ url, protocols, headers, signal })`. It answers `{ protocol, buffered, send(data), messages, close(code, reason), closed }`, with `messages` an async iterable of strings and `Uint8Array`s and `closed` resolving with `{ code, reason }`; a transport that has it says so with `traits.websocket`. The program gets `101 Switching Protocols` with the far end's protocol, then its frames go out as messages and the far end's come back as frames. Pings are answered by the proxy, and close codes pass both ways (a code that may not go on the wire becomes 1000 or 1011). A message over 64 MiB, or more than 16 MiB of payload from the far end waiting for the program, closes with 1009. The proxy stops reading the program while 1 MiB of its messages wait to be sent. A refused connection is `502` with the reason. A transport without WebSockets answers `501` with a message naming the option below, and the kernel logs a warning once, so a program never hangs. `fetchTransport({ webSocket: true })` opens them with the page's `WebSocket` (or pass a constructor); they are off by default because a browser WebSocket carries the user's cookies for the target site, which `fetch` here never sends (`credentials: 'omit'`), so a program could reach a site as the user. A page also cannot send headers on a WebSocket, and an `https:` page cannot open `ws:` to anything but loopback, which is a `502` that says so. `nodeTransport()` has WebSockets on (Node keeps no cookies) and sends the request's headers. An open fails as soon as Node's `WebSocket` reports an error, also on Node 22, which never follows the error with a close. A transport's `websocket()` may reject with an `Error` whose `code` is an errno, and the kernel hands that on to the program. Node's `WebSocket` doesn't say why an open failed, so when one fails, `nodeTransport()` finds out: it looks up the host's name (`EAI_NONAME` when that fails), then makes one TCP connection to the target, or a TLS one for `wss:`, for at most 3 s, and closes it at once. A refusal there is `ECONNREFUSED`, an unreachable host `EHOSTUNREACH` or `ENETUNREACH`, no answer `ETIMEDOUT`, and a TLS failure (a bad certificate) or a server that takes the connection but refused the upgrade `EPROTO`. A server that refused the upgrade therefore sees a second connection. The diagnosis never runs when an open succeeds, and never in a page (`fetchTransport()`), whose failed opens stay `ECONNREFUSED`. The credential-free path for a page is the coming slicc-node relay (`localProxyTransport()`, phase 2 of [#204](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/204)), which has no WebSockets yet.
- **`localProxyTransport({ url, key })`** sends every request to a local proxy on loopback, such as [slicc-node](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-node) or slicc-swift, which fetches it without CORS. Redirects reach the program unfollowed, with every `Set-Cookie`; bodies arrive decoded. `probeLocalProxy({ url, key })` resolves with the proxy's capabilities, or `null` when it is gone or refuses the key, so a page can fall back to `fetchTransport()`. `checkLocalProxy({ url, key })` says why: it resolves with `{ state }`, which is `ready` (with `probe`), `blocked` (the user denied Chrome's Local Network Access permission, `loopback-network`), `unreachable` (nothing answered; its `permission` is `granted`, `prompt` or `unknown`, and with `prompt` the page cannot tell a dismissed prompt from a proxy that is not running, since Chrome asks only once it has a connection), `refused` (with `status` and `error`, such as a stale key) or `incompatible`. The permission is not consulted when the page itself is on loopback. Both take a `fetch` option, and the transport `maxRequestBody` (default 64 MiB) and `bodyIdleMs` like `fetchTransport`.
- **The host machine** is `host.slicc.internal` (`SLICC_HOST_LOOPBACK`), as `host.docker.internal` is in Docker, while `localhost` and `127.0.0.1` stay the kernel's own loopback. The proxy forwards `http(s)://host.slicc.internal:<port>/…` to the transport as `http(s)://127.0.0.1:<port>/…`, so it reaches the real machine's loopback through the local proxy or Node's `fetch` (through the page's own `fetch`, a request needs CORS from that server and Chrome's Local Network Access permission); other loopback addresses are still refused. This is HTTP and HTTPS through the proxy only: `/etc/hosts` and WASIX name resolution give the name `10.0.2.2`, QEMU's address for its host, so a program that connects a socket to it directly fails with `ENETUNREACH` instead of reaching the kernel's loopback.
- **Without a transport**, every request is answered `502` with `slicc-kernel: no network transport (createKernel({ network: { transport } }))`.

**`AF_UNIX` datagram sockets** ([#891](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/891)), for syslog-style logging and `sd_notify`-style readiness:
- **Binding:** `bind` to a path creates a socket file, with the same owner and permission checks as a stream socket. The directory must be writable. The file stays after `close`, until something unlinks it. An abstract name (`\0…`) binds without a file.
- **Sending:** `sendto(path)` needs write permission on the socket file. One send is one message. `recvfrom` returns it whole (or truncated, with `MSG_TRUNC`, to the buffer), with the sender's bound path as the address, or an empty path for an unbound or socketpair sender.
- **Connecting:** `connect(path)` sets the default destination for `send`/`write`. A receiver that is connected to another socket refuses everyone else's datagrams with `EPERM`. `socketpair(AF_UNIX, SOCK_DGRAM)` gives two connected, unnamed sockets.
- **Errors:** a missing path is `ENOENT`, a file no datagram socket is bound to is `ECONNREFUSED`, a stream socket's path is `EPROTOTYPE` (both ways), and a taken path is `EADDRINUSE`.
- **Flow control:** a receiver queue holds 512 messages or 256 KiB. A full one blocks a sender, or fails a non-blocking send with `EAGAIN`, as on Linux.
- **Not supported:** passing descriptors or credentials (`SCM_RIGHTS`, `SCM_CREDENTIALS`) over datagrams, and `AF_UNIX` datagrams for Emscripten and JS programs.

#### DNS

The kernel answers DNS itself, so DNS clients (BIND's `dig`, `host` and `nslookup`) see the same names as `getaddrinfo` (#1244).
- **Where it listens:** UDP and TCP port 53 on the kernel's loopback; `/etc/resolv.conf` names `127.0.0.53`, as systemd-resolved does, but any loopback address reaches it. TCP takes several queries on one connection (2-byte length framing), at most 64 connections at once, each closed after 10 s without a query or when an answer can't be written within 10 s (a client that stops reading). Over UDP at most 64 questions are answered at once; while that many wait (on the forwarder, say), more are dropped, as by a full receive queue. Port 53 is the kernel's, as the proxy's and CDP's ports are: a program's `bind` to it, root's included, is `EADDRINUSE` on UDP and TCP, so no program can answer other users' questions, and `kernel.dial` refuses it.
- **What it answers itself,** from the [kernel's resolver](#node-name): `A` and `AAAA` for `/etc/hosts` names, the node name, `localhost` and `*.localhost`, `host.slicc.internal`, and the [uplink](#uplink)'s names (a tray's `<device>.slicc.internal`, MagicDNS); `PTR` from `/etc/hosts` and the built-in entries (`127.0.0.0/8` is `localhost`). TTLs are 30 s. Answers for `localhost` and `slicc.internal` are authoritative (`aa`), and a name there that nothing knows is `NXDOMAIN`. Other types for a name the kernel knows (`MX` for `host.slicc.internal`) get an empty answer.
- **Everything else** (other names, other types, other classes, address literals, an `AAAA` the uplink has no answer to, a name whose uplink lookup failed) goes to a forwarder, and there is none by default: those questions are `SERVFAIL`, and nothing leaves the kernel. An [uplink](#uplink) whose `nameserver` names its DNS server (an address it routes, such as Tailscale's `100.100.100.100`) gets them over its own TCP, length-framed as RFC 1035 has it, so they go where that uplink and its exit send them. Without one, `network: { doh }` names a DNS over HTTPS endpoint (RFC 8484, `POST`, id 0) that the [transport](#network) is asked; `doh: false` or none is no forwarder. A forwarder's answer must repeat the question (name, type and class) or it is `SERVFAIL`, as are a failed connection, an HTTP error or a non-`application/dns-message` answer, and no answer within 5 s. The answer goes back as the forwarder gave it, with the query's id. Over UDP, an answer longer than 512 bytes (or the query's EDNS size, at most 4096) comes back truncated (`tc`, no records), so the client asks again over TCP.
- **What a forwarder sees:** the question's bytes as the program wrote them (id set to 0), EDNS options included, so a program can carry data to the forwarder in DNS names, as anywhere DNS is resolved. That gives no process more than it has: every process may already use the proxy and the transport, and connect to what the uplink routes; the kernel has no per-user network policy that the stub would bypass.
- **`/etc/resolv.conf`:** at boot, the kernel writes `nameserver 127.0.0.53` and `options edns0` there when there is no such file (a symlink counts as one), owned by root with mode `0644`. A file root wrote stays, and so does none at all when `/etc/resolv.conf` is a [protected path](#protected-paths). One that root removes comes back at the next boot. It has no `search` line, because musl's `getaddrinfo` (WASIX programs) would then try every short name in that domain first.

### Node name

The kernel has one node name, `slicc` unless `createKernel({ hostname })` (or `createNodeKernel`) names another; it must be a valid host name, and not `localhost`, a `*.localhost` name, `host.slicc.internal`, `wasmer.sh` or an IPv4 address, or `createKernel` rejects. `no_proxy` lists it (and `emscripten`), so HTTP clients reach the kernel's own servers by it directly.
- **Where programs see it:** through `uname`, `gethostname` and `/etc/hostname`, never only from the environment ([#1272](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1272)). `HOSTNAME` is in the kernel's base and login environments, so `kernel.run`, terminals and login shells have it, and a program keeps whatever `HOSTNAME` its caller passes (`env -i env` shows none). One exception: a WASI or WASIX program always gets `HOSTNAME` set to the node name, because wasix-sysroot's `gethostname` reads it from the environment. So its `os.environ` shows it after `env -i`, until the sysroot asks the kernel instead. Every program can read it from `/etc/hostname`: the node name and a newline, a `0644` root-owned file that a real `/etc/hostname` in the root replaces ([#1297](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1297)). Emscripten programs also have the synthetic `/etc/hosts` and `/etc/passwd`, which only they have (WASI programs resolve names through the kernel instead), and `Module.sliccKernel.hostname`.
- **What `uname` says:** for an Emscripten program built with homescoop's `__syscall_uname` import (wasm-coreutils 9.12.0-7 on), the kernel answers `uname(2)`: sysname `Linux`, nodename the node name, release `6.0.0-slicc` (as `/proc/sys/kernel/osrelease`), version `#1 SMP PREEMPT_DYNAMIC` (as `/proc/version`), machine `wasm32` and domainname `(none)`, so `uname -a` and musl's `gethostname()` see them. `uname -o` is coreutils' compiled-in `Emscripten`. Older Emscripten builds still report their C library's `Emscripten 4.0.23` and `emscripten`, and WASIX programs `wasmer.sh`; the kernel has nothing to answer there.
- **Resolving it:** `/etc/hosts` maps the node name and both of those names to `127.0.1.1` (Debian's convention), and name resolution answers them, and `localhost.localdomain`, itself, as `127.0.1.1` and `::1`. So a program that looks up its own host name, as git does for every commit, never asks the [uplink](#uplink), and `127.0.1.1` is the kernel's loopback.
- **The trade-off:** a raw connection to the real `wasmer.sh` resolves to loopback too. HTTP(S) to it still goes through the realm proxy and the transport, which resolve names themselves; `wasmer.sh` is deliberately not in `no_proxy`.
- **A real file wins:** a root that has its own `/etc/hostname` (or `/etc/hosts`, `/etc/passwd`) keeps it, and programs read that file rather than the synthetic one. The kernel's name still drives `uname`, `gethostname` and name resolution. A program that opens one of these synthetic files for writing (as root, since `/etc` is root's) gets a real one first: a root-owned `0644` copy of the defaults. So `echo '10.0.0.9 db' >> /etc/hosts` keeps `localhost` and the other default entries, `>` replaces them as on Linux, and `rm` returns to the synthetic file. The copy is `0644` whatever the umask. Programs that read `/etc/hosts` themselves (WASIX/musl ones, such as python's `socket.gethostbyname`) resolve an added name. So does the kernel's own resolver, which Emscripten programs' `getaddrinfo`, WASIX `resolve` and `ctx.net` use ([#898](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/898)). It reads a real `/etc/hosts` as glibc does (`#` comments, aliases, IPv4 and IPv6, first entries first), re-reads it when its inode, size or mtime change, and answers a name the file lists before its built-in names. HTTP that goes through the transport by URL is resolved there, so `curl http://db/` doesn't use the file. A root-written `/etc/hosts` entry for `localhost` applies to `ctx.net` and `getaddrinfo`, as with glibc. The WASI imports' `net.connect` maps the literal name `localhost` to `127.0.0.1`.

### Uplink

`createKernel({ network: { uplink } })` (or `createNodeKernel`) gives the kernel a page-owned way into another network, such as a tailnet. It is the whole kernel's: while the page passes an uplink, every process gets its names. The uplink is an object with:

- `traits`: `{ tcp: true, udp, ipv6 }`. Without `ipv6: true`, the kernel asks only for, and keeps only, IPv4 addresses. With `udp: true` and a `udp` function, routed datagrams go through it.
- `routes`: the first route table, `{ prefixes, exit }`, which `kernel.setRoutes` replaces when it changes. The kernel matches it itself, so a destination outside it costs no round trip.
- `nameserver` (optional): an address the uplink routes where a DNS server answers on TCP port 53 (Tailscale: `100.100.100.100`). The kernel's [DNS](#dns) stub forwards the questions it can't answer there.
- `resolve(name, family, signal)`: resolves with the name's addresses, as a list or as `{ addresses, ttl }` (seconds). `[]` means the name is not the uplink's. `family` is `4`, `6` or `0` (either), and `signal` aborts when the kernel stops waiting.
- `dial({ network: 'tcp', host, port, signal })`: opens a TCP connection and resolves with `{ localAddr, remoteAddr, read(), write(bytes), closeWrite(), close() }`.
  - `read()` resolves with the next bytes, or `null` at the end.
  - `write` resolves with how many bytes it took.
  - `closeWrite()` is `shutdown(SHUT_WR)`.
  - A rejection's `code` (`ECONNREFUSED`, `ENETUNREACH`, `EHOSTUNREACH`, `ETIMEDOUT`, `ECONNRESET`) is what the program sees, and any other is `EHOSTUNREACH`.
  - `signal` aborts when the program gives up.

**Name resolution** (WASIX `sock_addr_resolve`; `Module.sliccKernel.net.resolve(name, family)` for Emscripten programs):
- `/etc/hosts` names are answered by the kernel itself: `localhost`, every `*.localhost`, `host.slicc.internal`, and literal addresses, IPv6 included.
- Any other name goes to the uplink. With no uplink or no answer, the name is not found (`EAI_NONAME`).
- Answers are cached for their TTL, at most 60 s, so a change of exit node takes effect quickly.
- **DNS cannot bridge into the kernel:** an answer that is loopback (`127.0.0.0/8`, `::1`), unspecified (`0.0.0.0`, `::`), the host (`10.0.2.2`), link-local, multicast or otherwise reserved, including the `::ffff:` forms, is dropped and logged with `console.warn`. A name cannot lead a program back to the kernel's loopback.
- An uplink that does not answer within 10 s resolves nothing.

**Connections.** A `connect()` decides in a fixed order:
1. Loopback (`127.0.0.0/8`, `::1`, `0.0.0.0`, `localhost`) is always the kernel's own, including the [CDP facade](#browser-automation-cdp) on `9222`.
2. `host.slicc.internal` (`10.0.2.2`) and reserved addresses are `ENETUNREACH`.
3. An address the route table matches goes through `dial`.
4. Anything else is `ENETUNREACH`.

- `udp({ family, signal })` (optional): opens a datagram socket for `family` `4` or `6` and resolves with `{ localAddr, send(bytes, host, port), messages, close() }`.
  - The kernel opens one per program socket and family on its first routed send, and closes it with the socket.
  - `send` resolves once the datagram is on its way. A rejection's `code` (`ENETUNREACH`, `EHOSTUNREACH`, `EACCES`, `EMSGSIZE`) is what the program sees, and any other is `EHOSTUNREACH`.
  - `messages` yields `{ bytes, host, port }` for each datagram that arrives, and may yield `{ error, host, port }` when a destination turned out unreachable (`error` is `EACCES`, `ENETUNREACH` or `EHOSTUNREACH`). Its end makes the next send open a new one.
  - `host` is always an address: the kernel resolves names before it sends.

`bind()` still takes only loopback and `0.0.0.0`, so nothing listens on an uplink address.

An uplink connection is an ordinary socket fd: it survives `dup`, `fork` and `exec`, and works with `select`, `poll` and WASIX epoll.
- **Blocking `connect`** waits for the dial, at most 30 s (then `ETIMEDOUT`, even if the uplink ignores `signal`; a connection it opens later is closed). A signal interrupts it with `EINTR` while the dial goes on.
- **Non-blocking `connect`** answers `EINPROGRESS`. The socket turns writable when the dial is done, and `SO_ERROR` says how it went.
- **Closing the socket** during the dial gives `ECONNABORTED`.
- **A connection that fails** (its `read()` rejects) closes; the program's next read fails with `ECONNRESET`, and its writes with `EPIPE`. `read()` resolving `null` is an orderly end of stream.
- **`getsockname` and `getpeername`** answer the uplink's `localAddr` and `remoteAddr`.
- **Flow control:** the kernel reads from the connection only while the socket's receive buffer (64 KiB) has room, and writes as the program does, so a slow side slows the other.

In the browser, the page serves the uplink to the kernel worker over the same port as the transport, with buffers copied and transferred. `terminate()` closes every connection and cancels whatever is pending, and the page refuses to dial loopback, `10.0.2.2` or reserved addresses itself as well.

`@ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/testing` is test support, kept out of the main entry so it never reaches a page bundle. It runs with the Node kernel and needs no wasm of its own. `fakeUplink({ names, routes, ipv6, peers, address, nameserver })` is an uplink to pass as `network.uplink`:
- **Names:** `resolve` answers from `names` (a list or `{ addresses, ttl }` per name) and records each question in `asked`.
- **Routes:** `routes` is the first table; `kernel.setRoutes` changes it as for a real uplink.
- **Dials:** each dial is recorded in `dialled`. `localAddr` is `address` (default `100.100.100.100`) with a fresh port. `peers`, keyed `'host:port'` (or `'[v6]:port'`), decides how a dial ends:
  - `{ error: 'ETIMEDOUT' }` (or any code) rejects with that code;
  - `{ hang: true }` never answers, until the kernel aborts the dial;
  - anything not listed is refused with `ECONNREFUSED`;
  - a function is called with the server's side of the connection.
- **The server's side**, `{ read(), write(bytes), end(), reset(code?), buffered, drained(), closed }`:
  - `read()` resolves with what the program wrote, and `null` after its `shutdown(SHUT_WR)` or `close()`. The program's write completes only once the server reads it, so a server that doesn't read holds the program back.
  - `write` queues bytes for the program, and `buffered` counts those the kernel hasn't taken yet; `drained()` resolves when it has taken them all.
  - `end()` is the server's end of stream.
  - `reset(code)` (default `ECONNRESET`) breaks the connection both ways: writes the peer has not read fail, the program's next read fails with `ECONNRESET`, and its writes with `EPIPE`.
  - `closed` resolves when the kernel closes the connection.

`fakeCronClock(start?, utc?)` is a clock for the kernel's cron to run on in tests, so a test moves time instead of waiting for it. It starts at `start` (default 2026-01-01T00:00Z) and reads schedules in UTC unless `utc` is `false`. `now()` is the fake time. `sleep(until, signal)` resolves when `advance(ms)` moves time to `until` or past it, or when `signal` aborts. `set(ms)` sets the wall clock, forward or back, and wakes every sleeper, as a scheduler's next periodic wake would after a real clock change. `sleepers` counts the sleeps still waiting. `flush()` resolves once every sleeper that a time move woke has gone back to sleep for a later time, which is the point where a scheduler has done what was due. It never resolves while nothing sleeps.

`setTestSyscallHook(hook)` is **test-only**: while set, every process kernel call (the `WasmSyscall` requests: process, fd, socket, signal and job calls; not the path-only file system requests such as `read`, `write` or `rename`) of a process in this Node process first awaits `hook({ pid, op })`. `pid` is the kernel's own pid of the process, which for an exec'd image is not the pid the program sees (`getpid()`, `ps` show the pid it took over). A test can then act at an exact point (write a ^C when a shell forks) or hold a call (delay a child's `proc-setpgid`). `setTestSyscallHook(undefined)` clears it. Unset, which is the default, it does nothing.

### Browser automation (CDP)

Programs drive a browser with the Chrome DevTools Protocol over a WebSocket, whichever host actually has the browser: slicc-extension's `chrome.debugger`, slicc-node's or slicc-swift's CDP proxy, or a test harness. The kernel relays; it does not launch a browser.

- **Programs** find the endpoint in `SLICC_CDP_URL`, `ws://127.0.0.1:9222/devtools/browser/<id>`, where `<id>` is random for each kernel boot. `http://127.0.0.1:9222/json/version` answers `{ "Browser", "Protocol-Version", "webSocketDebuggerUrl" }` with the same URL (carrying the request's query), and `/json/list` (or `/json`) lists the host's targets, without per-target sockets. Every other path is `404`. The listener starts on the first connection, on the kernel's loopback; a program that binds `9222` first keeps it.
- **Sessions** are Chrome's flattened ones: `Target.attachToTarget` with `flatten: true`, then commands with a top-level `sessionId`. Messages pass unchanged, as text frames up to 256 MiB (a larger one closes the socket with `1009`). Each program WebSocket gets a host connection of its own: closing the socket (or exiting) closes it, which detaches its sessions, and a host that closes is passed on as a close frame (`1011` with its reason). The kernel then waits up to a second for the program's close frame before it hangs up, as RFC 6455 has it, so a program that answers the close can still report the code. `terminate()` closes them all.
- **Runtime**: a program adds `runtime=<name>` to the query of the URL it opens, and the kernel gives it to the host; the kernel does not interpret it.
- **No host**: `/json/*` and the WebSocket handshake answer `503` with `slicc-kernel: no CDP host is attached (createKernel({ cdp }) or client.serveCdp)`, and a host that refuses (an unknown runtime, say) gives `502` with its message.
- **Kernel-only**: port `9222` is reserved (a program's `bind()` to it fails with `EADDRINUSE`, so none can squat it) and takes connections only from programs in the kernel. `kernel.dial`, a client's `dial` and `loopbackFetch` (and so `9222.kernel.localhost` and slicc-node's tunnel) are refused with `ECONNREFUSED`, since the facade drives the user's real browser.

An embedder offers a browser with the `cdp` option of `createKernel` or `createNodeKernel`, or from an attached client with `client.serveCdp(hook, { runtime })`:

```ts
interface CdpConnection {
  send(message: string): void;
  onmessage: ((message: string) => void) | null;
  onclose: ((reason?: string) => void) | null;
  close(): void;
}
type CdpHook = (request: { runtime?: string }) => Promise<CdpConnection>;
```

A connection is one browser-level CDP session; a host with only per-tab debugging emulates the `Target` domain behind it. A connection asking for a `runtime` goes to the newest client that offered that name; any other goes to the `cdp` option, or else to the newest client offer. In the browser the hook runs on the page, with a `MessagePort` per connection to the kernel worker.

Process workers can also use the transport directly, without a socket: the `net-request`, `net-read` and `net-close` syscalls (`Module.sliccKernel.http` in a process) open a request and read its body in pieces. `net-open` starts a request and answers its handle at once, and `net-head` waits for its response head, so `net-close` can abort a request whose head has not come yet. Requests are per process and closed when it exits, and a request body has no size limit of its own (the transport's `maxRequestBody` applies to the proxy). `net-open` with `stream: true` instead of `body` (only on a transport with `streamingBodies`, else `ENOTSUP`) takes the body in pieces: `net-write { handle, bytes }` returns once the transport has taken the piece (`EPIPE` once the request is closed or failed), and `net-end { handle }` ends it. JS programs' `fetch` uses it for stream bodies. `net-traits` answers the transport's `traits` with `crossOrigin`: `'cors'` when it is bound by CORS like `fetchTransport()`, `'any'` (the default for a transport that does not say) when it is not; without a transport it fails with `ENETUNREACH`.

### Page facades

A facade is a loopback port that only programs in the kernel can reach, whose connections the embedder serves, much like the CDP facade, but as a plain duplex byte stream with no protocol of the kernel's. slicc's link layer is the first user.

```ts
createKernel({ facades: { link: { port: 7400, hook } } });

type FacadeHook = (stamp: ConnectorStamp, info: { name: string }) => FacadeConnection | Promise<FacadeConnection>;

interface FacadeConnection {
  send(bytes: Uint8Array): void;
  onmessage: ((bytes: Uint8Array) => void) | null;
  onclose: ((reason?: string) => void) | null;
  close(reason?: string): void;
}

interface ConnectorStamp { pid: number; ppid: number; uid: number; gid: number; user?: string; groups: number[]; users: 'off' | 'report' | 'enforce'; tag?: string }
```

- **Declared at boot:** `facades` names each facade (`[a-z][a-z0-9-]*`) and its port, from 1 to 65535. A port may not be 9222 (the CDP facade), 3128 (the realm proxy) or another facade's; anything else throws a `TypeError`. `createNodeKernel` takes `facades` too. The hook may be left out and supplied later by an attached client's `client.serveFacade(name, hook)`, which works only for a declared name.
- **Programs** find each facade in their environment as `SLICC_<NAME>_URL`, upper-cased with `-` as `_`, e.g. `SLICC_LINK_URL=tcp://127.0.0.1:7400`, and connect to it with an ordinary TCP `connect()`: a JS program's `ctx.net.connect`, an Emscripten program's socket, or a WASIX program's `sock_connect`. Every ABI ends in the same kernel call.
- **The stamp** is the connecting process's identity, taken by the kernel at `connect()` from its credentials, as Linux's `SO_PEERCRED` is: its pid and parent pid as `ps` shows them, effective uid and gid, user name, groups, the `users` mode and the process's tag (see `kernel.run`), the same fields as a `/dev/sealed` request's `caller`. A program cannot forge it. The hook gets it and `{ name }`, so one function can serve several facades, and decides: it resolves with a connection, whose bytes then pass both ways until either side closes, or it rejects, and the program's connection is closed. A connection while no hook is attached is closed at once, the same as a refusal. Bytes from the hook wait in the kernel until the program reads them, up to 16 MiB per connection: past that, the connection is closed and the hook's `close(reason)` gets `slicc-kernel: more than 16777216 bytes are waiting for the program to read`. A program that connects to anything else pays nothing for this: the stamp is built only for a facade's port.
- **Handing a connection on hands over the identity:** a facade connection that a process passes to another (a fork, an exec, an inherited descriptor) still speaks as the process that connected, as on Linux. So a privileged process should not pass one to a less trusted one.
- **Reserved:** a facade's port is reserved from the start. A program's `bind()` to it fails with `EADDRINUSE`, so no program can squat it and impersonate the page, and ephemeral ports skip it. Port 9222 is reserved the same way. Like 9222, a facade's port is kernel-only: the page's `kernel.dial`, a client's `dial` and `loopbackFetch` (and so `<port>.kernel.localhost`) are refused with `ECONNREFUSED`.

### Local proxy protocol

The page and the proxy speak raw mode of SLICC's `/api/fetch-proxy`. Every request is one `POST {url}/api/fetch-proxy` with `credentials: 'omit'`:

- `X-Bridge-Token: <key>`, the per-process proxy key. It travels only in this header, never in a URL.
- `X-Slicc-Raw-Request: <json>`, the head to send upstream: `{ "url", "method", "headers": [[name, value], …] }`, with the headers in order and repeats kept apart. Characters past U+007E are `\u`-escaped so the value is a valid header.
- The request body, if any, as the hop's body.

The proxy answers `200` with `Content-Type: application/vnd.slicc.raw-fetch`: a big-endian `u32` length, that many bytes of UTF-8 JSON `{ "status", "statusText", "headers": [[name, value], …], "url" }`, then the upstream body until the end of the hop. Any other answer is a refusal with a JSON `{ "error" }` body and `X-Proxy-Error: 1`; programs see its status (`400` malformed head, `403` wrong origin or key, `413` body too large, `502` upstream unreachable). A probe is a `POST` with the key and `X-Slicc-Raw-Probe: 1` instead of a head; it is answered with JSON `{ "rawFetch": 1, "requestBodyStreaming", "maxRequestBodyBytes" }` and fetches nothing. A proxy that tunnels to the kernel for `<port>.kernel.localhost` adds `"kernelTunnel"` and `"kernelPort"`, and one that serves a `/cdp` endpoint adds `"cdp"` (a version, e.g. `1`). `checkLocalProxy` and `probeLocalProxy` pass these on when they are numbers. The server side (origin allowlist, CORS, the Private Network Access preflight) is specified in [slicc-node's README](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-node#protocol).

## Filesystem

`/` is the OPFS root. Each process mounts the top-level directories that exist when it starts (plus `/usr` and `/bin`), so files in them are shared by all processes and visible through the OPFS API as soon as the process that wrote them has closed them; `run` resolves only after that. `createKernel` creates `/tmp` and `/home` in OPFS, so they are shared too. Files directly in `/`, and directories created there after a process started, are OPFS as well, the same for Emscripten and WASI programs; only `/dev` and `/proc` live in each process's memory. A program's `mkdir` (WASI `path_create_directory`, Emscripten's `mkdir`) is exclusive, as on Linux: it fails with `EEXIST` when the name exists, and of several processes or threads making one name at once exactly one succeeds, so a directory works as a lock. Under a missing directory it fails with `ENOENT`, as on Linux (`mkdir -p` and `os.makedirs` make the parents in the program); a JS program's `ctx.fs.mkdir` makes parents itself. The kernel makes the system `PNPM_HOME` (`/usr/local/share/pnpm`, or the embedder's) when it starts, since pnpm makes only its `bin` directory there. A full origin storage (`QuotaExceededError`, which also comes early in a browser's private or off-the-record contexts, whatever `navigator.storage.estimate()` says) is `ENOSPC`, never retried. A read that meets `NotFoundError` while a writer outside the kernel swaps the file in with `createWritable` (and a `stat`, `lstat` or `utimes` that meets it) looks the name up again, up to three times, before it fails ([#1075](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1075)), so a program whose package is overwritten in place keeps starting. An OPFS or IndexedDB failure that has no POSIX counterpart is `EIO`; its message names the DOM error and the operation (`lookup`, `read`, `write`, `commit` or `sidecar`), and the kernel worker logs the first of each kind to its console. File contents are buffered per open file and written back on close, `fsync` and exit, so a refused write (`ENOSPC`, `EIO`) surfaces at that close or `fsync`, and a program that ignores close errors doesn't see it (`echo x > f` can exit 0 with `f` empty, as with any buffered write-back). A write-back error a close has already reported to a WASI program isn't raised again when it exits, so the program keeps its own exit status. Metadata operations (`mkdir`, `rename`, `rm`, …) go straight to OPFS. The kernel worker is the only writer.

**Renaming a directory on the OPFS root** ([#1122](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1122)): Chrome's OPFS can move a file but not a directory. So the kernel renames a directory by creating the new tree and moving each file into it (copying a file only if it can't move, e.g. while it's open elsewhere), then removing the old tree. Within the kernel the rename is atomic to readers: a lookup or listing under the old or the new name, or of either parent, waits until the whole tree has moved. So a stage-then-rename updater (pnpm's, an atomic config write) is never seen half there. Another tab sharing the root sees it all at once too (see below). A root whose directories can move (the Node memory root) renames them in one step. **If the page reloads or crashes mid-rename**, some files are already in the new tree and the rest are still in the old one. So before the first move, the kernel writes the rename's intent (`{ from, to }`) to its metadata store, and clears it after the last. At the next boot, when no other tab shares the root (the same rule as for the boot's other repair of stale metadata), the kernel finishes every recorded rename: it moves what's left into the new tree, removes the old name, and clears the record. The moved directory and its files keep their owner, mode and inode, as after a rename that wasn't stopped. While another tab shares the root, a kernel finishes only renames whose kernel is gone ([#1141](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1141)): the renaming kernel holds a Web Lock (`slicc-rename:<id>`, named in the record) for the whole rename, and the browser releases it when that tab closes or its worker ends. A kernel that boots beside a peer, and every kernel that sees a peer go, finishes each recorded rename whose lock is free, taking that lock while it does, so two survivors never replay the same rename and a live kernel's rename is never touched. A record without a lock (written by an older kernel, or where Web Locks are missing) waits for a boot that's alone, as before. While another tab finishes a rename, this kernel's readers wait as for its own, and so do the other tabs'. A kernel whose wait for another tab's rename ends with the lock free and the record still there finishes the rename itself, under that lock, and a boot that's alone takes a record's lock too, so a third tab that boots meanwhile waits for it.

**A rename by another process** ([#1257](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1257)): as on Linux, where a descriptor names the file and not its path, a program's open file follows a rename of that file, or of a directory above it, by another process.
- **The journal:** the kernel keeps the last 4096 renames it applied, each from and to a path, and counts them in the change buffer it shares with its processes. A rename in another tab on the same root reaches it through the sidecar's move notice.
- **Catching up:** before a WASI or Emscripten program uses an open file's path (a read, a write, a seek, an `fsync`, a close, an open), it reads that count once. If another process renamed something since, and the program holds files open, it asks the kernel once for the renames of the paths it holds and moves its open files to their new names. The kernel answers only about the paths the program sent, and, under `users: 'enforce'`, drops every rename whose old or new name the program couldn't look up (search permission on each directory above it), so no process learns names in a directory it can't search. A program's own rename moves the count without asking, when nothing else came in between.
- **A late rename:** a write that finds its file's name gone asks for renames once more and tries again at the new name before it fails.
- **Watches:** a typed watch (`fs.watch`) follows a rename in another tab of its root or a directory above it, as it does for a rename in this kernel ([#1356](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1356)), so this tab's later changes there arrive.
- **Limits:** a program that falls more than 4096 renames behind, or holds a file by a name that goes through a symlink to the renamed path, keeps the old name, and its next write fails with `ENOENT` as before. A file removed while open isn't moved by a later rename of its old name.

**Directory renames across tabs** ([#1140](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1140)): a tab sharing the root sees a directory rename in another tab all at once, as the renaming kernel's own readers do.
- **The notice:** before the first file moves, the renaming kernel tells the other kernels on the root, and waits until each has answered. A kernel answers once the calls it has in flight under either name, or on either parent, are done (a file rename into or out of the tree included, a symlink by the link's own path, calls that began before the other kernel joined too, and calls through a symlink into the tree, and an open write session there counts until it's closed). From then on, its calls under either name, and its listings of either parent, wait until the rename's Web Lock is released. So a listing in the other tab shows the old tree or the new one, never a mix, and every write that returned success there is in the new tree.
- **A missed notice:** a kernel also checks the held Web Locks while it shares the root, and reads the recorded renames when it sees a rename lock it doesn't know. Only a call starts a check: it runs in the background once the last answer is 50 ms old, and a call waits for it only when that answer is 100 ms old. An idle tab makes no checks. A rename lock whose record isn't written yet holds the call until the record appears or the lock is released. A call through a symlink whose target is in the renaming tree waits as one under the tree does. So a kernel that missed the notice, or joined the root in the middle of a rename, trusts "no rename in flight" for at most 100 ms.
- **Overlapping renames:** of two kernels renaming overlapping trees at once (`/t` and `/t/a`), one goes first and the other retries against what is there by then, so it may fail with `ENOENT`. Renames of unrelated trees, siblings like `/t/a` and `/t/ab` included, don't wait for each other.
- **A stuck peer:** a peer whose tab is alive (its tab's Web Lock is held) is waited for up to 10 s. If it hasn't answered by then, the rename fails with `EBUSY` and nothing has moved. The kernel logs one line naming the peer and the tree, and `/proc/sys/kernel/slicc_rename_ack_busy` counts it. A peer that's gone isn't waited for.
- **Counters:** `/proc/sys/kernel/slicc_rename_acks` reads five numbers: the renames that asked, the median and the longest wait in ms, the times this kernel waited for another kernel's rename, and the lock checks it made.
- **A tab that hasn't finished joining:** a kernel that's alone checks the root's held tab locks once before it renames a directory. If another tab holds one but hasn't answered yet (it answers once its own exclusive creates are done), the rename waits for it to join, up to 10 s, and then fails with `EBUSY` with nothing moved.
- **Alone on the root,** nothing else runs: no notice, no ack, and a call costs one counter.
- **Limits:**
  - A kernel without `/proc/sys/kernel/slicc_rename_acks` (older than #1140) doesn't answer and isn't waited for ([#1309](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1309)). It can still see a tree mid-move, and a write it has in flight when the rename starts can land in the old copy and be lost.
  - An open descriptor follows a rename by another process, of its file or of a directory above it ([#1257](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1257), see "A rename by another process" above), also when the rename was in another tab.
  - A peer's call that is already in flight and then reaches into the renaming tree through a call of its own (a rename removing the name it replaces) waits for the rename, while the rename waits for that peer's answer. After the 10 s cap the rename fails with `EBUSY`, nothing has moved, and the peer's call goes on.

A lookup follows at most 40 symlinks, as Linux does: the 41st is `ELOOP`, for WASI programs, JS programs and the kernel API alike, with or without `..` in a link's target. Emscripten programs resolve paths in their glue, which stops one link earlier, at 40.

Directories are renamed with `FileSystemHandle.move()` where available, else by copy and delete.

**FIFOs** (`mkfifo`, #896):
- **Creating one:** an Emscripten program's `mkfifo`/`mknod(S_IFIFO)` makes a FIFO node on the OPFS root (in `/tmp`, `/home` and the rest), owned by the caller, with mode `mode & ~umask`. `stat`, `ls -l` (`p`) and `test -p` show it as a FIFO, in Emscripten, WASI and JS programs. A WASIX program makes one through `slicc_fs.path_mkfifo` (see [File modes and the umask](#file-modes-and-the-umask); its libc uses it from wasix-sysroot -33), and `stat`s, `open`s, reads and writes one like any program. An existing name is `EEXIST`. tmpfs mounts (`/dev/shm`, a tmpfs `/tmp`) have FIFO nodes too, through the driver's `fifos` capability ([#907](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/907)). hostfs, `fsa` and media mounts have none, so `mkfifo` there is `EPERM`, and `EROFS` on a readonly path.
- **Opening one** works as on Linux (fifo(7)). A read-open waits until a writer opens, and a write-open until a reader does. `O_RDWR` doesn't wait. With `O_NONBLOCK` a read-open returns at once, and a write-open with no reader is `ENXIO`. A waiting open is interrupted by a caught signal (`EINTR`), restarts under `SA_RESTART`, and isn't ended by a stop.
- **The data path** is the kernel's pipe: the same capacity, `EOF` once the last writer closes, and `SIGPIPE`/`EPIPE` once the last reader closes. The data is dropped when no end is open.
- **The node's identity, not its name, picks the FIFO:** an open FIFO keeps working after an `unlink` or a `rename`, and a new `mkfifo` at the same name is another FIFO. `/proc/<pid>/fd/N` names the FIFO's path, also in children that inherit the end and in a WASIX program's `readlink("/proc/self/fd/N")` of an end it opened itself ([#909](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/909)).
- **One kernel per storage root:** the table of open FIFOs is per kernel, so two tabs (two kernels on one origin) opening the same FIFO don't connect. Record locks and `flock` do reach across tabs (see below).
- GNU make 4.4's `-jN` uses a FIFO jobserver this way (`--jobserver-auth=fifo:PATH`), so sub-makes join it by path.
- **Below an open** ([#908](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/908)): a FIFO or socket node is never read or written as a plain file. A JS program's `ctx.fs.readFile`/`writeFile` and the kernel API's `readFile`/`writeFile` (and the positioned reads, writes and truncates under them) fail with `ENXIO` on either, because they read or write a whole file rather than open one. Emscripten and WASI programs' opens of a FIFO go through the kernel's FIFO open as above, and a WASI program's open of a socket node is `ENXIO`, as on Linux.

`mmap` of a regular file works on every file system, for Emscripten programs built with `mmap` (whose glue exports `emscripten_builtin_memalign`) as well as for WASIX ones: the kernel reads the mapped range into fresh memory of the process, as Emscripten's MEMFS does. A `MAP_PRIVATE` mapping may be written, and its changes stay in the process. A writable `MAP_SHARED` mapping of such a file is `ENODEV`; a file in the process's own in-memory file system (MEMFS, as under `/dev`) keeps Emscripten's own mapping, which writes back. A read-only `MAP_SHARED` mapping is a copy taken at `mmap`, so later changes to the file do not show in it.

### Mounts

`kernel.mount({ type, source, target, options })` mounts a file system on an existing directory. Every process sees it, Emscripten and WASI alike, and so does every attached client. `kernel.umount(target)` unmounts it, and fails with `EBUSY` while a process has a file open under it. `kernel.mounts()` lists the table, as does `/proc/mounts`. The Node entry and attached clients have the same three calls (client protocol 1.2).

`/dev/shm` is a tmpfs the kernel mounts at boot (source `shm`), mode `1777` and owned by root, so with `users` the sticky bit keeps one user from removing or renaming another's file there (`EPERM`). It's in `/proc/mounts`, but not in `kernel.mounts()`, which lists the mounts a caller made. Every process sees the same files, Emscripten (whose glue otherwise keeps a private `/dev/shm` in memory), WASI and JS programs alike, and they last until the kernel terminates, which is what musl's `shm_open` and `sem_open` need.

- **Built in:** `tmpfs`, which lives in memory until unmounted, in 1 MiB chunks; `fsa`, a folder the user picks (see [Removable media](#removable-media-fsa)); and `hostfs`, a folder the local proxy exports (see [Host folders](#host-folders-hostfs)).
- **Package drivers:** a package declares a type in `"slicc": { "filesystems": { "<type>": { "module": "<file>" } } }`, and the kernel starts that module in a worker of its own for each mount. The module is a single self-contained file: it can't import bare specifiers.
  - Its default export receives `{ fetch }`, the kernel's network transport. It returns `{ handlers, capabilities }`.
  - Its worker gets a JS program's lockdown before the module loads: its own `fetch`, `WebSocket`, storage and workers are `undefined`, and a worker where one cannot be removed does not start the driver.
  - The handlers are path-based: `getattr`, `readdir`, `open`/`read`/`write`/`release` on handles, `mkdir`, `rmdir`, `unlink`, `rename`, and optionally `symlink`, `readlink`, `setattr` (`mode`, `mtime`, `atime`, `size` to truncate, and `uid`/`gid`) and `statfs`. An attribute may carry `atime`; a driver that leaves it out reports its `mtime` as the access time, and ignores the `atime` of a `setattr` (hostfs and fsa do). tmpfs keeps both: `touch -a` and `utimensat` set its access time, and reading a file leaves it, as with `noatime`.
  - An optional `mount({ source, options })` handler validates the source.
  - Errors carry a POSIX `code`.
  - `@ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/driver` exports the types and `fsError`.
- **Capabilities:** `readonly`, `symlinks`, `links` (hard links: the driver answers `link(from, to)`, reports `nlink` in its attributes, and gives every attribute a stable `ino`, the same for all names of a file; a mount whose driver offers `links` but answers an attribute without `ino` loses them: `link` is `EPERM` there and `st_nlink` is 1), `chmod`, `owners`, `ranges`, `maxIo` (the largest read or write per message), `maxFile`, `listingStats`, `sessions`, `linkTimes`, and `attrTtl`/`entryTtl` (in ms, default 1000). `sessions` promises that a handle opened for writing keeps its writes to itself until it is released, so the kernel keeps one open per file being written instead of one per write (the `fsa` driver has it). `linkTimes` promises that `setattr` with `mtime` on a symlink sets the link's own time. Without it, setting a link's own times (`touch -h`, `utimensat` with `AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW`) fails with `EOPNOTSUPP` and never reaches the target. tmpfs has it; hostfs does not. Setting times through a link follows it in the kernel, so `setattr` gets the target. `ranges` (driver protocol 1.1) promises that a write lands in the file at once, with no copy of the whole file on `open` or `release`, and that `setattr` takes `size`. `owners` (driver protocol 1.2) promises that the driver stores owners: its attributes carry `uid` and `gid`, `open` with `create`, `mkdir` and `symlink` may carry the new path's `uid`, `gid` and `mode` (the `owner` of the open flags, the second or third handler argument), a new path without them takes its directory's owner, and `setattr` takes `uid` and `gid`. tmpfs has it. `fifos` (driver protocol 1.3) promises that the driver answers `mkfifo(path, owner)` with a FIFO node: its attributes say `kind: 'fifo'`, `readdir` lists it so, and an `open` of it is `ENXIO`, since the kernel, not the driver, carries a FIFO's data ([#907](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/907)). tmpfs has it, so `mkfifo` works on `/dev/shm` and on a tmpfs `/tmp`; on hostfs, `fsa` and media mounts it's still `EPERM`.
- **Caching:** the kernel caches attributes and listings for those TTLs, and a driver can push `invalidate` for changes made outside.
- **File contents:** on a mount with `ranges`, as `tmpfs` is, and on the root file system when it is in OPFS (in a browser with sync access handles) or an in-memory root, a program reads and writes a file in 64 KiB pages: reads come from the driver 1 MiB at a time, and changed pages go back in 1 MiB runs once 8 MiB have changed, at `fsync`, and when the file is closed (from the kernel's own descriptors also 250 ms after a write). A file of any size the mount can hold works, with memory to spare for about one more copy of it. In OPFS a write opens a sync access handle for just that write. An `fsa` mount (a picked folder) has no sync access handles, and Chrome copies the whole file into a swap file for every writable stream, so it writes through one stream per open file: the first write back opens it, later runs go into it at their offsets, and it is committed, one copy of the file, when the last process writing the file closes it (or exits), before another process reads what was written, and before a rename, unlink or unmount. Until then other processes see the new size but read the old bytes outside what was written. Elsewhere (`hostfs` with an older proxy, and a root outside OPFS), file contents are read whole when a program opens a file and written back when it closes it. Before a program's next call into the kernel (any wait, read of a pipe or terminal, sleep or spawn), its changed pages also go back, so other processes and the embedder see a write as soon as the writer does anything but compute: a daemon's pidfile, a log another process follows. On a whole-file mount that holds only for files of up to 64 KiB; a larger one goes back at close, fsync and exit, as before. Beyond that, both give close-to-open consistency, as NFS has. A write-back that can't reach the folder (a `hostfs` port or proxy gone, a medium ejected) keeps the pages and isn't dropped quietly ([#1062](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1062)): the next `fsync` or close of the file fails with the error (`ENOMEDIUM`, `ENODEV` for WASI), and so does every later write or truncate on it, each of which first retries the write-back, until one succeeds. When a mount's folder goes away, every file the kernel buffers for it is checked at its next write (one with unwritten pages retries them; one already written back asks the folder once, and fails while it's unreachable), so `for …; do echo …; done > /mnt/h/f` stops reporting success as soon as the folder is gone. A program's own whole-file buffer (`hostfs` without `ranges`, one process, no fork) still reports only at close or `fsync`, and a shell ignores a redirection's close error, as on Linux. A process that ends without closing its files keeps what it wrote: on exit, on a trap, and when a signal kills it. For a killed WASI program, the kernel lets its worker write its changed pages back before `waitpid` reports the death. The only exception is a worker that makes no call into the kernel for 2 seconds after the signal; it is stopped without that write-back.
- **Exclusive create across tabs** ([#912](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/912)): two kernels on one OPFS root (two tabs, or a tab and a worker page) agree on `O_CREAT|O_EXCL` and `set -C`: exactly one wins a name. While another kernel on the root is present, the create runs under a Web Lock for its path and checks the OPFS entry itself. A kernel alone on its root takes no lock. Each kernel holds an alive Web Lock, granted before it first looks for peers. A kernel that finds peers waits for their answer (at most 500 ms) before its first exclusive create, and a kernel answers a newcomer only once its unlocked creates are done. So of two kernels that boot at the same moment, the later one to look sees the other, and no unlocked create overlaps a locked one.
- **A name another process or tab just removed** ([#950](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/950)): sqlite3 deletes its `-journal` at every commit and the next writer creates it again, so two writers (two processes in one kernel, or two tabs on one OPFS root) keep removing and re-creating one name. Two things used to turn that into `unable to open database file`. The root removes a file's sidecar record a few milliseconds after the file itself (removals are batched), and an exclusive create read that pending record as the name being taken: a plain record whose file is gone now counts as gone, and the new file gets a record of its own. A symlink, which lives only in the sidecar, still takes its name. And an Emscripten program keeps the nodes it has looked up, so after another tab removed a name it still held the old node, and `open(O_CREAT)` opened the gone file instead of creating one. Now, when the kernel answers `ENOENT` for a cached node, for `open`, `stat`, `lstat`, `readlink`, `truncate`, `unlink` or a rename's source, or the program's own lookup answers `EEXIST` to `open(O_CREAT|O_EXCL)`, the node is dropped and the call runs once more, against what is there now. A node the program has open or has unwritten changes in is kept. A rename over a name another tab removed needs nothing: the kernel's rename replaces whatever is there.
- **One file, several processes** ([#665](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/665)): a process that keeps a file open sees what another process writes to it. Every change the kernel applies to a file (a write back, a truncate, an append, a rename or a removal) bumps a change ring the kernel shares with all its processes. Before each read, write or seek to the end, a process's open file checks the ring (one atomic load) and, if its file changed, drops its clean pages and reads the size again, keeping its own unwritten changes. A write by one process is visible to another one's next read once it has gone back to the kernel: at the latest at the writer's next `fsync`, close, exit or call into the kernel (see above), so sqlite, which `fsync`s every commit, sees another connection's commit at its next transaction. A write through a file the kernel holds (a JS program's `ctx.open`, a WASI program's file after a fork or spawn) is in the kernel already, so other processes see it at their next read, before it is written back. A file the kernel itself holds open (a JS program's file, a WASI program's file after a fork or spawn) is the one copy: reads, writes, truncates and appends by path from other processes go to it, and their writes go through to storage at once, so a commit is in storage (and in `kernel.readFile`) when the committing process returns from `fsync`. `O_APPEND` writes go to the kernel at once, and the kernel picks the offset, so appenders in several processes keep every line. A file system without ranged I/O (`hostfs` with an older proxy, a root outside OPFS) rereads a file another process changed, but writes it back whole, so two writers there still need locks. The same file under two names is one file too ([#669](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/669)): a write by path through a symlink reaches the file the kernel holds under its real path (resolved without following links in a `sudo` process's no-links view, so an untrusted link is still refused), a WASI program's open file also watches its real path (resolved once, the first time another file changes), and an open file with more than one hard link checks its size, version and mtime when anything changed, so a write through another name is seen. Limits for now: an alias on a mount that has no `realpath` is still only the name it was opened by; another tab's writes reach this tab's processes only as far as the root's change notifications (OPFS's `BroadcastChannel` watch) carry them. A by-path `stat` or `lstat` of a file the kernel holds open has its current size, writes not yet written back included ([#780](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/780)).
