- **`crontab`** is a command of the kernel itself, in `/usr/bin` (a package's `crontab` never wins over it), with Vixie's options: `crontab -l` prints the caller's table, `crontab -r` removes it, `crontab <file>` and `crontab -` (standard input) install one, and `crontab -e` copies the table to `/tmp`, runs `$VISUAL` (or `$EDITOR`) on the copy through `/bin/bash -c '<editor> "$1"'`, and installs it if it changed and the editor exited with 0. If the edit doesn't parse, the copy is kept and its path is printed. `-u <user>` works on another user's table and needs an effective uid of 0 (`sudo crontab -u cone -l`). An install is checked first: a table with a line that doesn't parse is refused with every error (`"-":<line>: <message>`), and the old one stays. The table becomes the user's `0600` file in the spool, written to a dotfile of a random name and renamed, so the service, which skips dotfiles, never reads half a table. The command only reads the file it is given and passes it on (`ctx.crontab`, see [JS programs](#js-programs)). The kernel decides whose table it is from the caller's real uid, so a user reaches only their own table, and `/etc/crontab` stays root's to edit.
- **Format:** Vixie's. Five fields (minute, hour, day of month, month, weekday) with lists, ranges, steps and the names `jan`–`dec` and `sun`–`sat` (7 is Sunday too), or one of `@yearly`/`@annually`, `@monthly`, `@weekly`, `@daily`/`@midnight`, `@hourly` and `@reboot`. As in cronie, when the day of month or the weekday starts with `*`, a day must match both fields; when both are restricted, it may match either. `/etc/crontab` has a user field after the schedule; a user's table doesn't. `NAME=value` lines set the environment for the jobs after them, `#` lines are comments, and `# name: <id>` on the line above a job gives it a stable id (letters, digits, `_`, `.`, `-`), which names its log. Without one, the id is a hash of the table, the user, the schedule and the command. `%` is not special. Times are the kernel's local time.
- **Environment:** only what a login of that user gets, and nothing from the page or the embedder's `env`. That is `HOME`, `USER`, `LOGNAME`, `PATH`, `PNPM_HOME`, `NPM_CONFIG_PREFIX`, the kernel's network settings (`http_proxy`, `SSL_CERT_FILE`, …), `SHELL`, `PWD`, the table's `NAME=value` lines, and `SLICC_CRON=1`, `SLICC_CRON_JOB=<id>`, `SLICC_CRON_DUE=<ISO time of the due run>` and `SLICC_CRON_MISSED=<runs folded into this one>`. An `agent send` from a job therefore knows it was scheduled.
- **Missed runs:** when the kernel wasn't running (a closed tab) or time jumped forward, every due run since the last one is folded into **one** run, with `SLICC_CRON_MISSED` saying how many more were due (counted up to 100 000) and `SLICC_CRON_DUE` naming the latest due time, even past the count's cap ([#843](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/843)). The next run is then computed from now. A clock set back more than two minutes runs nothing until the next due time after the new time.
- **At most once:** the service writes each due run to `/var/spool/cron/stamp` before it starts the job. A kernel that ends mid-job (a reload, a crash) never runs that due again; a stamp that can't be read counts every job as run just now.
- **`@reboot`** runs once per kernel boot, when that kernel first fires jobs.
- **Overlap:** a job's runs don't wait for each other, so a slow job can still be running when it is due again. At most 16 jobs of one user run at once; a run past that is skipped, with a line in the job's log and in `/var/log/cron/cron.err`.
- **Logs:** each run appends `--- <due time> exit=<status> missed=<n> ---` (or `signal=<name>`), then the job's standard output and error, to `/var/log/cron/<user>/<id>.log`. The directory is root's `0755`, so a user can't put a symlink or another file in place of a log; the file is the user's `0600` and keeps its last 256 KiB. The service writes a log only when its directory is a real directory of root and the log is a regular file of that user or root. Otherwise it leaves the path alone and says why in `cron.err`. There is no mail. Lines that don't parse and tables the service won't run go to `/var/log/cron/cron.err` (root's `0600`), once per version of the table, and so does each skipped run.
- **Tags:** a job runs with the tags of the process that installed its line through `crontab` (see `kernel.connect`), kept in the root-only `/var/spool/cron/tags` and never in the table's text. A line reinstalled unchanged keeps its tags, with the new installer's added; a new or edited line gets the installer's. With `users` on, a process with any tag can't change `/etc/crontab` or anything under `/var/spool/cron` itself, also through a symlink or a hard link (`EACCES`; renaming or removing a symlink that points there is refused too), so it schedules only through `crontab`; untagged processes keep direct access. With `users: 'off'` this isn't enforced, as every process is root there ([#1380](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1380)).
- **Trust:** with owners on (`users` not `'off'`), a user's table runs only if it is a regular file (not a symlink) owned by that user or root and writable by its owner only, and `/etc/crontab` only if it is root's and writable by root only. A table named for no user is skipped. The kernel creates `/var/spool/cron` (`0755`), `/var/spool/cron/crontabs` (`0700`) and `/var/log/cron` (`0755`), all root's.
- **Changes** to a table (here or in another tab) take effect without a restart. The service also re-reads the tables at least once a minute.
- **Several tabs:** only one kernel per root fires jobs. It holds the Web Lock `slicc-kernel-cron:<metadata database>` (the database that holds the root's owners and modes, so tabs on one root share it), and when its tab closes, another kernel takes over and catches up. In a background tab the browser throttles timers to once a minute, so a job may run up to a minute late there. A tab that was closed catches up on its next open, by the missed-run rule above.
- **On Node,** `createNodeKernel({ cron: { clock } })` runs the service on a clock of your own, such as `fakeCronClock()` from `@ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/testing`. Kernels on the same `root` in one process share one lock. That uses Node's `navigator.locks`; on a Node without it, every kernel fires on its own, so run one kernel per root there.

### `/dev/sealed`

A character device (`0666`) through which a program talks to the embedder's `sealed` hook (`createKernel({ sealed })`), the secrets vault on the page. Emscripten programs, WASI programs (`std::fs`) and JS programs (`ctx.open`, `ctx.sync.open`, listed by `ctx.fs.readdir('/dev')`) all open it, through the same kernel device, with the same framing, errnos and stamp: a JS program's stamp is built in the kernel from its own process, as for any other. The kernel holds the mode a descriptor was opened with, in every runtime, for its `dup`s and for the copies children inherit through fork, spawn and exec: one opened read-only refuses writes with `EBADF`, and one opened write-only refuses reads. Emscripten programs see it in `/dev` and open it by any path that leads to it (`./sealed` from `/dev`, `..`, a symlink to it or to `/dev`), and `fstat` and `/proc/self/fd` show it as `/dev/sealed`, a character device, after fork and exec too; WASI and JS programs open it by a path with no symlink on the way, and WASI programs by one without `..`.
- **State per open:** each `open()` is independent; `dup` and fork share one, as on Linux.
- **One write is one request:** a single UTF-8 JSON object of at most 64 KiB with `v` 1 and a known `op` (`seal`, `placeholder`, `list`, `test`, `request`, `status`, `cancel`, `materialize`). The write takes all the bytes or fails: `EMSGSIZE` over 64 KiB, `EINVAL` for anything else that isn't such an object. These are the only errnos; every other outcome is a JSON reply. The kernel zeroes the bytes it was given once it has parsed them.
- **The stamp:** the hook gets the request with `caller: { pid, ppid, uid, gid, user?, groups, users, tag? }`, from the process that writes (at the write; not the one that opened it): `uid` and `gid` the effective ids, `user` the `/etc/passwd` name of `uid` read at that moment (left out when there is none), `groups` the process's supplementary groups (only root can change them), `users` the kernel's `users` mode, since a uid means more under `'enforce'`, and `tag` the process's tag (see `kernel.run`) when it has one. A `caller` in the request is replaced.
- **Reading:** the reply comes from the same descriptor, as JSON (`{ v: 1, … }`). A read waits for it, drains it, then returns end-of-file; with no request outstanding it returns end-of-file at once. A signal ends the wait with `EINTR` and the request stays outstanding, `O_NONBLOCK` gives `EAGAIN`, and `poll`/`select` report the descriptor readable once the reply is there. A new write drops an unread reply. The kernel zeroes its copy of a reply once it has been read to the end, dropped or closed.
- **The hook:** `signal` aborts with `'timeout'` after 10 s (the reply is then `{ v: 1, error: { code: 'ETIMEDOUT' } }`), `'replaced'` when a new write on the descriptor comes first, and `'withdrawn'` when the writer exits or the descriptor is closed. A throw or a reply that isn't an object becomes an `EIO` error reply, with the first line of the message; no hook is `ENOTCONN`. On the page, `createKernel` relays the request from the kernel worker and back, and the abort too.

**Exec tickets.** A `materialize` request needs `argv` (an array of strings), `path` and `cwd` (strings), and `names` (an array of strings) or `shellGrants: true`; anything else is `EINVAL` at the write. When the hook's reply carries a `ticket` (32 lowercase hex digits, no `error`), the kernel books it for the process that wrote the request: the pid, the `path`, the sha256 of the JSON of `argv`, and the `cwd`, for 30 s, at most 256 at a time. A spawn or exec whose environment has `SLICC_SEALED_TICKET` (from WASIX `proc_spawn`/`proc_exec`, an Emscripten `exec`, a JS program's `spawn` or `sync.run`, or `sudo`) redeems it, and the kernel takes the variable out of the child's environment either way. It also takes it out of what `run`, `spawn` and `openTerminal` are given. The ticket is used up by any attempt, and the spawn fails with `EACCES` (status 126 in bash, with no message beyond the errno) unless all of these hold:
- the ticket is booked and under 30 s old;
- the process spawning or exec'ing is the one that sent the `materialize`;
- the program's file is absolute and, with its symlinks resolved, the same file as `path` resolved the same way when the ticket was booked (so fnox sends the path it would exec, from a `PATH` lookup or not; bash hands over the file it found, with `argv[0]` as typed);
- the sha256 of the `argv` the spawn or exec asked for, with the `argv[0]` the child really gets (a spawn's `argv0`, unless the command's manifest fixes its own), is the booked one; arguments a command's manifest adds in front (`args`) are the command's, and aren't part of it;
- the child's directory, symlinks resolved, is `cwd`, and the child starts in that resolved directory.

The kernel then asks the hook `{ v: 1, op: 'materializeConsume', ticket, stamp, path, resolved, argv, cwd, interpreters, trusted }` (slicc-vault#10):
- `stamp` is the child's caller stamp, as above (`pid` the child's; `ppid` the process that spawned or exec'd it, which is the one that sent the `materialize`, so for an exec both are that process's pid), also given as `caller`, as on every request;
- `path` and `cwd` are the ones the `materialize` named, and `resolved` the file that runs, symlinks resolved;
- `interpreters` is the chain of `#!` and `binfmt` interpreters the kernel will really run, outermost first, each absolute with its symlinks resolved, and empty for a program of the catalog (Emscripten, WASI or JS) and the `/usr/bin/<name>` entries for one. A plain `#!/usr/bin/env X` (or `env -S X …`) is reported as `X`: a command of the catalog as `/usr/bin/X`, a script command by its file. `env` with any other option or a `NAME=value` assignment is run and reported as `/usr/bin/env` itself;
- `trusted` is true when the program and every interpreter are regular files owned by root and not writable by group or others, under directories that are all the same, up to `/` (for a command of the catalog, its package files). It's false unless `users` is `'enforce'`: under `'off'` files have no owners, and under `'report'` owners don't stop a write. The reply must be `{ values, placeholders }` (string maps) within 10 s; anything else is `EACCES`. Each variable of the child's environment whose value is exactly `placeholders[NAME]` gets `values[NAME]`; nothing else changes. The kernel keeps no copy and logs nothing of either. Like any environment, the values are inherited by whatever the child itself spawns or execs, as on Linux. A ticket isn't carried across `sudo`, which builds its own environment: a `sudo` run with `SLICC_SEALED_TICKET` set fails with `EACCES`, and burns the ticket.

**The child's output is scrubbed (S2).** The kernel compiles a [slicc-scrub](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-scrub) scrubber (1.0.0, bundled; the published package has no runtime dependency) over exactly the values it swapped in, and gives the child new open file descriptions for fd 1 and fd 2, for every other descriptor it holds on the same descriptions (a `dup` of them), and for every descriptor on a terminal (fd 0 of a terminal session is one), that pass every write through it into the originals (one wrapper per original description, so `2>&1` keeps its order). A value appears as its placeholder, and a fragment or encoded form of it (JSON, percent, HTML, base64, hex, wrapped) as `[redacted:NAME]`. Bytes go through at once unless they may still be the start of a match; such a tail is let out after 50 ms without a write when it's shorter than 16 bytes, and otherwise at the next write or at close. The child's descendants inherit the scrubbed descriptions through fork, spawn, exec and `dup`, and a descendant that opens its controlling terminal (`/dev/tty`, or a terminal by name) gets one scrubbed too, as does a pseudo-terminal slave (`/dev/pts/N`) or [`/dev/clipboard`](#devclipboard) it opens, and a `/dev/clipboard` descriptor it inherited is wrapped at the redeem like fd 1 and 2, so the page's clipboard gets placeholders, never the values. Files, sockets and pipes the child or a descendant opens itself are not scrubbed, so `printf %s "$T" > f` writes the value. A scrubbed fd 1 or 2 is a stream: it doesn't seek. Under `O_NONBLOCK` it gives `EAGAIN` when the pipe or terminal under it would. A write that finds room returns at once, taking at most 64 KiB, and the scrubbed bytes the file couldn't take yet go out before anything written later. Until they have, `poll` reports it unwritable and a nonblocking write gets `EAGAIN`, taking nothing. A blocking write that waits for them, or for room, ends with `EINTR` at a signal, taking nothing; one interrupted after it began writing returns its whole count, and the rest follows. A descendant that redeems a ticket of its own adds its values: its output is scrubbed for both sets. A value under 8 bytes is swapped but can't be scrubbed. The scrubber goes when the last process and description holding it do.

**A2 sessions (`sessionMaterialize`, [#1090](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1090)).** For a kernel-terminated TLS session (see **Sealed values in an A2 session** under JS programs' `ctx.net`), the kernel asks the hook `{ v: 1, op: 'sessionMaterialize', caller, stamp, session, host, port, protocol, position, verified, handshakeAt, exit, placeholders, head? }` (slicc-vault 1.10.0, Sessions), once per session:
- `stamp` (also `caller`) is the stamp of the process that owns the socket; `session` the kernel's id for it (16 to 128 of `A-Za-z0-9_-`); `host` the name the leaf was verified against, lower-cased without a trailing dot; `handshakeAt` when the handshake finished (epoch ms); `exit` the exit the session leaves by, as the uplink reported it (`tailscale` or `device:<id>`, the id of `[A-Za-z0-9._:-]`);
- `protocol` is `http1`, `smtp` or `pg`, and `position` one of its own: `header:<lower-cased name>`, `auth-plain` or `auth-login`, or `password-cleartext`; `head` (`{ method, path }`) is there for `http1` only, and required there;
- `placeholders` are the 1 to 16 distinct `fnox_proxy_<24 hex>` placeholders found at that position.

The kernel never asks for a session that isn't `verified`, whose handshake is more than 60 s old (or in the future), or whose fields aren't of that shape. The reply is `{ values: { [placeholder]: value } }` within 10 s; the kernel takes only the placeholders it asked for, with a non-empty value free of CR, LF and NUL, and anything else (an error such as `{ v: 1, error: { code } }`, a timeout, a malformed reply, no hook) means nothing is substituted: the placeholders go out as written.

### `/dev/clipboard`

A character device (`0666`), there in every `users` mode, through which programs read and write the embedder's clipboard (`createKernel({ clipboard: { read, write } })`; on the page, bios answers with `navigator.clipboard`). Emscripten programs (`cat /dev/clipboard`, `cat > /dev/clipboard`, see it in `/dev`, reach it by any path that leads to it, and see it as a character device after fork and exec), WASI programs (`std::fs`) and JS programs (`ctx.fs.readFile`, `ctx.fs.writeFile`, `ctx.open`) all open it.
- **State per open file description:** `dup`'d and fork-inherited descriptors share one snapshot and one buffer, as on Linux; each `open()` is new. `lseek` fails with `ESPIPE`, and `poll`/`select` report it readable and writable at once.
- **Reading:** the first `read()` asks `read(stamp)` and takes a snapshot of the text as UTF-8; later reads continue it, and its end is end-of-file. A failed read keeps no snapshot, so the next read asks again. A signal ends the wait with `EINTR`.
- **Writing:** the bytes are buffered, and the last close of the description calls `write(text, stamp)` with all of them as one string, and waits for it, so `close()` returns its error. A description opened `O_WRONLY` always commits (with nothing written, `write('')` clears the clipboard, so `: > /dev/clipboard` works); one opened `O_RDWR` commits only once it has been written to, and one opened `O_RDONLY` never. More than 1 MiB in all fails that `write()` with `EFBIG`, and the description stays failed: later writes fail too and its close commits nothing. Bytes that aren't valid UTF-8 fail the close with `EILSEQ`, and the hook isn't called. With `O_RDWR`, reads come from the snapshot and writes go to the buffer, independently.
- **Errors:** a hook that throws a `NotAllowedError` (no focus, or permission denied) gives `EACCES`; anything else, a hook that doesn't answer within 10 s ("clipboard timed out") included, gives `EIO`; no `clipboard` hook gives `ENOTSUP` on read and on a committing close (writes still buffer). The first line of the hook's message reaches JS programs in the error's message (`fd-close: … : EACCES`). Emscripten and WASI programs get the errno, and when the hook refuses or fails (`EACCES` or `EIO`, a timeout included) at a read or at a commit, the kernel first writes `<argv0>: /dev/clipboard: <message>` to the process's standard error, before the call returns, so it comes before the program's own error line (`cat: /dev/clipboard: The clipboard needs…` then `cat: write error: Permission denied`). It is one line: control characters are written as `\t`, `\n`, `\r` or `\xNN`, and it is cut at 200 characters. It's skipped when standard error is closed or is `/dev/clipboard` itself, and at a teardown commit.
- **Standard output at exit:** an Emscripten program's libc never closes its standard output, so the kernel does what coreutils' `close_stdout` would: when it closes standard error at exit (or, failing that, when the runtime shuts down) with `/dev/clipboard` still on standard output, it commits it, and on an error prints `<program>: write error: <strerror>` to standard error and makes a 0 exit status 1. So `printf x | cat > /dev/clipboard` prints `cat: write error: Permission denied` and fails when the hook refuses. Only `/dev/clipboard` descriptors report their close errors this way; every other descriptor's close is as before.
- **Exit without close:** a process that ends with a description still open commits at its teardown, stamped with that process; the error is then lost, as nobody is left to get it.
- **The stamp:** `{ pid, uid, gid, users, tag? }`: the pid as the program sees it, the effective ids, the kernel's `users` mode, and the process's `tag` (see `kernel.run`; a process inherits it from its parent, and a client's processes get the client's `connect({ tag })`), in every `users` mode. A read is stamped with the process that reads first; a commit with the process that closes last (or ends, at teardown).
- **Cost:** other files' reads and closes check one property more, and their writes nothing; an Emscripten close checks one flag.
- **On the page,** `createKernel` relays the calls from the kernel worker and back, keeping the error's `name` and `message`.

### Protected paths

`createKernel({ protect: { paths: ['/node_modules', '/os'] } })` makes those directories read-only for every process, root included, in every `users` mode. They hold code every later process runs, which only the embedder writes, straight to OPFS.
- **What fails:** any change under a path fails with `EROFS` ("Read-only file system"): a write, a create, a truncate, `mkdir`, removing or renaming (from or to), a hard or symbolic link created there, `chmod`, `chown` and setting times. So does removing or renaming a directory that holds one (`rm -rf /`). A missing working directory for `run` or `openTerminal` isn't created there either. Reads are unaffected. `'/'` protects the whole file system.
- **Stats and symlinks:** files under a path show as on a read-only mount, also when reached through a symlink or a symlinked directory, so opens for writing fail at once, and `access(W_OK)` (`[ -w ]`) answers `EROFS` (with `AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW`, for the link itself: a link elsewhere to a protected file is writable). A change refused there fails with `EROFS` before any permission check, as on Linux, also removing or renaming a directory that holds a path, so a user sees "Read-only file system" rather than "Permission denied". `paths` must be a list of absolute paths.
- **Where it's checked:** the root file system itself checks every change, from the moment the kernel is constructed, inside the change and against the path it really lands on. So a symlink swapped mid-way can't redirect a write. The kernel's own writes there are refused too, so don't protect what the kernel writes after it starts:
  - `/etc` (`passwd`, `group` and the proxy's CA);
  - `/home` and `/root` (new users' homes);
  - `/tmp`;
  - the system `PNPM_HOME` (`/usr/local/share/pnpm`);
  - `/.slicc-unlinked` (files removed while open);
  - `/` itself, which holds all of these.
  Paths in the layout the kernel stamps with root's owner at boot are skipped when protected.
- **Symlinks as paths:** a path that is itself a symlink protects the directory it names too, by its real path, once the kernel has prepared.
- **Mounts:** nothing can be mounted at, under or above a path, not even by the embedder (`kernel.mount`, `/etc/fstab`), and not by a writer. Above includes `/`: with any path protected, nothing can be mounted on `/`. So a path always lies on the root file system, where that check holds.
- **Clients:** a client's (`kernel.connect`) file calls, mounts and unmounts see the paths read-only, as its processes do.
- **Not affected:** the page's own writes to OPFS.
- **Writers:** `protect: { paths, writers: ['bios-installer'] }` names process tags whose processes may change the paths, such as the embedder's own installs (`kernel.run(['pnpm', 'install'], { tag: 'bios-installer' })`). A tag matches exactly, with no prefix or case folding. A process with several tags (see `kernel.connect`) is a writer only when every one of them is a writer tag, so a tag added to a writer's spawn takes away its write access, and one added to a connection without a writer tag never makes a writer. A process gets its tag only from the embedder, through `kernel.run`, `kernel.openTerminal` or `kernel.connect({ tag })`, and passes it to its children, forks and `exec`s; a program or a client can't choose one. Writers work on their own view of the root file system, so a file a writer changes is seen by other processes as between two tabs: close-to-open, at their next open after the writer closes it.
- **Integrity:** `protect: { paths, integrity: { lookup, unknown, sources } }` also checks what a program is made of before it runs. That covers a wasm module, an Emscripten program's glue, a WASI program's imports module, a JS program's module, the `package.json` of each package under a path (the kernel builds its commands, `binfmt` suffixes and drivers only from manifests that pass, so a package whose manifest changed has no commands, 127, and no drivers, `ENODEV`), a script started through its `#!` line or a `binfmt` suffix, a package driver's module and the TLS engine, when the file lies under a path.
  - **The check:** the kernel hashes the exact bytes it is about to compile or hand to a worker (SHA-256, hex), and asks `lookup(path)` (the real path; on the page, it may be async) for the hash it expects. On a mismatch the program doesn't start: `kernel.run` rejects and a spawn fails with `EACCES` ("integrity check failed"; 126 in a shell, not `ENOEXEC`, which would make a shell run the file as a script), a driver fails to mount, TLS fails, and `onDenial` gets `{ op: 'exec-integrity', path, errno: 'EACCES', pid, uid, tag?, expected, actual }`. A `lookup` that throws counts as a mismatch.
  - **No hash known:** when `lookup` has none (`undefined`), `unknown: 'allow'` (the default) runs the file and reports `{ op: 'exec-unverified', errno: 'ENODATA', … }` once per path, while `unknown: 'deny'` refuses it as a mismatch.
  - **The cache:** a result is kept per real path and the file's version: what OPFS itself reports (its `lastModified` and `size`), plus the count of writes kernels made to the file, which the metadata store keeps (Chrome doesn't move `lastModified` for a write through a sync access handle). A kernel with `integrity` set counts a write to a protected file before its bytes change, and again once the write is over (also when it failed), at wherever the file is by then; the count follows a rename, and no `utimes` turns it back. The version also holds the count of moves into protection: a rename or a symlink at or above a protected path counts one before and after it, so a move into a protected path re-verifies every protected program once. So a change made around the kernel, same size included, is checked again at the next start. A kernel without `integrity` counts nothing (so writes into protected paths cost no metadata update), and its writes don't move the version another kernel on the origin reads: give every kernel on one origin the same `integrity`. The cache is the kernel's own, in memory, so nothing verified outlives it. The compiled module is cached by the same key, so a program that hasn't changed costs nothing more to start.
  - **Revoking:** a result is kept until the file changes, so when `lookup`'s answers change (an updated hash list, a package revoked or reinstalled), call `await kernel.integrity.revoke(path?)` (on the page and in Node). It drops what the kernel verified for that path, or for everything under it when it's a directory (its real path and the name a link reached it by both count), or for every path without one, and rebuilds the command catalog from the manifests as checked again. A load that was under way when `revoke` was called keeps nothing. It takes effect at the next start: running processes keep what they loaded. `path` must be absolute; without `integrity` it does nothing.
  - **Where `lookup` gets its hashes:** from a source independent of the installed files, such as the per-file hashes the embedder derives from the certified package tarball when it builds its catalog. Hashes read back from the installed files prove nothing.
  - **Links out of the paths:** a file reached through a protected path but whose real path lies outside every path (a symlink at or below a protected path, or one of its directories, pointing elsewhere, a command of the catalog included) is still checked, under the last protected name on the way: the kernel asks `lookup(name)` for the bytes of the real file, `unknown` applies when it has no hash, and `onDenial` reports the name as `path`. Writes outside the paths aren't counted, so such a result isn't cached: the file is hashed again at every start.
  - **Sources a program reads itself** ([#454](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/454)): a runtime that loads code on its own is checked too, when a process reads the file through the kernel. That covers jsh's and slicc-node's modules and the `package.json` files whose `main`/`exports` they follow, python's `.py`, `.pyc`, `.pth` and zip imports, `dlopen`'s side modules, and a script's interpreter reading the script after its exec check (a file swapped in between is refused then).
    - **Which files:** those under a path whose name ends in one of `integrity.sources`, plus every regular file with an execute bit (a `#!` script without a suffix, a sourced script). The default is `['.js', '.mjs', '.cjs', '.json', '.py', '.pyc', '.pth', '.zip', '.so', '.wasm', '.sh']`. An embedder can replace the list, and `sources: []` turns these checks off and leaves only the ones above. Data files aren't checked, since a package can hold files no `lookup` knows (lock files, caches), which `unknown: 'deny'` would make unreadable.
    - **Where:** a whole-file read (a JS program's `ctx.fs.readFile` and `ctx.sync.fs.readFile`, a `.json` included, and the dynamic linker's) gets exactly the bytes that were hashed. An open (WASI and Emscripten programs, so ranged reads too) is checked when the file is opened. The result is cached per real path and version, as for programs, so an unchanged file costs a version check: OPFS's own `lastModified` and size, plus one metadata read.
    - **Refused:** a read or an open fails with `EACCES`, and `onDenial` gets `{ op: 'read-integrity', … }`. A file with no known hash is reported once as `read-unverified`.
    - **Writers** (`protect.writers`) aren't checked, nor their children and the lifecycle scripts they run, which inherit the tag. A writer may change these files anyway, and an install loads modules while it replaces them. Its writes are counted, so the next read or start by any other process verifies them against `lookup` again. A writer misled by a file changed around the kernel can't get unverified code run elsewhere, so writing OPFS around the kernel gains nothing.
    - **Not caught:** a file changed around the kernel between an open and a later ranged read of it is read as it is then. Checking every ranged read would cost a metadata read for each one. Nor is a same-size rewrite by a writer in another kernel on the origin (another tab), read before that tab's change notice arrives: these checks take the write count from the metadata store's cache, which the notice clears, so the window runs from the write to the notice. OPFS's own `lastModified` and size are read every time, so a change made outside every kernel is still caught.
- **Where it is a boundary:** it's one in a browser, where programs' own code can't reach OPFS around the kernel (see [JS programs](#js-programs)). Under the Node entry it isn't, until [#407](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/407), and there `kernel.writeFile` under a path fails too, so write those files into `root` before creating the kernel.

### `/dev` entries

`/dev` and `/proc` hold the kernel's own entries, and a program can't add or remove names there. Only `/dev/shm`, a tmpfs, takes new files.
- **Removing** an entry fails and leaves it: `EACCES` for a user, `EPERM` for root on a device, `EBUSY` for `/dev` itself.
- **Creating** a name that doesn't exist fails in every runtime, whether it's a file, a directory, a symlink, a device node, or the new name of a link or a rename: `EACCES` for a user, `EPERM` for root in `/dev`, and `EROFS` in `/proc`.
- **Why:** an Emscripten program's `/dev` and `/proc` live in its glue's own memory, so such a file used to look written and then vanish when the process exited. On the root file system no other runtime would see it either.
- **Existing entries** (`/dev/null`, the terminals, `/dev/std*`) open and write as before.

### `/proc`

Every process of the kernel has `/proc/<pid>/` with `cmdline`, `comm`, `stat`, `statm` and `status`, in the formats procps reads, and `exe`, a link to the file the process was started from: the glue or `.wasm` that ran, as resolved (a copy run by its own path names the copy, a symlink its target) (#1264). For WASI and WASIX programs, as for Emscripten ones, `stat`, `open` and reading `/proc/self/exe` act on the program file, while `lstat` and `readlink` show the link (#1273). A symlink to it is followed the same way, and once the program's name is moved away or holds another file, `exe` no longer opens it: `readlink` ends in ` (deleted)` and `stat` and `open` are `ENOENT` (#1317). As on Linux without ptrace access, reading another process's `exe` link is `EACCES` unless the reader is root or its uid and gid match all of the target's real, effective and saved ids; `cmdline` and `status` stay readable, and `/proc/self` is a link to the `/proc/<pid>` of the process that reads it, as on Linux (`readlink /proc/self` is its pid, the one it was exec'd under included). They come from the kernel's process table when they are opened, so a terminal sees the processes of every client. A process that exits while a program reads the `/proc` directory doesn't fail or cut short the listing: its pid is still listed, and opening its files fails with `ENOENT`, which `ps`, `pgrep` and `pidof` skip, as on Linux; an exec'd program shows under the pid its parent knows. `/proc/uptime`, `/proc/loadavg`, `/proc/stat` and `/proc/meminfo` are there too, and `/proc/mounts`. `/proc/version` names the kernel, Linux-shaped, with the slicc-kernel version that built it: `Linux version 6.0.0-slicc (slicc-kernel <version>) #1 SMP PREEMPT_DYNAMIC`. `/proc/sys/kernel/osrelease` is `6.0.0-slicc` and `/proc/sys/kernel/ostype` is `Linux`. The version is the one npm publishes, set when the package is built (`0.0.0-dev` when run from the sources). A JS program reads it as `ctx.kernel.version`. A process with no parent in the kernel (one a page, a client or the Node entry started) has parent 1, as children of init do, in `getppid()`, `/proc/<pid>/stat` and `ps` alike, and so does a process whose parent has ended, waited for or not (`getppid()` asks the kernel each time; a shell's `$PPID` keeps the value it started with, as on Linux). Pids, parents, process groups, sessions, command lines, terminals, start and boot times are real, and so are the credentials in `status` (`Uid:`, `Gid:`, `Groups:`; a process with a tag, see `kernel.run`, also has `Tag:\t<tag>` after `Gid:`, one line per tag when it has several, shown to root and to the same uid only, with newlines, tabs and carriage returns written `\n`, `\t` and `\r`) and each process's memory: the size of its wasm memory (`VSZ` and `RSS` alike, as wasm has no paging), which `/proc/meminfo` counts as used. CPU times are measured per process, its threads and the images it exec'd included: time a worker spends waiting for the kernel's answer is system time (at most 20 ms of each call; the rest of a longer one, such as a write into a full pipe, counts as blocked), time it spends blocked (a read, a `poll` or `select` that waits, a sleep, `wait`, a futex, while stopped) is neither, from the moment the call actually starts to wait (a call that could wait but answers at once, such as a `select` with a zero timeout or a ready descriptor, a `waitpid` with `WNOHANG`, or a futex wait whose value has already changed, is system time), and the rest is user time; a process's times stop when it ends, a call still in flight then adds nothing, so a loop that never calls the kernel shows as user time while it runs; they show in `/proc/<pid>/stat`, `ps` and `ps()`, and the times of the children a process has waited for (theirs and their own waited-for children's, as POSIX counts them) show as its `cutime` and `cstime` in `/proc/<pid>/stat`; a stopped process is `T` there, in `/proc/<pid>/status` (`T (stopped)`) and in `ps()`; a WASI or WASIX program's `CLOCK_PROCESS_CPUTIME_ID` and `CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID` (`clock_time_get`) count only the time its thread runs, its own waits and blocked kernel calls left out (a sleep is not CPU time), per thread for now, so a threaded program's process clock is its calling thread's; and `/proc/stat` counts every process of the kernel, the reaped ones included, and `/proc/loadavg` averages the processes not blocked, sampled every 5 seconds. A JS program's time isn't measured yet. `MemTotal` (from `navigator.deviceMemory`, else 4 GiB) is a placeholder. A process that isn't root sees only its own user's processes (see [Users](#users)). WASI and WASIX programs see the same `/proc`, read-only (opening a file in it for writing, or linking one of its files elsewhere, fails with `EROFS`), from every thread, also through a symlink that points into it (where every change, `mkdir`, `rename`, `unlink`, `chmod`, a hard link from it or into it, and so on, fails too: with `EROFS` when the root file system has a physical `/proc`, which stays hidden and untouched, and otherwise with the `ENOENT` of the missing directory, since that check is only paid for when a physical `/proc` exists; with one, a symlink whose target passes `..` after a file or a missing name, such as `/f/../proc`, fails with `ENOTDIR` or `ENOENT`, as on Linux), and `/` lists it, so a WASI program gets a preopen for it; `/proc/self` is the program's own pid there too (the pid an exec'd program took over). WASI and WASIX programs also have `/proc/<pid>/task/<tid>/stat` for each of a process's threads (the main thread is tid 1), whose state is `S` while the thread is blocked in the kernel (a `sem_wait`, a blocking read, a sleep, a futex) and `R` otherwise (`T` and `Z` as for the process). JS programs read the same `/proc` through `ctx.fs` and `ctx.open` (see [JS programs](#js-programs)). Neither has `/proc/<pid>/fd` yet.

The kernel writes `/etc/passwd` and `/etc/group` when it starts (see [Users](#users)). While the root has none of them, or no `/etc/mtab`, reading them gets per-process ones: root is uid 0 and the realm user, uid 1000, is named after `USER`, or `web_user` (Emscripten's default) without one, so it matches `$USER`, with `HOME` as its home, so `id`, `whoami`, `ls -l` and `ps` show names. Nothing is written to OPFS, and a real file there wins.

### How many processes at once

In a browser every process worker of the page shares one renderer. V8 reserves about 8 GiB of address space for each 32-bit wasm memory, whatever its declared maximum, and its sandbox holds about 1 TiB. So a page runs about 125 wasm processes (or programs that own a wasm memory) at once, counting everything in the renderer (#970). Node has no such ceiling, and neither does Linux. The kernel keeps processes inside that ceiling:
- **The ceiling:** 112 by default in a browser and none in Node (`createNodeKernel({ wasmMemories: { ceiling, idleMs } })` sets one in Node; `idleMs`, the quiet spell before `EAGAIN` below, 10 s by default, is there for tests). It's the kernel's count of live wasm processes, with the kernel's other wasm memories in the page left outside it. When an instantiation runs out of memory anyway, the kernel learns the real ceiling at that moment (the live count minus one), and that learned value wins over the default. A learned ceiling is never below 16, since an OOM at a low count means something outside it (another tab in the same renderer) filled the renderer. It's raised by 8 for each minute without another OOM, back to the default.
- **Admission:** a spawn, fork, exec or `kernel.run` at the ceiling waits for another process to exit. It keeps waiting as long as processes exit, are admitted or finish starting (a process becoming ready counts, so a slow host that is still booting the processes ahead of it doesn't fail the spawn, #1196), and fails with `EAGAIN` only after 10 s with none of these. A long `make -j` or `xargs -P` therefore queues, and a pipeline that needs more live processes than the ceiling fails cleanly. bash's own retry on a fork's `EAGAIN` (`fork: retry: Resource temporarily unavailable`) handles the rest.
- **JS workers count too** (#1133): each JS process and each `thread.spawn` thread takes one slot, whether or not it creates wasm memories, so a worker that creates several under-counts within a small factor, and a shared memory posted to threads is one reservation however many threads use it. A JS worker also costs the renderer about 65 MB of RAM, which the same bound keeps in check. `thread.spawn` waits for a slot as a spawn does, and rejects with `EAGAIN` after the same quiet spell. With many JS workers live, wasm processes wait earlier, which is deliberate. Node has no ceiling, so nothing changes there, and the per-process thread cap (`SLICC_JS_THREADS`) still applies on top.
- **Close to the ceiling** (within 16 of it), the kernel waits for each new process to finish instantiating before the spawn returns. If it ran out of memory, the kernel starts it again, quietly, once there's room, so the caller sees no failure.
- **The rare residual case:** a process that fails to instantiate with nobody waiting on it, which happens only when something outside the count fills the renderer. It ends with status 126 and `<program>: cannot allocate memory for a new process (too many processes at once)` on its stderr, never a JS `RangeError`.
- **Below a learned ceiling,** a process that still runs out of memory is started again once another process exits, and the spawn fails with `EAGAIN` after 10 s with none.
- **`/proc/sys/kernel/slicc_wasm_memories`** reads `<live> <ceiling> <source>`. The source is `default`, `learned`, or `none` (with the ceiling 0).

`/proc/sys/kernel/slicc_shared_root` reads `1` while another kernel shares the root (a tab, or a second kernel in the page) and `0` otherwise. A peer whose kernel ends (its tab closes or reloads) stops counting at once: each kernel queues a request for every peer's alive Web Lock, and the grant tells it the peer has gone ([#984](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/984)). `/proc/sys/kernel/slicc_fs_calls` reads the number of path calls (lookups, stats, opens, creates, removals and the like) the kernel has served its programs since it started. `/proc/sys/kernel/slicc_opfs_calls` counts the OPFS calls the kernel has made to look names up and read stats (`getFile`, `getFileHandle`, `getDirectoryHandle`, `resolve`). `/proc/sys/kernel/slicc_opfs_ops` counts every request the root file system makes of OPFS: those lookups, plus creates, writes (one per write, truncate or write session), reads, listings, renames and removals ([#1017](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1017)). With an idle peer, a tar unpack makes at most 1.5 times the calls it makes alone ([#984](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/984)). `/proc/sys/kernel/slicc_cwd_checks` counts the times a process asked the kernel whether its [working directory](#the-working-directory) is still the one it holds.

### Metadata

OPFS stores names, bytes, sizes and modification times, nothing else. Everything POSIX needs on top of that lives in an IndexedDB sidecar, so it survives reloads and new kernels:

- mode bits (`chmod`; a `chmod` to the mode a file or directory already has writes nothing and leaves its ctime as it was, which saves a sidecar write for every installer that sets the mode a file was made with);
- access and change times, and a modification time set explicitly with `utime` (it holds until the file is written again: any write or truncate moves it to the time of the write, also in Chrome, whose OPFS doesn't move a file's `lastModified` for writes through a sync access handle, so the kernel records the write's time itself, and it reaches the metadata store up to 100 ms after the write, so a tab closed inside that window keeps the old mtime on disk; the time follows a rename and is dropped with the file); a directory's times move whenever an entry is created, removed or renamed in it, as POSIX asks, and a time set explicitly after such a change keeps winning, also when the change was made in another kernel and its write lands later; an Emscripten program reads a directory's times again after any call that can wait on another process (a pipe or socket read, `wait`, `poll`, a sleep), so a program that waits for a child or a peer to fill a directory sees the new times (rsync's final directory times rely on it), while a walk without waits reads each directory once; `chown`/`fchown` leave the times alone (Emscripten stamps the setattr of a chown with the current time, which isn't taken as an mtime);
- inode numbers that stay stable across renames. A file with no record has an inode derived from its path, and a rename keeps it on the file; the rename leaves a small record at the name it vacated with a fresh inode, so a file, symlink or directory made there later doesn't share the renamed one's ([#954](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/954)). Removing a directory leaves the same record, so a directory made at its name has a new inode, which is how a process's [working directory](#the-working-directory) notices the swap. The record becomes the new entry's own when something is created there; one with nothing at its name is pruned by the next boot that's alone on the root. An exclusive create doesn't count that record as a taken name, and the boot reconcile keeps it only while a live record still carries the inode it guards;
- symbolic links (`symlink`, `readlink`, `lstat`, and following them in paths), which exist only in the sidecar, with their own times: `touch -h`, `lutimes` and `utimensat` with `AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW` set the link's, never the target's. A link's own mode can't be changed, as on Linux: `chmod -h`, `lchmod` and `fchmodat` with `AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW` fail with `EOPNOTSUPP` (which `chmod -h` and `tar` ignore for links) and never follow it, and `open` with `O_PATH | O_NOFOLLOW` gives a descriptor for the link itself.

OPFS stays authoritative: an entry is consulted only for a path that exists in OPFS (symlinks only for paths that don't), so files written straight to OPFS, for example by the BIOS installer or a page, get the defaults: directories `0755`, files `0644`, and files under `node_modules/*/bin/` `0755`. A file deleted through the OPFS API disappears at once; its leftover entry is ignored and removed when the next kernel starts. Changes go to OPFS first and then to the sidecar, so a crash in between at worst loses the metadata of that one change. Each change is a read-modify-write inside one IndexedDB transaction, so two kernels (two tabs) on the same origin don't lose each other's updates.

**Stamping files written to OPFS:** `import { writeMetadata } from '@ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/meta'` and `await writeMetadata(root, [{ path, mode?, uid?, gid? }], { metadata?, indexedDB? })` is the supported way for an embedder to give files it wrote straight to OPFS a mode and an owner, also while no kernel runs (an installer before the first boot). `root` is the directory handle that becomes `/`, each `path` is absolute (`EINVAL` otherwise) and must exist there (`ENOENT` otherwise), `mode` is the permission bits (`0` to `0o7777`), and `uid` and `gid` come together or not at all (`EINVAL` otherwise). Entries for the same path (after normalization) are merged in order, a later field winning. All entries are written in one IndexedDB transaction, to the database a kernel on that root reads (`metadata`, default `slicc-kernel`), keeping what else a record holds and setting its change time (`ctime`) to now, and a running kernel on the same origin drops what it cached of them. The record format is the kernel's own and changes with it, so use this rather than writing the database. The kernel gives its layout paths (see [Permissions](#permissions)) an owner and mode only when they have no owner yet, so a path stamped here with `uid` and `gid` keeps them.

**Telling running kernels about files written to OPFS:** a writer outside the kernel (an installer worker, a service worker, the page) that changes files on a root a kernel may be running on calls `import { postFsNotice } from '@ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/meta'` and `postFsNotice({ paths?, trees? }, { metadata? })` **after** each write has committed: after `close()` of its writable, `removeEntry` or `move()` has resolved. `paths` are absolute paths written, created or removed. List a created or removed name's parent directory too, so listings refresh. `trees` are absolute directories whose whole subtree changed, for a recursive replace or a sweep. The notice goes on the BroadcastChannel `slicc-kernel-dirs:<metadata>` (default `slicc-kernel-dirs:slicc-kernel`), the one kernels on that root already use between themselves, as `{ paths, trees }`. One notice per batch of writes is enough, if it's posted after the last of them. A kernel that receives it drops its cached lookups, handles and misses for those paths (for a tree, everything under it), tells its open files to reread, and publishes the paths to its programs' caches. A tree marks every path changed for them, so a running program's next look at any path goes to the kernel. **The residual window:** a reader can still see the old state between the write's commit and the notice's delivery (a message, usually well under a millisecond, but not bounded). A writer that runs while no kernel is up needs no notice: a kernel's caches start empty when it boots. A notice for a path that exists as a regular file, listed in `paths` (a `trees` entry refreshes caches but stamps no times, since a tree can hold thousands of files), also gives it a new modification time, before running programs are told of the change: the notice's arrival, with the change time along, unless its modification time is already later (an explicit future one is kept, with its change time), as a write does on Linux, recorded once per burst of notices for the same file ([#1275](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1275)). That matters because Chrome can keep a file's OPFS `lastModified` across an append through `createWritable({ keepExistingData: true })`, and the kernel reports `lastModified` for files it didn't write, so `-nt` and make wouldn't see the change. A notice without a real change still moves the time, so a make may rebuild once. An outside write that posts no notice keeps OPFS's time. Notices between kernels (the array form they post for their own writes) don't stamp: those kernels record their times themselves. That's part of the channel's contract: kernels post their own change notices on the dirs channel as a plain array of paths, and only the `{ paths, trees }` object form that `postFsNotice` sends counts as an outside writer's. A kernel must keep posting arrays there, which `fs-notice-page.test.mjs`'s peer row checks. **Without a notice** a kernel that is alone on its root keeps what it cached: a running program's stats of a path stay as they were for as long as the program runs, and the kernel's own directory handles for up to a second. So a writer that doesn't post notices must not change files a running kernel uses ([#1156](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1156)).

The database is `slicc-kernel` (or the `metadata` option), schema version 1, with one object store, `entries`, keyed by absolute path (`keyPath: 'path'`) and an index `dir` on the parent directory of symlinks. A record looks like `{ path, mode?, atimeMs?, mtimeMs?, mtimeFor?, ctimeMs?, ino?, link?, dir?, uid?, gid?, heir?, writes?, epoch? }`, where `mtimeFor` is the OPFS `lastModified` an explicit `mtimeMs` belongs to, `uid`, `gid` and `heir` (`[uid, gid]`) are the owners of [File owners](#file-owners), `writes` counts the writes kernels with `integrity` made to a protected file (see [Protected paths](#protected-paths)), whose `lastModified` OPFS leaves alone for a write through a sync access handle, and `epoch`, on the entry of `/`, counts their renames and symlinks at or above a protected path. A future schema bumps the version and migrates in `onupgradeneeded`; an older kernel then closes its connection. The sidecar is keyed by path from the `root` handle, so use a different `metadata` name for each distinct root.

Not covered: hard links (OPFS cannot share contents between names; they work only within a mount whose driver offers `links`, such as `tmpfs`). File owners are stored only with `users: 'report'` or `'enforce'` (see [File owners](#file-owners)). With `users: 'off'`, an Emscripten process built against the kernel's credential calls (its glue imports `slicc_cred_js`, as homescoop's H1 builds do) sees every file as owned by its own effective uid and gid: root's 0 by default, a user's own ids when it runs as one, and the new ids after a `setuid`. Owner checks such as git's `safe.directory`, ssh's key checks and gnupg's home directory check then pass, as WASIX programs already see through their libc. An older program, whose libc says it is uid 1000 whoever runs it, sees every file as owned by 1000:1000 as before, so its own checks pass too. The kernel decides this once, when it loads the program. Emscripten also doesn't enforce permission bits, so a file runs whether or not its exec bit is set; the bits are what `ls`, `stat` and `test` report. With `users: 'off'` a file or directory with no permission bits at all shows as `0666` or `0777` to Emscripten programs; with owners on they see the real mode (`0`), and the kernel, not Emscripten's own checks, decides who may read, write or enter it (`cd` asks the kernel for `x` on the directory), so root still enters a `000` directory.

Reads retry briefly when a concurrent write has invalidated the OPFS file snapshot (`NotReadableError`).

At boot, a kernel drops sidecar records whose file is gone (and symlink records whose name became a real file or whose directory is gone), left behind by a tab that closed mid-change. It does this only when it is the only kernel on the origin (the watch channel's join handshake finds no other), and it drops a record only if it is still the one it found: another tab's live changes made while it looked, such as a placeholder tar replaces with a symlink, are kept. Up to 1.119.2, a tab that booted while another extracted symlinks with tar could drop some of them.

A kernel caches the directory handles it has walked and the sidecar entries it has read. Its own changes keep both caches current, and the other kernels on the origin hear of them through a `BroadcastChannel` named after the database (one for the sidecar, one for directories). A cached directory is checked against its path (`resolve()`) at most once a second before a lookup goes through it, so a directory moved, removed or replaced by another writer, such as a page using the OPFS API, is found at its new place or reported missing within a second. It also keeps the file handles it has looked up (up to 4,096, the least recently used dropped first) for a second after each lookup. Before it uses one, it reads the file through it, which is the read a `stat` needs anyway, and that read serves the handle's uses of the next 2 ms. So a file another writer removed is missing, and a rewritten one shows its new contents, within 2 ms. The kernel's own writes, truncates, rewrites and renames drop the read at once, and a read that started before an own change isn't kept, so the kernel's own changes always show at once. One replaced by a directory is seen as a directory within a second. Its own changes, and the other kernels' notices, drop the handles they touch at once. A lookup of a directory it has cached and checked within the second is answered from the cache, so a directory replaced by another writer is seen as replaced within a second, as above. A name it has just found missing stays missing for 2 ms, which serves the several checks a single create makes of a name that isn't there yet (about ten per file in a pnpm install); its own creates, renames and links in that directory end that at once, so only a file another kernel or a page using the OPFS API makes in those 2 ms is seen up to 2 ms late. While another kernel shares the root, both 2 ms windows follow the contract below. From [#945](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/945) until [#984](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/984) they were off then, which made every stat and every miss an OPFS call.

**What another tab sees, and when** ([#984](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/984)): one rule covers everything a kernel caches about a shared root.
- **Notices:** while another kernel shares the root, a kernel posts a notice before each changing call returns. A notice names the sidecar records it changed (owners, modes, links, times, write counts), the directories it created, removed or renamed into, and the files it wrote back ([#962](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/962)).
- **What a notice drops:** the receiving kernel drops at once what the notice names, and everything under it: sidecar entries, directory and file handles, the 2 ms file reads, the 2 ms missing names, and its programs' clean contents.
- **Unlocked, unsynchronized reads:** a read like this sees another tab's change once that change's notice has arrived. A read that races the notice can get the old answer, by at most a message's delivery. Even with the notice lost, a stat or lookup is at most 2 ms of this kernel's clock behind its own last look at that name.
- **Exact, whatever the notices:**
  - a lock grant (`flock`, `fcntl`, `lockf`, tryLock) drops every cached file read and missing name, so the holder's first look at each name goes to OPFS ([#949](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/949), [#958](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/958));
  - `fsync`, close and unlock write through and post their notice before they return ([#961](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/961));
  - an exclusive create (`O_EXCL`, `mkdir`, `symlink`, `link`) and a rename's check of its target decide against OPFS itself, never against a cached missing name ([#942](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/942));
  - the join handshake writes back every open file before a new kernel counts as a peer, and a change in whether the root is shared drops both 2 ms windows.

So a locked read-modify-write across tabs (sqlite, a JSON counter under `flock`) is exact, and anything else between tabs is as fresh as the last notice. The same holds for what a WASI program caches in its own process: a notice, a lock grant or a peer joining or leaving drops all of it, and a late or lost notice leaves an unlocked path stat at most 100 ms behind the kernel's answer; opens, descriptor calls and anything under a lock are exact ([#1017](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1017)). Emscripten programs add their own rule on top: see "Another tab's writes" above. `program-cache-tabs.test.mjs` holds the rows for programs; it freezes a program's clock through `page/notice/program-clock-worker.js`. `crowded-lookups.test.mjs` and `notice-caches.test.mjs` hold the rows. The `notice-caches` rows freeze one kernel's clock and drop the other's notices, through the kernel workers in `test/integration/page/notice/`.

**Within one kernel** ([#1026](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel/issues/1026)): a WASI process keeps the stats it has read, and checks each one against the kernel's change ring at every look. So a process that keeps stat-ing a file, even in a loop that never sleeps, sees another process's change to that path at its next stat. Before #1026's fix, from v1.4.0 on, a stat it had looked up lasted until the process slept, polled, waited for a child or wrote the path itself. The ring names the changed paths only, so in a loop with none of those calls, a stat stays stale until the next sleep, poll, wait or own write when the change was named elsewhere:
- a write through another hard link of the same file. Only tmpfs mounts support `link`; on the OPFS root and every other mount, a file's link count is always 1;
- a change to a symlink's target (the notice names the target, not the link);
- an ancestor renamed or replaced (`mv /a /c; mv /d /a`: the notices name `/a`, `/c` and `/d`, not `/a/f`).

The filesystem is our own rather than [ZenFS](https://github.com/zen-fs/core) (which SLICC uses), because OPFS stays the single source of truth: other writers, such as the BIOS installing packages or a page writing files, need no index to stay consistent with, and nothing is preloaded into memory at mount. POSIX metadata lives in the IndexedDB sidecar described above, which has per-entry transactions instead of one JSON file rewritten on every change.

## What is in here

The kernel is ported from SLICC's `packages/webapp/src/kernel/` with the browser-specific VFS replaced by an OPFS one:

- `src/kernel/`: the kernel side, per process (`host.ts` starts the worker and answers its syscalls in `process.ts`) and shared tables (descriptors, pipes, children, jobs, signals, terminals and pseudo-terminals, `select`), and process credentials (`cred.ts`).
- `src/process/`: the runtime inside each process worker. It evaluates the Emscripten glue, mounts the live VFS, routes descriptors through the kernel and implements `fork` by copying the whole linear memory into a new worker (Asyncify). `fork` is the only call allowed to suspend through Asyncify: an `fsync` that Emscripten made asynchronous is answered synchronously by the file's own stream instead. `src/process/wasi/` runs WASI and WASIX programs.
- `src/realm/`: the synchronous bridge (`SharedArrayBuffer` + `Atomics.wait`) and the live Emscripten filesystem on top of it. When a program's file call gets no answer, the bridge treats it like an NFS mount (#990). A read-only call (`read`, `pread`, `stat`, `lstat`, `readdir`, `readlink`, `exists`, `access`, `statfs`) fails with `EIO` once nothing has come back for 25 s; the clock starts again with every chunk of a long answer, so a slow transfer that keeps answering never fails (the host keeps a long answer for 30 s after each chunk it hands over, not after the first), and a late answer is dropped, which is harmless because the call changed nothing. A call that changes the file system (writes, `rename`, `unlink`, `mkdir`, creates, `chmod`, holds…) waits for its answer like a hard mount, however long the backend is gone, so it never returns an error for an effect that lands later. Only a signal that ends the process (`SIGKILL`) or the kernel shutting down ends that wait. JavaScript programs' synchronous `ctx.sync.fs` calls get the same treatment on their own lane (#1033): a read-only call (`readFile`, `stat`, `lstat`, `readdir`, `readlink`, `exists`, `statfs`) throws `EIO` after 25 s without an answer, and every other call, as well as descriptor calls such as a read from a pipe or a terminal, waits for its answer.
- `src/fs/`: the OPFS filesystem and the virtual command directories.
- `src/launcher.ts`, `src/commands.ts`, `src/serve.ts`, `src/index.ts`, `src/users.ts`: command resolution, the kernel worker protocol, the page API and the user database; `src/node.ts`, `src/node-process-worker.ts` and `src/node/` the headless Node entry. An embedder that uses `Launcher` directly, without `createKernel`, calls `await launcher.prepare()` first: it creates `/tmp` and `/home` and writes the CA certificate.
- `src/kernel/net/`: the proxy, HTTP/1.1, TLS termination and the local CA, and the bridge to the page's transport; `src/transport.ts` and `src/local-proxy-transport.ts` are the page side.
- `src/cdp/`: the CDP facade on `127.0.0.1:9222`, its WebSocket framing, the registry of hosts and the `MessagePort` bridge to them.
- `src/cron/`: the cron service (`service.ts`), the schedule and table parsers, the clock, and the `crontab` command with the kernel side of `ctx.crontab`. An embedder that uses `Launcher` directly passes `cron: { lock }` to share the firing lock among the kernels on one root; the default, `slicc-kernel-cron`, is one lock for the whole origin.


## Installing from git

`npm install github:ai-ecoverse/slicc-kernel#<sha>` works: the `prepare` script builds `dist/`.

## Development

```sh
npm install
npm run lint
npm test
npm run test:unit
```

`npm test` builds `dist/` and runs the integration tests in Chrome for Testing, installed by `slicc-cdp install-browser`, over raw CDP through the [harness from slicc-shared-web](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-shared-web#integration-test-harness), against a cross-origin isolated test page that installs bash and coreutils into OPFS. Each test writes screenshots and console logs to `artifacts/`. With `SLICC_COVERAGE=1`, V8 coverage from the page and every worker, mapped back to `src/` through the source maps, goes to `coverage/` (each worker pauses at its exit so its coverage is taken first); without it, which is how CI runs, the harness attaches no debugger or profiler to the workers, because nothing reads that coverage (unit-test diff coverage comes from the unit store). With `SLICC_PROFILE=1` the tests also record CPU profiles, and `artifacts/hotspots.md` lists where the time went.

The recorder behind that coverage changes timing and memory in long runs. It attaches to every page and worker with the debugger, profiler and precise coverage on, and pauses every process worker at its exit breakpoint (`exits` in `test/integration/chrome.mjs`) until the coverage of that worker is collected, keeping every collected script until the run ends. A run with thousands of process exits or hundreds of page loads therefore grows the test process by gigabytes, and each exit waits longer as it grows, so a parent can sit in `proc-exec` for tens of seconds while its child has already finished; each further page load also gets slower (in one tab reloaded 300 times, a boot went from 80 to 500 ms with no kernel in any other tab). When a long run looks stuck or leaky, measure it again with a `launch` that passes `coverage: ['/nothing/']` and no `exits` before blaming the kernel (#991).

Unit tests live in `test/unit/`, which stays out of git. They run real wasm bash and coreutils through the whole stack in Node `worker_threads` against an in-memory OPFS. The pre-commit hook runs them under coverage and requires every changed line in `src/` to be covered. `tools/heavy.sh` runs them two files at a time, in one of two unit slots of the host (`/tmp/slicc-unit-N.lock`, waiting while both are taken; a lock it can't take for any other reason, such as a lock file it can't open, ends the run with `lockf`'s status; `SLICC_UNIT_SLOTS` sets how many, `SLICC_UNIT_SLOT=0` takes none, as for CI; a HUP, INT or TERM to `heavy.sh` stops the run it holds or waits for), never in the browser runs' slots (those go through `slicc-heavy`; `SLICC_HEAVY_SLOT=1` runs the unit tests through `~/.bb/coord-bin/slicc-heavy` instead, when it is there), and stops the run if its processes use more than 3,000,000 KB together or 2,000,000 KB in one process (2,800,000 KB for the command itself, the coverage tool that merges the report at the end; `SLICC_HEAVY_ROOT_KB`, never below the per-process cap), or make no progress for 900 s once it has its slot, that is, no process of the run starts or ends in that time (`SLICC_HEAVY_TIMEOUT`; each test also has 180 s), so a long selection that keeps moving is never cut off (`SLICC_HEAVY_REPORT=1` prints the peak). `SLICC_HEAVY_TREE_KB` and `SLICC_HEAVY_PROC_KB` raise the caps, but only for measuring a run under a higher ceiling that still stops a runaway, never to get a commit through. The guard follows the command's process group and every process it has seen, including children that leave the group, but a process that forks, calls `setsid` and whose parent exits within one 0.25 s sample can escape: macOS has no unprivileged containment, and a test that daemonizes is a bug. The compiler tests (zig, rustc and cargo), each a few GB, are integration tests in `test/integration/compilers.test.mjs`, which CI runs.

The Biome, TypeScript, lefthook, Renovate and CI configuration comes from [slicc-shared-web](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-shared-web), which also provides the `slicc-lint-comments` (no comments anywhere), `slicc-no-unit-tests` (no unit tests in git) and `slicc-diff-cover` (100% coverage of changed lines) commands that `npm run lint` and the pre-commit hook use.

`test/integration/fixtures/dlfork/` is a WASIX PIE program (`dlfork.c`) that `dlopen`s a side module (`side.c`, built as `lib/libdlside.so`), forks, and has the child read the module's data and call through function pointers taken before the fork, for the fork-after-`dlopen` test. It is built with wasix-sysroot 2025.9.30-28's legacy-EH PIC variant and homescoop's pinned wasixcc, then Asyncify for `proc_fork`: `INSTALL_WASIXCC=<homescoop>/scripts/install-wasixcc.sh WASIX_SYSROOT_28=<unpacked -28 package> test/integration/fixtures/dlfork/build.sh`. `bin/dlfork.wasm` is about 800 KB, far bigger than the other fixtures: wasixcc links a position-independent main with `--whole-archive --export-all`, so side modules can find any libc symbol, and neither `--gc-sections` nor a narrower export list (`--no-export-dynamic --export=…`) takes that out.

`test/integration/fixtures/preh1stat/` is a tiny Emscripten program (`preh1stat.c`) that prints its `getuid`/`geteuid`/`getgid`/`getegid` and the owner of each path it's given. It's built **without** the credential library (`slicc_cred_js`), with today's emcc from homescoop's pinned `emsdk@0.4.0`, so it stands for the packages installed before H1. Those see every file as `1000:1000` (`stat-owner.test.mjs`), and every published `@ai-ecoverse/wasm-*` package now links the library, so no package can carry that row. Build it with `test/integration/fixtures/preh1stat/build.sh`: it fetches `emsdk@0.4.0` unless `emcc` is on `PATH`, `PREH1STAT_BUILD` sets its work directory, and it refuses a build whose glue carries the credential library.

`test/integration/fixtures/clocklink/` is a tiny Emscripten program (`clocklink.c`) for `crowded-lookups.test.mjs` (#984). It creates the file named by its second argument (the signal to the other kernel), reads the link named by its first, then reads only the wall clock until the deadline given as its third argument (milliseconds since the epoch), reads the link again and prints both answers (`first=EINVAL second=t`). Build it with `test/integration/fixtures/clocklink/build.sh`, which fetches `emsdk@0.4.0` (emcc 4.0.23) unless `emcc` is on `PATH`; `CLOCKLINK_BUILD` sets its work directory. The committed build is `bin/clocklink` sha256 `0096e2ff68fb380d1b71aaa5fae64d96d6fe60aa2a3933e7207394d30436bf72` and `bin/clocklink.wasm` sha256 `e251dfa3b9d19d855cd0704a9cf67e7a7d28270083292ecde18809932bfbba16`.

`test/integration/fixtures/linktest/` is a WASIX program (`linktest.c`) that calls `linkat(AT_FDCWD, from, AT_FDCWD, to, AT_SYMLINK_FOLLOW)` and prints the new name's type, whether it shares the target's inode, and its link count, for `link-follow.test.mjs`. Coreutils' `ln -L` (Emscripten) takes another path, so the WASI program covers `path_link` with the follow flag. Build it with homescoop's pinned wasixcc and wasix-sysroot 2025.9.30-28: `INSTALL_WASIXCC=<homescoop>/scripts/install-wasixcc.sh WASIX_SYSROOT_28=<unpacked -28 package> test/integration/fixtures/linktest/build.sh`.

`tools/big-file.mjs [bytes]` is a manual check, not part of any suite. It writes a file of that size (64 MiB by default) through a tmpfs mount with the unit-test launcher, reads it back, and prints the peak RSS and its ratio to the file size. A 256 MiB file peaks at about 1.8 times its size. Before ranged I/O (#127), a 1.1 GiB file peaked at 18 GB of RSS. **Do not run it past 1 GiB on a workstation.** It refuses anything over 256 MiB unless `SLICC_BIG_FILE_I_HAVE_THE_RAM=1` is set. Measure a small file and extrapolate instead. The unit suite checks a 32 MiB file the same way, and allows a fixed 192 MiB for garbage V8 has yet to collect, which dominates at that size.

### CI and releases

`.github/workflows/ci.yml` is the whole CI and release: one caller of [slicc-shared-web's pipeline](https://github.com/ai-ecoverse/slicc-shared-web#pipeline), pinned to the release of the `@ai-ecoverse/slicc-shared-web` devDependency, so the workflow and the harness the tests use move together. `test/integration/workflows.test.mjs` holds it to that.

- **Pull requests and the merge queue:** `npm run lint` (Biome, TypeScript and the shared rules), then the integration tests (`npm test`) split by their timings across 4 shards on the pool, and on a pull request the Claude and Codex reviews, with the kernel's prompt in `review-prompt`. `ci / integration` and `ci / review` are the checks `main`'s merge queue requires. Renovate's pull requests get no review: their integration tests hold them.
- **Releases:** every push to `main` runs semantic-release on a GitHub-hosted runner, which tags the commit, creates the GitHub release and publishes to npm through trusted publishing, with provenance from `.github/workflows/ci.yml` on `refs/heads/main`. There is no release workflow, no manual release and no certification after the merge: what reaches `main` went through the merge queue's `ci / integration` and the pull request's `ci / review`, so bios and homescoop can pin any release by its provenance. `fledgling.workflow` in `package.json` names `ci.yml`, and `npm run trust` registers it with npm.
- **The packed tarball:** `packed-kernel.test.mjs` packs the package, installs the tarball on its own and runs homescoop's bash and coreutils through its Node entry, so a release that ships without a file its entries load fails CI, and `types-package.test.mjs` type-checks a consumer of its declarations.

## Test fixtures

The WASIX fixtures of `test/integration/epoll.test.mjs` (#674), under `test/integration/fixtures/`, are committed with their sources, build scripts and built wasm.

### `dnstest`

`test/integration/fixtures/dnstest`: a C WASIX program, built by `build.sh` with `wasixcc`, for the [DNS](#dns) rows (#1244). `dnstest udp|tcp|tcp2 <server> <name> <type> [edns]` sends one question to `<server>:53` (`tcp2` sends it twice on one connection), then prints the reply's id check, rcode and `aa`/`tc`/`ra` flags and section counts, and each answer as `<name> <ttl> <type> <data>`. `dnstest flood <server> <name> <type> <n>` sends `n` questions over UDP without waiting, `dnstest bind` tries to bind `127.0.0.1:53` over UDP and TCP and prints each result, and `dnstest stall <server> <name> <type> <s>` writes questions over TCP until the connection is full, waits `s` seconds, then counts the answers it can read (`closed` when the stub hung up before answering them all).

### `pg-fixture` with `plus`

`test/integration/pg-fixture.mjs`, `pgFixture(t, cert, password, { plus: true })`: pg-gateway's SCRAM-SHA-256 server, which offers `SCRAM-SHA-256-PLUS` too and refuses as PostgreSQL's `auth-scram.c` does (jsh#531):
- `y,,` after it offered `-PLUS`: "SCRAM channel binding negotiation error";
- `p=` with `SCRAM-SHA-256`, and `SCRAM-SHA-256-PLUS` without `p=`;
- a channel-binding type other than `tls-server-end-point`;
- a `c=` other than base64 of the GS2 header plus, for `-PLUS`, the SHA-256 of its leaf.

The refusals and the client-final messages are recorded in `seen.plus`. The real-server check is slicc-jsh's, against PostgreSQL over SSL.

### `heldcwd`

`test/integration/fixtures/heldcwd`: a Rust wasm32-wasip1 program, built by `build.sh` with rustup's wasm32-wasip1 target, for the [working directory](#the-working-directory) rows (#1164).
- `heldcwd`: lists `.`, waits for a line on stdin, then lists `.` again, tries a relative create and stat, and lists `..`.
- `heldcwd loop N`: stats `old` N times, reads a line, then stats it N more times, for the count of `cwd-check` calls.

### `tokiotest`

A small tokio program built for WASIX (`wasm32-wasmer-wasi`), the fixture for `test/integration/epoll.test.mjs` (#674). It runs on tokio's current-thread runtime, or on the multi-thread one (2 workers) with `--multi` first:

| Mode | What it does | Prints |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `echo` | a TcpListener on 127.0.0.1:4701 and a TcpStream to it; the client sends `hello`, the server answers `echo hello` | `echo hello` |
| `stdin` | copies `tokio::io::stdin()` through a loopback socket (port 4702) and back to stdout | the input |
| `sleep MS` | `tokio::time::timeout` of a future that never ends | `timed out not early` |
| `wake` | a `spawn_blocking` thread wakes the reactor through `Notify` (the mio waker's eventfd) | `woken, blocking done` |
| `backpressure` | writes 4 MiB to a nonblocking loopback socket (port 4703) whose reader starts 100 ms later | `sent 4194304 received 4194304` |
| `serve PORT` / `client PORT` | two processes over loopback; the server answers in upper case | `served PING` / `client got PING` |

The ports are fixed because `local_addr()` reports `0.0.0.0:0` with this toolchain: Rust's std for `wasm32-wasmer-wasi` doesn't import `sock_addr_local`.

In `backpressure`, the client socket is set nonblocking by hand (`into_std`, `set_nonblocking`, `from_std`). The WASIX mio fork (1.0.3+wasix.1) opens its sockets blocking, unlike upstream mio's `SOCK_NONBLOCK`. On a blocking socket a full send buffer blocks the runtime's thread, so tokio never sees `EAGAIN` and never waits for `EPOLLOUT`.

**Build:**

```sh
WASIX_RUST=/path/to/wasix-rust WASIX_SYSROOT_28=/path/to/wasix-sysroot ./build.sh
```

- `WASIX_RUST`: the unpacked [wasix-org/rust](https://github.com/wasix-org/rust) toolchain `v2026-09-18.1+rust-1.97`, aarch64-apple-darwin (`rust-toolchain-aarch64-apple-darwin.tar.gz`, sha256 `815927a3894ac7a0d4b9709a5825a85678c94733225b9e4da574cfb9674b4059`). `rustc -vV` says `1.97.1-dev`, LLVM 22.1.6. Cargo is the host's (1.98.1 here), with `RUSTC` pointing at the toolchain's.
- `WASIX_SYSROOT_28`: the unpacked `@ai-ecoverse/wasix-sysroot` 2025.9.30-28 package (sha256 of the tarball `ce48e444ca4677a7afa570fb9076c60a897ed4681a10b1767704a003c674e81d`). It's linked with `-C link-self-contained=no`, its `libc.a` and `crt1-command.o`.
- The crates come from the WASIX registry (`.cargo/config.toml`, source replacement to `sparse+https://cargo-registry.wasix.org/`), pinned by `Cargo.lock`: tokio 1.47.0+wasix.1, mio 1.0.3+wasix.1, libc 0.2.186+wasix.1, socket2 0.6.0+wasix.1, wasix 0.13.2.
- Build output goes to `.build/` (or `TOKIOTEST_BUILD`), which git ignores.

The build is reproducible: `bin/tokiotest.wasm` is 258,642 bytes, sha256 `3b230fc0f03ad7290fb0cd78724985cc021b8ab19b2e6401bf6bc62989dfd039`.

### `maskentry`

A WASIX C program for `test/integration/mask-entry.test.mjs` (#799).
